# Chemistry — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Elements, bonding and properties

#### Atomic structure and isotopes

**A1.** B. 20
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-1 -->

Explanation: Neutrons = mass number − atomic number = 37 − 17 = 20. 17 is the number of protons, and 37 is the total number of nucleons.

**A2.** D. number and arrangement of electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-2 -->

Explanation: Isotopes have the same number of protons and therefore, as neutral atoms, the same electron configuration. Chemical behaviour depends on the electrons. Their neutron numbers and mass numbers are different.

**A3.** A. 26 protons, 30 neutrons and 23 electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-3 -->

Explanation: Fe has atomic number 26, so it has 26 protons. Neutrons = 56 − 26 = 30. A 3+ charge means 3 electrons have been lost: 26 − 3 = 23. Gaining 3 electrons (29) would give a 3− ion.

**A4.** B. 63.6
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-4 -->

Explanation: Ar = (63.0 × 0.692) + (65.0 × 0.308) = 43.6 + 20.0 = 63.6. 64.0 is the simple average, which ignores that copper-63 is more abundant.

**A5.** C. an atom is mostly empty space with a small, dense, positively charged nucleus.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-5 -->

Explanation: Most particles passing through shows the atom is mostly empty space. Strong deflection of a few shows a tiny, dense, positive centre. Fixed energy levels come from Bohr's model, and the evenly spread charge describes Thomson's model, which this result disproved.

**A6.** Isotopes are atoms of the same element (same number of protons/atomic number) with different numbers of neutrons (different mass numbers); both isotopes have 6 electrons with the same configuration (2,4), and chemical reactions depend on electrons, so they react identically.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** Ar = (10.0 × 19.9 + 11.0 × 80.1) ÷ 100; = (199 + 881.1) ÷ 100 = 10.8.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** 16 protons means the element is sulfur; mass number = 16 + 18 = 34, symbol ³⁴S²⁻ (³⁴₁₆S²⁻); there are 2 more electrons than protons, so the charge is 2−.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-8 -->

Explanation:

**A9.** B. protons in the nucleus of an atom.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-9 -->

Explanation: The atomic number is the number of protons, and it defines the element. Protons plus neutrons is the mass number, so that option describes the wrong quantity.

**A10.** A. The total number of protons and neutrons in its nucleus.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-10 -->

Explanation: Mass number = protons + neutrons, always a whole number for a particular isotope. The weighted average mass of the isotopes is the relative atomic mass, which is usually not a whole number.

**A11.** C. 35.5
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-11 -->

Explanation: Ar = (35.0 × 0.758) + (37.0 × 0.242) = 26.5 + 9.0 = 35.5. 36.0 is the simple average, which ignores that ³⁵Cl is about three times as abundant as ³⁷Cl.

**A12.** A. 10
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-12 -->

Explanation: Magnesium has atomic number 12, so a neutral atom has 12 electrons. A 2+ charge means two electrons have been lost, leaving 10. 12 is the number of protons, which does not change when the ion forms.

**A13.** D. 2,8,8,2
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-13 -->

Explanation: Shells fill 2, 8, 8 and the remaining two electrons go in the fourth shell, giving 2,8,8,2. 2,8,10 is wrong because the third shell is filled with 8 electrons before the fourth shell begins, in this model for the first 20 elements.

**A14.** A. mass-to-charge ratio.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-14 -->

Explanation: Ions are deflected by a magnetic field by an amount that depends on their mass-to-charge ratio, so isotopes of an element are separated. Neutron number matters only because it contributes to the mass.

**A15.** C. Neutron
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-15 -->

Explanation: The neutron has about the same mass as a proton but is uncharged. The proton has similar mass but a positive charge, and the electron is negatively charged with almost no mass.

**A16.** B. ¹⁶₈O and ¹⁸₈O
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-16 -->

Explanation: Isotopes have the same atomic number but different mass numbers, as in ¹⁶₈O and ¹⁸₈O. ¹⁴C and ¹⁴N have the same mass number but different atomic numbers, so they are different elements.

**A17.** C. ³⁵Cl⁻
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-17 -->

Explanation: 17 protons identifies chlorine and 17 + 18 = 35 is the mass number. One more electron than protons gives a 1− charge, so it is ³⁵Cl⁻. A 1+ charge would need fewer electrons than protons.

**A18.** D. Group 15, period 3.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-18 -->

Explanation: Three occupied shells means period 3. Five outer electrons means Group 15 (the group number is 10 + number of valence electrons for main groups 13 to 18). Group 5 does not apply to main-group elements.

**A19.** B. a weighted average of the masses of the naturally occurring isotopes.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-19 -->

Explanation: Ar is a weighted average of isotopic masses using their relative abundances, so it is rarely whole. Electrons contribute almost no mass, and using only the most abundant isotope would give a whole number.

**A20.** C. 24.3
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-20 -->

Explanation: Ar = (24.0 × 0.790) + (25.0 × 0.100) + (26.0 × 0.110) = 18.96 + 2.50 + 2.86 = 24.3. 25.0 is the simple average of the three masses, which ignores the abundances.

**A21.** A. 6
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-21 -->

Explanation: Sulfur's configuration is 2,8,6, so the outer (valence) shell holds 6 electrons. 8 is the number in the second shell, and 16 is the total number of electrons.

**A22.** D. ¹⁴₆C
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-22 -->

Explanation: 6 protons means carbon, and the mass number is 6 + 8 = 14, so the symbol is ¹⁴₆C. ⁸₆C would result from using the neutron number as the mass number.

**A23.** B. ⁷⁹X is the more abundant isotope.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-23 -->

Explanation: A 50:50 mixture would give an Ar of 80.0. Because 79.9 is closer to 79, ⁷⁹X must be slightly more abundant.

**A24.** Thomson's model had positive charge spread evenly through the whole atom with electrons embedded in it [1]; Rutherford's model placed all the positive charge and almost all the mass in a tiny, dense nucleus at the centre [1]; the electrons are outside the nucleus, so the atom is mostly empty space [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-24 -->

Explanation:

**A25.** Ar = (20.0 × 90.5 + 21.0 × 0.3 + 22.0 × 9.2) ÷ 100 = (1810 + 6.3 + 202.4) ÷ 100 [1]; = 20.2 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-25 -->

Explanation:

**A26.** Al atom: 2,8,3 [1]; Al³⁺ ion: 2,8 [1]; the atom loses its three outer-shell electrons to form the 3+ ion, so the third shell is no longer occupied [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-26 -->

Explanation:

**A27.** Protons = 30 [1]; neutrons = 65 − 30 = 35 [1]; electrons = 30 − 2 = 28 because the 2+ charge means two electrons have been lost [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-27 -->

Explanation:

**A28.** Let the fraction of ⁷Li be x: 7.0x + 6.0(1 − x) = 6.94 [1]; x = 0.94, so ⁷Li is 94% [1]; ⁶Li is therefore 6% [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-28 -->

Explanation:

**A29.** The horizontal axis shows mass-to-charge ratio (mass of each isotope for singly charged ions) and each peak represents one isotope [1]; the height of each peak gives the relative abundance of that isotope [1]; the relative atomic mass is the weighted average of the isotopic masses using these abundances [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-29 -->

Explanation:

**A30.** Electrons are in fixed shells (energy levels) at set distances from the nucleus, each with a specific energy [1]; electrons can move between energy levels only by absorbing or emitting specific amounts of energy [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-30 -->

Explanation:

**A31.** X and Y are isotopes because they have the same number of protons (12) but different numbers of neutrons [1]; the element is magnesium, so X is ²⁴Mg [1] and Y is ²⁶Mg [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-31 -->

Explanation:

**A32.** The ion has 20 − 2 = 18 electrons [1]; configuration 2,8,8 [1]; the same configuration as argon [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-32 -->

Explanation:

#### Electron configuration and subshells

**A33.** D. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 4s¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-1 -->

Explanation: K has 19 electrons. The 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, so the 19th electron goes into 4s. A p subshell can hold at most 6 electrons, so 3p⁷ is impossible.

**A34.** C. 10
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-2 -->

Explanation: A d subshell has 5 orbitals, and each orbital holds 2 electrons, giving 10. An s subshell holds 2, a p subshell 6 and an f subshell 14.

**A35.** A. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d¹⁰ 4s¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-3 -->

Explanation: Copper adopts 3d¹⁰ 4s¹ because a full d subshell is more stable. 3d⁹ 4s² is the configuration predicted by the simple filling order, but it is not the observed ground state.

**A36.** B. [Ar] 3d⁵
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-4 -->

Explanation: Fe is [Ar] 3d⁶ 4s². When transition metals form ions, the 4s electrons are removed first, then the 3d electrons. Removing 2 electrons from 4s and 1 from 3d leaves [Ar] 3d⁵. [Ar] 3d³ 4s² would mean the 3d electrons were removed first.

**A37.** C. period 4, group 15 of the p-block.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-5 -->

Explanation: The 4p subshell places the element in period 4 and the p-block. The configuration ends 4s² 3d¹⁰ 4p³, so there are 5 valence electrons (4s² 4p³), which puts it in group 15. The element is arsenic.

**A38.** S: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁴; S²⁻: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ (two electrons added to 3p, same configuration as argon).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-6 -->

Explanation:

**A39.** Subshells fill in order of increasing energy, and the 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, so it fills first; each orbital holds a maximum of two electrons, which have opposite spins.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-7 -->

Explanation:

**A40.** Heat energy promotes (excites) electrons to higher energy levels; when the electrons return to lower energy levels they release the energy difference as light (photons) of specific energies/wavelengths, giving discrete lines; each element has a unique set of energy levels (different nuclear charge and electron arrangement), so the energy differences, and therefore the line pattern, are unique to that element.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-8 -->

Explanation:

**A41.** A. 3
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-9 -->

Explanation: A p subshell contains 3 orbitals, each holding up to 2 electrons. The number 6 is the maximum number of electrons in the subshell, not the number of orbitals.

**A42.** B. 3s, 3p and 3d
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-10 -->

Explanation: The third shell contains three subshells: s, p and d. An f subshell does not appear until the fourth shell, and every shell contains an s subshell.

**A43.** C. 18
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-11 -->

Explanation: The third shell has 3s (2), 3p (6) and 3d (10) subshells, giving 18 electrons. A value of 8 would only count the 3s and 3p subshells.

**A44.** D. [Ar] 3d⁵ 4s¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-12 -->

Explanation: Chromium has 24 electrons and adopts a half-filled 3d subshell with one electron in 4s. The configuration [Ar] 3d⁴ 4s² follows the simple filling order but is not observed.

**A45.** A. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-13 -->

Explanation: A magnesium atom (1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s²) loses its two 3s electrons to form Mg²⁺. Keeping the 3s² electrons would describe the neutral atom.

**A46.** B. [Ar] 3d¹⁰
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-14 -->

Explanation: Zinc is [Ar] 3d¹⁰ 4s². Both 4s electrons are removed to form Zn²⁺, leaving a full 3d subshell. [Ar] 3d¹⁰ 4s² is the neutral atom.

**A47.** C. 3d
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-15 -->

Explanation: The 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, so 3d fills next, followed by 4p. The 3p subshell has already been filled before 4s.

**A48.** D. 3
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-16 -->

Explanation: Nitrogen is 1s² 2s² 2p³. By Hund's rule the three 2p electrons occupy separate orbitals with parallel spins, giving 3 unpaired electrons. Pairing two of them would raise the energy.

**A49.** A. Ca²⁺
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-17 -->

Explanation: Ca has 20 electrons, so Ca²⁺ has 18, the same as Ar. Mg²⁺ and Na⁺ each have 10 electrons (like neon) and O²⁻ also has 10.

**A50.** B. ns² np⁴
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-18 -->

Explanation: Group 16 elements have 6 valence electrons, arranged as ns² np⁴. The configuration ns² np⁶ belongs to the noble gases (group 18).

**A51.** C. aluminium
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-19 -->

Explanation: The configuration contains 2 + 2 + 6 + 2 + 1 = 13 electrons, so the atomic number is 13, which is aluminium. Boron has a similar valence pattern (2s² 2p¹) but only 5 electrons.

**A52.** D. 3p, 4s, 3d, 4p
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-20 -->

Explanation: The 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, and 3d is lower than 4p. Placing 3d before 4s ignores the energy overlap between the third and fourth shells.

**A53.** A. [Ar] 3d¹⁰
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-21 -->

Explanation: Copper is [Ar] 3d¹⁰ 4s¹. Removing the single 4s electron gives [Ar] 3d¹⁰. Removing a 3d electron instead would give [Ar] 3d⁹ 4s¹, which is incorrect since 4s electrons are lost first.

**A54.** B. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-22 -->

Explanation: Oxygen has 8 electrons: 1s² 2s² 2p⁴. The 2p subshell therefore holds 4 electrons, not 6, because it is not yet full.

**A55.** C. have opposite spins
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-23 -->

Explanation: An orbital holds at most two electrons and they must have opposite spins. Having the same spin in one orbital is not permitted.

**A56.** D. [Ar] 3d⁸
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-24 -->

Explanation: Nickel is [Ar] 3d⁸ 4s². The two 4s electrons are removed first, leaving [Ar] 3d⁸. [Ar] 3d⁶ 4s² would imply the 3d electrons were removed first.

**A57.** Al: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p¹ [1]. Al³⁺: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ [1]. Al³⁺ has the same configuration as neon [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-25 -->

Explanation:

**A58.** A subshell is a group of orbitals of the same type (s, p, d, f) within a shell, with the same energy [1]. An orbital is a region of space where an electron is likely to be found, holding a maximum of two electrons [1]. A d subshell contains 5 orbitals [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-26 -->

Explanation:

**A59.** V: [Ar] 3d³ 4s² [1]. V³⁺: [Ar] 3d² (the 4s electrons are removed first, then one 3d electron) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-27 -->

Explanation:

**A60.** Calcium (1s² … 4s²) has its last electron enter the 4s subshell, so it is an s-block element [1]. Scandium ([Ar] 3d¹ 4s²) has its last electron enter the 3d subshell, so it is a d-block element [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-28 -->

Explanation:

**A61.** Co²⁺: [Ar] 3d⁷ [1]. There are 3 unpaired electrons (3d⁷ gives two paired and three unpaired in five orbitals) [1]. Cobalt is [Ar] 3d⁷ 4s²; the two 4s electrons are removed first when the ion forms [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Periodic table trends

**A62.** B. core charge increases while the number of occupied shells stays the same.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-1 -->

Explanation: All period 3 atoms have three occupied shells. Core charge rises from +1 to +7, so the valence electrons are pulled more strongly towards the nucleus. The number of shells does not decrease, and neutrons have no effect on electron attraction.

**A63.** D. Argon
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-2 -->

Explanation: First ionisation energy generally increases across a period as core charge increases and atomic radius decreases. Argon, with a core charge of +8, holds its valence electrons most strongly. Sodium has the lowest value in the period.

**A64.** B. +6
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-3 -->

Explanation: Core charge = number of protons − number of inner-shell electrons = 16 − 10 = +6. +16 is the full nuclear charge, which ignores shielding by the 10 inner electrons.

**A65.** A. the valence electron is further from the nucleus, so it is lost more easily.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-4 -->

Explanation: Both atoms have a core charge of +1. Potassium has an extra occupied shell, so its valence electron is further from the nucleus and less strongly attracted. It therefore has a lower ionisation energy and is lost more readily in reactions.

**A66.** C. be less reactive and have a lower electronegativity.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-5 -->

Explanation: X is chlorine (2,8,7). It has the same core charge as fluorine, but its valence shell is further from the nucleus, so it attracts electrons less strongly. This makes it less electronegative and less reactive as an oxidising agent. Its radius is larger, and as a non-metal it tends to gain electrons, not lose them.

**A67.** Both ions have the same number of electrons in the same shells (isoelectronic); Na⁺ has 11 protons compared with 9 in F⁻, so its greater nuclear charge pulls the electrons closer, giving a smaller radius.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A68.** First ionisation energy decreases down the group; core charge stays constant at +1, but the number of occupied shells increases, so the valence electron is further from the nucleus; the weaker attraction (with more shielding) means less energy is needed to remove it.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A69.** Electronegativity increases across period 3 from Na to Cl; core charge increases and atomic radius decreases with the same number of shells, so the nucleus attracts bonding electrons more strongly.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A70.** A. +2
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-9 -->

Explanation: Core charge = 12 protons − 10 inner-shell electrons = +2. +12 is the full nuclear charge and ignores shielding by the inner electrons.

**A71.** C. Sodium
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-10 -->

Explanation: Sodium and magnesium have three occupied shells, so they are larger than lithium and beryllium. Of these two, sodium has the lower core charge (+1 compared with +2), so its valence electron is held less tightly and its radius is largest.

**A72.** B. It decreases because the valence shell is further from the nucleus.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-11 -->

Explanation: Core charge is +7 for all halogens, but the number of occupied shells increases, so bonding electrons are further from the nucleus and attracted less. More protons does not increase attraction because inner electrons shield them.

**A73.** D. Fluorine
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-12 -->

Explanation: Electronegativity increases across a period and decreases down a group, so it is greatest at the top right of the table (excluding noble gases). Fluorine has a high core charge and the smallest radius of these, so it attracts bonding electrons most strongly.

**A74.** A. F₂ > Cl₂ > Br₂ > I₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-13 -->

Explanation: Halogens react by gaining an electron, and this is easiest when the attracting nucleus is closest to the incoming electron. Reactivity therefore decreases down the group. The reverse order is the trend for group 1 metals, not halogens.

**A75.** C. Aluminium
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-14 -->

Explanation: The outermost electron of aluminium is in a 3p subshell, which is slightly higher in energy and further out than the 3s electrons of magnesium, so it is easier to remove. This is a small exception to the general increase across the period.

**A76.** B. the same number of valence electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-15 -->

Explanation: The number of valence electrons determines how an atom bonds and reacts, and it is the same for all members of a group. Atoms in a group have different numbers of shells, radii and protons.

**A77.** D. Magnesium
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-16 -->

Explanation: Metallic character decreases across a period as core charge rises and valence electrons are held more tightly. Magnesium is furthest to the left of these, so it loses electrons most readily. Sulfur is a non-metal.

**A78.** B. the ion has lost its entire third shell, so it has fewer occupied shells.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-17 -->

Explanation: Mg (2,8,2) loses its two valence electrons to form Mg²⁺ (2,8), which has only two occupied shells. The number of protons does not change when an ion forms, and electrons are lost, not gained.

**A79.** C. the same nuclear charge attracts more electrons, and the greater repulsion between electrons expands the electron cloud.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-18 -->

Explanation: Chlorine gains an electron into the same valence shell. The nuclear charge is unchanged, but there are more electrons to attract and they repel one another more, so the radius increases. No new shell is added and the number of protons stays at 17.

**A80.** D. +5
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-19 -->

Explanation: The atom is phosphorus, with 15 protons and 10 inner-shell electrons, so core charge = 15 − 10 = +5. +15 is the full nuclear charge and ignores shielding.

**A81.** A. Carbon, because it has a lower core charge than fluorine.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-20 -->

Explanation: Carbon and fluorine both have two occupied shells, but carbon's core charge is +4 compared with +7 for fluorine, so its valence electrons are attracted less strongly. More protons in fluorine increases attraction, giving a smaller radius.

**A82.** C. Nitrogen
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-21 -->

Explanation: All four are in period 2, where first ionisation energy generally increases with core charge. Nitrogen has the greatest core charge (+5) of the four and the smallest radius, so its valence electrons are held most strongly.

**A83.** A. a lower first ionisation energy and a larger atomic radius.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-22 -->

Explanation: Both have a core charge of +1, but rubidium has five occupied shells compared with three. Its valence electron is therefore further out and more easily removed. Being in the same group means similar, not identical, values.

**A84.** A. in a shell with fewer occupied shells beneath it, so they are closer to the nucleus and held more strongly.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-23 -->

Explanation: Both have a full valence shell and the same core charge of +8, but neon has only two shells, so its electrons are closer to the nucleus and harder to remove. Both have eight valence electrons and neither has a lower core charge.

**A85.** Core charge is the net positive charge experienced by valence electrons: number of protons minus number of inner-shell (non-valence) electrons [1]; aluminium has 13 protons and 10 inner-shell electrons, so core charge = +3 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-24 -->

Explanation:

**A86.** Both atoms have a core charge of +2 and two valence electrons [1]; calcium has four occupied shells compared with three for magnesium, so its valence electrons are further from the nucleus [1]; they are less strongly attracted, so they are lost more easily, making calcium more reactive [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-25 -->

Explanation:

**A87.** Atomic radius decreases from sodium to silicon across the period [1]; the number of occupied shells stays the same (three) while core charge increases from +1 to +4 [1]; the increasing attraction pulls the valence electrons closer to the nucleus [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-26 -->

Explanation:

#### Metallic bonding and properties of metals

**A88.** A. a lattice of positive ions surrounded by a sea of delocalised electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-1 -->

Explanation: Metal atoms give up their valence electrons, which are delocalised throughout a lattice of cations. Bonding is the electrostatic attraction between the cations and the delocalised electrons. Alternating positive and negative ions describes an ionic lattice.

**A89.** C. layers of cations can slide over each other without breaking the non-directional metallic bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-2 -->

Explanation: Metallic bonding is non-directional. When layers of cations shift, the delocalised electrons still hold them together, so the metal bends instead of shattering. Fixed, directional bonding would make the metal brittle.

**A90.** D. The magnetic behaviour of iron, cobalt and nickel
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-3 -->

Explanation: The sea-of-electrons model explains conductivity (mobile electrons) and ductility (non-directional bonding). It cannot explain why only some metals, such as Fe, Co and Ni, are magnetic. This is a known limitation of the model.

**A91.** B. softer and more ductile, with larger crystals.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-4 -->

Explanation: Slow cooling allows larger crystals (grains) to grow, so there are fewer grain boundaries and the metal is softer and more workable. Rapid cooling (quenching) produces small crystals and a hard, brittle metal.

**A92.** B. the delocalised electrons can move through the lattice when a potential difference is applied.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-5 -->

Explanation: The delocalised valence electrons are free to move and carry charge through the lattice. The cations stay in fixed positions in the solid, and metals do not contain free negative ions.

**A93.** Magnesium contributes two delocalised electrons per atom and forms Mg²⁺ ions, while sodium contributes one and forms Na⁺; the higher cation charge and greater density of delocalised electrons give stronger electrostatic attraction in magnesium, so more energy is needed to disrupt the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A94.** The small carbon atoms occupy spaces (interstices) between the iron ions in the lattice; this distorts the lattice and makes it harder for layers of iron ions to slide past each other, so the alloy is harder and less malleable.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A95.** Quenching (rapid cooling) produces very small crystals, giving a hard but brittle metal; tempering involves reheating the quenched metal to a moderate temperature and cooling it, which allows partial rearrangement of the crystals; tempered steel keeps much of its hardness but is tougher and less brittle.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-8 -->

Explanation:

**A96.** A. delocalised electrons at the surface absorb and re-emit light of most visible wavelengths.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-9 -->

Explanation: Mobile delocalised electrons can absorb and re-emit visible light, giving the characteristic metallic sheen. Cations do not emit light of their own in this way.

**A97.** B. delocalised electrons move rapidly and transfer kinetic energy through the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-10 -->

Explanation: Energetic delocalised electrons pass kinetic energy to other electrons and cations as they move. The cations only vibrate about fixed positions and do not migrate through the solid.

**A98.** C. Metallic bonding is non-directional, so cations can shift position while remaining attracted to the delocalised electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-11 -->

Explanation: Because the bonding is non-directional, the cations can move past one another and the electron sea keeps holding them together. Removal of electrons would destroy the bonding, not allow ductility.

**A99.** D. Na < Mg < Al
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-12 -->

Explanation: Na, Mg and Al contribute 1, 2 and 3 delocalised electrons per atom and form Na⁺, Mg²⁺ and Al³⁺. Higher cation charge and electron density give stronger metallic bonding, so melting points rise across the period.

**A100.** A. larger cations mean the delocalised electrons are further from the nuclei, so the electrostatic attraction is weaker.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-13 -->

Explanation: Each Group 1 atom donates one electron, so the charge does not change. The cations get larger down the group, weakening the attraction to the electron sea. A change in charge is not the cause.

**A101.** B. a substitutional alloy, with zinc atoms replacing some copper atoms in the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-14 -->

Explanation: Atoms of similar size can substitute for each other in the lattice, giving a substitutional alloy. Interstitial alloys need much smaller atoms, such as carbon, to fit in the gaps.

**A102.** C. the zinc atoms are a different size from copper atoms, which disrupts the regular layers and makes it harder for them to slide.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-15 -->

Explanation: Different-sized atoms distort the regular lattice and hinder movement of layers. Zinc is similar in size to copper, so it does not sit in the gaps, and it does not remove delocalised electrons.

**A103.** D. harder and more brittle, as the crystals are deformed and broken into smaller ones.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-16 -->

Explanation: Cold working distorts and fragments the crystal structure, giving harder, more brittle metal. Larger crystals and a softer metal come from annealing, not from hammering.

**A104.** A. lower electrical conductivity, because the irregular lattice scatters the moving delocalised electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-17 -->

Explanation: Different-sized atoms disrupt the regular lattice, impeding electron flow. Alloys still contain delocalised electrons, so they still conduct.

**A105.** B. 6.02 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-18 -->

Explanation: Each Mg atom forms Mg²⁺ and releases two electrons, so 0.50 mol Mg gives 1.00 mol electrons = 6.02 × 10²³. Using one electron per atom would give 3.01 × 10²³.

**A106.** C. 9.00 g
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-19 -->

Explanation: Each Al atom contributes three electrons, so 1.00 mol electrons comes from 1.00 / 3 mol Al, which is 9.00 g. 27.0 g would contain 3.00 mol of electrons.

**A107.** D. the ions are held in fixed positions and there are no mobile charge carriers.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-20 -->

Explanation: In an ionic solid the Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ ions are locked in the lattice, so nothing can carry charge. Metals conduct because of mobile delocalised electrons; the ions in a solid ionic compound are not free to move.

**A108.** A. shifting NaCl layers brings ions of like charge together so they repel, whereas shifting metal layers keeps the cations surrounded by the electron sea.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-21 -->

Explanation: In ionic lattices a shift of one layer lines up like charges, causing repulsion and fracture. Metallic bonding is non-directional, and strength is not the reason; NaCl has no delocalised electrons.

**A109.** B. copper and tin.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-22 -->

Explanation: Bronze is mainly copper with tin. Copper and zinc make brass, and iron with carbon makes steel.

**A110.** C. the total positive charge of the Na⁺ ions equals the total negative charge of the delocalised electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-23 -->

Explanation: Each Na atom loses one electron, so there is one delocalised electron for every Na⁺ ion and the charges cancel. Sodium metal contains no chloride ions.

**A111.** D. mobile delocalised electrons, and non-directional bonding that lets layers of cations slide, respectively.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-24 -->

Explanation: Conductivity comes from electrons free to move, and ductility from non-directional bonding. Cations are fixed in the solid and do not carry charge.

**A112.** n(Al) = 2.70 / 27.0 = 0.100 mol [1]; each Al atom contributes 3 delocalised electrons, so n(e⁻) = 0.300 mol [1]; number = 0.300 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.81 × 10²³ electrons [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-25 -->

Explanation:

#### Ionic bonding and ionic compounds

**A113.** C. Al₂(SO₄)₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-1 -->

Explanation: Al³⁺ and SO₄²⁻ combine in the ratio 2 : 3 to give zero overall charge: 2(+3) + 3(−2) = 0. Brackets are needed around the polyatomic ion.

**A114.** B. the blow shifts layers so that ions of like charge align and repel.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-2 -->

Explanation: A shift of one ion layer brings cations next to cations and anions next to anions. The resulting repulsion splits the crystal. Ionic compounds have no delocalised electrons, and the bonding between ions is ionic, not intermolecular.

**A115.** D. Magnesium oxide, MgO
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-3 -->

Explanation: MgO consists of Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ ions, which have doubled charges and small radii. This gives much stronger electrostatic attraction than in the 1+/1− compounds, and its melting point is about 2850 °C.

**A116.** B. transfers two electrons to an oxygen atom.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-4 -->

Explanation: Mg (2,8,2) loses its two valence electrons to form Mg²⁺. O (2,6) gains them to form O²⁻, and both ions then have stable noble gas configurations. Sharing electrons would be covalent bonding.

**A117.** B. the ions are free to move and carry charge.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-5 -->

Explanation: In the solid, the ions are fixed in the lattice. Melting frees the ions so they can move towards the electrodes and carry charge. Ionic compounds do not have delocalised electrons.

**A118.** Formula Ca₃N₂; each Ca atom loses 2 electrons to form Ca²⁺ and each N atom gains 3 electrons to form N³⁻; three Ca atoms transfer a total of 6 electrons to two N atoms, so the charges balance (3 × +2 = 2 × 3).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-6 -->

Explanation:

**A119.** (a) iron(III) oxide; (b) ammonium nitrate; (c) potassium dichromate.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-7 -->

Explanation:

**A120.** Ionic bonding; high melting point: strong electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions throughout the lattice needs a lot of energy to overcome; conductivity: ions are fixed in the solid but free to move in solution, so only the solution conducts; brittleness: displacing layers aligns like charges, which repel (any two explanations).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-8 -->

Explanation:

**A121.** B. Ca₃(PO₄)₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-9 -->

Explanation: Ca²⁺ and PO₄³⁻ combine in the ratio 3 : 2 so that 3(+2) + 2(−3) = 0. Ca₂(PO₄)₃ is wrong because it swaps the subscripts and gives a net charge.

**A122.** C. Mg²⁺
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-10 -->

Explanation: Mg loses two electrons to leave 2,8, the same as Ne. Cl⁻, K⁺ and S²⁻ all have 2,8,8, the configuration of argon.

**A123.** A. copper(II) nitrate
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-11 -->

Explanation: Two NO₃⁻ ions require a 2+ charge on copper, so it is copper(II). Nitrate is NO₃⁻, whereas nitrite is NO₂⁻.

**A124.** B. water molecules are attracted to the Na⁺ and Cl⁻ ions, and these attractions can overcome the forces holding the ions in the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-12 -->

Explanation: Polar water molecules surround each ion and the ion–water attractions release enough energy to separate the ions. NaCl contains ions, not molecules, and no new covalent bonds form.

**A125.** C. 6
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-13 -->

Explanation: The NaCl lattice is arranged so that each ion has six nearest neighbours of opposite charge. The 1 : 1 formula does not mean one-to-one pairing.

**A126.** D. 5
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-14 -->

Explanation: One formula unit has 2 Fe³⁺ ions and 3 SO₄²⁻ ions, giving 5 ions. The sulfate ion is a single polyatomic ion, so its atoms are not counted separately.

**A127.** A. shows the simplest whole-number ratio of ions in the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-15 -->

Explanation: An ionic lattice is a continuous array, so the formula only gives the ratio of cations to anions. Ionic compounds do not form discrete molecules.

**A128.** B. O + 2e⁻ → O²⁻
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-16 -->

Explanation: Oxygen gains two electrons to fill its valence shell. The equation with electrons on the right shows loss of electrons, which would give a cation, and O₂ must be balanced to give two O²⁻.

**A129.** C. Na⁺ is smaller than K⁺, so the electrostatic attraction to Cl⁻ is stronger.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-17 -->

Explanation: Both ions carry 1+, but the smaller Na⁺ sits closer to Cl⁻ and attracts it more strongly. The charges are equal, and a larger cation would weaken the attraction.

**A130.** B. (NH₄)₃PO₄
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-18 -->

Explanation: NH₄⁺ and PO₄³⁻ combine in a 3 : 1 ratio to balance charge, and brackets are needed around the polyatomic ammonium ion. NH₄PO₄ has a net charge of 2−.

**A131.** C. X₂Y₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-19 -->

Explanation: X forms X³⁺ and Y forms Y²⁻, so the ratio for zero charge is 2 : 3. X₃Y₂ reverses the ratio.

**A132.** A. 18
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-20 -->

Explanation: A sulfur atom has 16 electrons and gains two to form S²⁻, giving 18. The 14 results from subtracting instead of adding.

**A133.** (a) Na₂S [1]; (b) Mg(NO₃)₂ [1]; (c) Fe(OH)₃ [1]; (d) (NH₄)₂CO₃ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-21 -->

Explanation:

**A134.** Water is a polar molecule with partial positive and partial negative ends [1]; the partial negative oxygen ends are attracted to Na⁺ and the partial positive hydrogen ends are attracted to Cl⁻ [1]; the energy released by these ion–water attractions is enough to overcome the ionic bonds in the lattice, so the ions separate and become surrounded by water molecules [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-22 -->

Explanation:

**A135.** Al atom: 2,8,3 [1]; Al³⁺ ion: 2,8 [1]; Al loses its three valence electrons to achieve a stable noble gas configuration (same as neon), leaving 13 protons and 10 electrons, so the charge is 3+ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-23 -->

Explanation:

**A136.** A continuous three-dimensional array of alternating positive and negative ions [1]; held in fixed positions by strong electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions (no discrete molecules) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-24 -->

Explanation:

**A137.** Mg (2,8,2) loses two electrons to form Mg²⁺ (2,8) [1]; each Cl atom (2,8,7) gains one electron to form Cl⁻ (2,8,8), so two Cl atoms are needed [1]; the oppositely charged ions attract by electrostatic forces to form a lattice with formula MgCl₂ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-25 -->

Explanation:

#### Nanomaterials and reactivity of metals

**A138.** A. 1–100 nm
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-1 -->

Explanation: The nanoscale is about 1–100 nm (1 nm = 10⁻⁹ m). 0.01–0.1 nm is about the size of a single atom, and the µm range describes much larger particles, such as cells.

**A139.** B. 10 times larger.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-2 -->

Explanation: Original: 6 × (1.0 cm)² = 6.0 cm². Each small cube: 6 × (0.10 cm)² = 0.060 cm². Total: 1000 × 0.060 cm² = 60 cm², which is 10 times larger. Dividing particles further increases the surface area to volume ratio, which is why nanoparticles are so reactive.

**A140.** C. Potassium
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-3 -->

Explanation: Potassium is a highly reactive group 1 metal: 2K(s) + 2H₂O(l) → 2KOH(aq) + H₂(g). Iron reacts only very slowly (rusting needs both oxygen and water), and copper and silver do not react with water.

**A141.** B. zinc chloride and hydrogen gas.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-4 -->

Explanation: A reactive metal and a dilute acid produce a salt and hydrogen: Zn(s) + 2HCl(aq) → ZnCl₂(aq) + H₂(g). Chlorine is not produced, and the metal oxide or hydroxide does not form in acid.

**A142.** C. materials at the nanoscale can show different properties from the same material in bulk.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-5 -->

Explanation: At the nanoscale, particle size affects how a material interacts with light, along with its other properties, so gold nanoparticles look red. The gold is still metallic gold, not ions or a new compound, and isotopes have no effect on colour.

**A143.** (a) Ca(s) + 2H₂O(l) → Ca(OH)₂(aq) + H₂(g); (b) 2Mg(s) + O₂(g) → 2MgO(s).
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A144.** Order of reactivity: A > B > C; A could be magnesium (reacts rapidly with dilute acid); B could be iron (reacts slowly), and C could be copper (does not react with dilute HCl).
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A145.** Nanoparticles have a much larger surface area to volume ratio, so a larger proportion of silver atoms are exposed at the surface; this allows more contact with and greater release of silver ions to bacteria, so a small mass is very effective; concern: nanoparticles may penetrate cells or tissue, accumulate in the body or the environment, or harm beneficial organisms, and their long-term health effects are not fully known.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A146.** A. 10⁻⁹ m
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-9 -->

Explanation: The prefix nano means 10⁻⁹, so 1 nm = 10⁻⁹ m. 10⁻⁶ m is one micrometre and 10⁻³ m is one millimetre.

**A147.** B. 3.0 cm⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-10 -->

Explanation: Surface area = 6 × (2.0 cm)² = 24 cm² and volume = (2.0 cm)³ = 8.0 cm³, so the ratio is 3.0 cm⁻¹. 6.0 cm⁻¹ is the ratio for a 1.0 cm cube, not a 2.0 cm cube.

**A148.** C. alkaline, because sodium hydroxide forms.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-11 -->

Explanation: 2Na(s) + 2H₂O(l) → 2NaOH(aq) + H₂(g). Sodium hydroxide is a strong base, so the solution is alkaline even though hydrogen gas is also released.

**A149.** D. Silver
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-12 -->

Explanation: Silver is below hydrogen in the reactivity series and does not displace it from dilute acid. Zinc, magnesium and iron are all more reactive than hydrogen and release H₂.

**A150.** A. A reddish-brown deposit forms on the zinc and the blue colour fades.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-13 -->

Explanation: Zinc is more reactive than copper, so Zn(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Zn²⁺(aq) + Cu(s). Copper metal is deposited and the Cu²⁺ ions that make the solution blue are used up.

**A151.** B. Magnesium, zinc, iron, copper
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-14 -->

Explanation: From most to least reactive, the order is Mg > Zn > Fe > Cu. The other lists place at least one pair of metals in the wrong order.

**A152.** C. a 'pop' sound is heard.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-15 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen burns explosively with oxygen in air, giving a squeaky pop. An extinguished splint suggests carbon dioxide or nitrogen, and a relighting glowing splint indicates oxygen.

**A153.** D. absorb ultraviolet light but are small enough to appear transparent on the skin.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-16 -->

Explanation: Nanoparticles are much smaller than the wavelengths of visible light, so they do not scatter it and the sunscreen looks clear while still absorbing UV. Their composition does not change; only their size does.

**A154.** A. have a larger surface area, so more atoms are exposed to reactants.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-17 -->

Explanation: A greater surface area to volume ratio exposes more platinum atoms where reactants can adsorb and react. The atoms themselves are identical in nanoparticle and bulk form.

**A155.** C. Magnesium atoms lose electrons and copper ions gain electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-18 -->

Explanation: Magnesium is oxidised, losing two electrons per atom, and the Cu²⁺ ions are reduced by gaining those electrons to form copper atoms. Electron transfer is what drives all metal displacement reactions.

**A156.** B. magnesium oxide and hydrogen gas.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-19 -->

Explanation: Mg(s) + H₂O(g) → MgO(s) + H₂(g). Hydrogen, not oxygen, is the gas given off, as the metal displaces hydrogen from water.

**A157.** D. is coated by a thin, unreactive layer of aluminium oxide that protects the metal beneath.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-20 -->

Explanation: Aluminium reacts rapidly with oxygen to form a tightly bonded Al₂O₃ layer that prevents further reaction. It is the oxide coating, not low reactivity, that protects it.

**A158.** B. Fe(s) + 2HCl(aq) → FeCl₂(aq) + H₂(g)
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-21 -->

Explanation: Iron reacts with dilute acid to form iron(II) chloride and hydrogen. The Fe³⁺ product is not formed under these conditions, and chlorine is not produced because Cl⁻ is not oxidised.

**A159.** D. a greater surface area exposed to the acid.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-22 -->

Explanation: More surface atoms are in contact with the acid, so collisions are more frequent. The metal's intrinsic reactivity as an element is the same for powder and ribbon.

**A160.** Ratio for a cube = 6 ÷ side length, so for 10 nm: 6 ÷ 10 = 0.60 nm⁻¹ [1]; 1.0 cm = 1.0 × 10⁷ nm, so ratio = 6 ÷ (1.0 × 10⁷) = 6.0 × 10⁻⁷ nm⁻¹ [1]; the 10 nm cube's ratio is 10⁶ (one million) times larger [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-23 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Covalent substances

#### Lewis structures and covalent bonding

**A161.** B. 1
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-1 -->

Explanation: N has 5 valence electrons. Three are used in N–H bonds, and the remaining two form one lone pair, which completes an octet around N.

**A162.** C. N₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-2 -->

Explanation: Each N atom has 5 valence electrons and needs 3 more, so the two atoms share three pairs (N≡N). O₂ contains a double bond, and H₂O and CH₄ contain only single bonds.

**A163.** B. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-3 -->

Explanation: The structure is O=C=O. Carbon has no lone pairs, and each O has two lone pairs, giving 4 in total. 16 valence electrons − 8 bonding electrons = 8 electrons = 4 lone pairs.

**A164.** C. 6
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-4 -->

Explanation: Ethene has four C–H single bonds (4 pairs) and one C=C double bond (2 pairs), giving 6 bonding pairs. The 12 valence electrons are all used in bonding, so there are no lone pairs.

**A165.** D. the electrostatic attraction between a shared pair of electrons and the nuclei of the two bonded atoms.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-5 -->

Explanation: In a covalent bond, two atoms (usually non-metals) share a pair of electrons, and both nuclei are attracted to it. The other options describe ionic bonding, metallic bonding and intermolecular forces.

**A166.** Carbon is the central atom; C forms a double bond to O and single bonds to each of the two H atoms; O has two lone pairs while C and H have none (C and O have octets, H has 2 electrons).
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A167.** Valence electrons: 5 + 3 × 7 = 26; 3 bonding pairs (three P–Cl single bonds); lone pairs: 1 on P + 3 on each Cl = 10 lone pairs (26 − 6 = 20 electrons).
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A168.** Melting only requires overcoming weak intermolecular (dispersion) forces between molecules, not the strong covalent bonds within them, so little energy is needed; the molecules are neutral and all electrons are localised in bonds or lone pairs, so there are no free charged particles to carry a current.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A169.** C. 18
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-9 -->

Explanation: S has 6 valence electrons and each O has 6, so 6 + 2 × 6 = 18. The value 12 counts only the two O atoms' electrons and leaves out S.

**A170.** B. BF₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-10 -->

Explanation: B has only 3 valence electrons, so in BF₃ it forms three single bonds and has just 6 electrons around it. N in NH₃, O in H₂O and C in CF₄ all reach 8 electrons.

**A171.** C. 12
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-11 -->

Explanation: Each Cl forms one bond to C and has 3 lone pairs, so 4 × 3 = 12 lone pairs. Carbon has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs, so 4 is not the total.

**A172.** A. 2
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-12 -->

Explanation: There are 2 × 5 + 4 = 14 valence electrons, and the 5 bonds use 10 of them. The remaining 4 electrons form 2 lone pairs, one on each N atom.

**A173.** B. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-13 -->

Explanation: Carbon has 4 valence electrons and needs 4 more to complete an octet, so it forms four covalent bonds. Three bonds would leave carbon without an octet.

**A174.** C. 10
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-14 -->

Explanation: There are two C–H single bonds (2 pairs) and one C≡C triple bond (3 pairs), giving 5 shared pairs or 10 shared electrons. The value 6 counts only the three pairs in the triple bond and ignores the two C–H bonds.

**A175.** D. Hydrogen can form only one covalent bond because it needs just two electrons to fill its valence shell.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-15 -->

Explanation: H has one valence electron and is stable with 2 electrons, so one shared pair is enough. A central atom must bond to at least two others, which H cannot do.

**A176.** A. 3
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-16 -->

Explanation: Cl has 7 valence electrons and uses one in the H–Cl bond, leaving 6 electrons as 3 lone pairs. Cl would have only 1 lone pair if it had formed three bonds, which is not the case.

**A177.** B. 5
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-17 -->

Explanation: The bonds are 3 C–H, 1 C–O and 1 O–H, all single, giving 5 bonding pairs. The oxygen's remaining 4 electrons form 2 lone pairs, which are not counted as bonding pairs.

**A178.** C. C≡C in ethyne, C₂H₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-18 -->

Explanation: More shared pairs pull the two nuclei closer together, so the triple bond is shortest (and strongest). The lengths differ because the number of shared pairs differs.

**A179.** D. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-19 -->

Explanation: A double bond consists of two shared pairs, which is 4 electrons. Two electrons is the number shared in a single bond.

**A180.** A. both electrons in that bond were supplied by the nitrogen atom.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-20 -->

Explanation: The N atom in NH₃ donates its lone pair to an H⁺ ion, which has no electrons, so both electrons in the new bond come from N. Once formed, the bond is identical to the other N–H bonds.

**A181.** B. 1
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-21 -->

Explanation: The structure is H–C≡N. H and C have no lone pairs, and N has 5 − 3 = 2 non-bonding electrons, which is one lone pair. The total of 10 valence electrons is shared as 4 bonding pairs and 1 lone pair.

**A182.** D. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-22 -->

Explanation: There are 14 valence electrons and 3 bonds use 6, leaving 8 electrons or 4 lone pairs (2 on each O). Counting only 2 lone pairs would leave each O without an octet.

**A183.** D. 24
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-23 -->

Explanation: 4 + 3 × 6 = 22, and the 2− charge adds 2 extra electrons, giving 24. Using 22 forgets to add the electrons that give the ion its negative charge.

**A184.** A. 8
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-24 -->

Explanation: There are 6 + 2 × 7 = 20 valence electrons and 2 bonds use 4, leaving 16 electrons or 8 lone pairs (2 on O and 3 on each F). Using 6 would count only the lone pairs on the fluorine atoms.

**A185.** A bonding pair is a pair of electrons shared between two atoms, forming a covalent bond [1]. A lone pair is a pair of valence electrons that belongs to one atom only and is not involved in bonding [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A186.** N₂ contains a triple bond (three shared pairs) whereas O₂ contains a double bond (two shared pairs) [1]. More shared pairs mean a greater electrostatic attraction between the shared electrons and the two nuclei [1]. This makes the N≡N bond shorter and stronger, so more energy is needed to break it [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A187.** Total valence electrons = 4 + 2 × 6 = 16 [1]. Carbon is the central atom and forms a double bond to each sulfur atom (S=C=S) [1]. Each S atom has two lone pairs and C has none, so all atoms have an octet [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A188.** Oxygen (6 valence electrons) shares one electron with each of two hydrogen atoms, forming two single bonds, and has two lone pairs, giving it 8 electrons (an octet) [1]. Each hydrogen shares one pair, giving it 2 electrons in its valence shell, the same as helium [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A189.** Non-metal atoms have high and similar attractions for electrons, so neither atom can remove electrons completely from the other [1]. Instead they share electrons, so that each atom attains a stable noble-gas electron configuration [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A190.** In CH₄, the central C has 4 bonding pairs and 0 lone pairs [1]. In NH₃, the central N has 3 bonding pairs and 1 lone pair [1]. In H₂O, the central O has 2 bonding pairs and 2 lone pairs, so the central atom has an octet (4 pairs in total) in each molecule [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A191.** Total valence electrons = 2 × 4 + 6 × 1 + 6 = 20 [1]. All bonds are single bonds: C–C, C–O, O–H and five C–H bonds [1]. This gives 8 bonding pairs (16 electrons) [1]. The remaining 4 electrons form 2 lone pairs, both on the oxygen atom, and no other atom has lone pairs [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-31 -->

Explanation:

#### VSEPR and molecular shape

**A192.** D. trigonal pyramidal.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-1 -->

Explanation: N has four electron pairs around it (3 bonding and 1 lone pair), arranged roughly tetrahedrally. Molecular shape describes only the positions of the atoms, so NH₃ is trigonal pyramidal. Trigonal planar would require three bonding pairs and no lone pairs.

**A193.** B. CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-2 -->

Explanation: In CO₂, carbon has two double bonds (two regions of electron density) and no lone pairs, so the two regions are 180° apart. H₂O and H₂S have lone pairs on the central atom and are V-shaped, and SO₂ is also bent.

**A194.** B. 104.5°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-3 -->

Explanation: Water has four electron pairs around O (2 bonding, 2 lone). Lone pairs repel more strongly than bonding pairs, so the angle is squeezed from the tetrahedral 109.5° to about 104.5°.

**A195.** C. SiH₄
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-4 -->

Explanation: Si has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs, so the four H atoms sit at the corners of a tetrahedron. PH₃ is pyramidal, H₂S is V-shaped and HCN is linear.

**A196.** A. trigonal planar, 120°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-5 -->

Explanation: A double bond counts as one region of electron density. Three regions with no lone pairs spread out in a flat triangle at about 120°. A pyramidal shape would need a lone pair on C.

**A197.** The O atom has four electron pairs (two bonding, two lone pairs) which repel each other and arrange themselves tetrahedrally to be as far apart as possible; the shape is described by the atoms only, so the two H atoms and O form a V (bent) shape with an angle of about 104.5°.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A198.** (a) HCN: linear, 180° (two regions of electron density around C, no lone pairs); (b) SiCl₄: tetrahedral, 109.5° (four bonding pairs, no lone pairs on Si).
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A199.** Both central atoms have four electron pairs arranged tetrahedrally; N in ammonia has one lone pair, which repels more strongly than bonding pairs, pushing the N–H bonds closer together and reducing the angle, while methane has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-8 -->

Explanation:

**A200.** A. trigonal planar.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-9 -->

Explanation: Three regions of electron density with no lone pairs spread out in one plane at 120°. Trigonal pyramidal would need a lone pair on B.

**A201.** B. NCl₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-10 -->

Explanation: N in NCl₃ has three bonding pairs and one lone pair, so the atoms form a pyramid. BF₃ is trigonal planar, CCl₄ is tetrahedral and OF₂ is V-shaped.

**A202.** C. 2
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-11 -->

Explanation: O has six valence electrons and uses two in bonding to the two F atoms, leaving four electrons, which form two lone pairs. This gives a V-shaped molecule.

**A203.** D. V-shaped, slightly less than 120°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-12 -->

Explanation: Three regions of electron density are arranged in a triangle, but only the two atoms are counted in the shape, so SO₂ is V-shaped. The lone pair squeezes the angle slightly below 120°. SO₂ is not linear like CO₂.

**A204.** A. 180°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-13 -->

Explanation: Two regions of electron density are furthest apart when 180° from each other, so the molecule is linear. 120° would need three regions.

**A205.** B. tetrahedral.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-14 -->

Explanation: All four electron pairs around N are bonding pairs, so the H atoms sit at the corners of a tetrahedron. Unlike NH₃, there is no lone pair to make the shape pyramidal.

**A206.** C. trigonal pyramidal.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-15 -->

Explanation: O has three bonding pairs and one lone pair, so the three H atoms form a pyramid with O at the apex. It is isoelectronic with NH₃ and has the same shape.

**A207.** D. trigonal planar, about 120°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-16 -->

Explanation: The C=C double bond counts as one region, plus two C–H bonds, giving three regions and no lone pairs. These spread into a flat triangle at about 120°.

**A208.** A. linear.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-17 -->

Explanation: Each C has two regions of electron density (one C–H bond and one triple bond) and no lone pairs, so the arrangement is linear with a 180° angle.

**A209.** B. tetrahedral.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-18 -->

Explanation: C has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs, so the four atoms are at the corners of a tetrahedron. Having different atoms attached does not change the basic shape.

**A210.** C. H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-19 -->

Explanation: The angles are 109.5° for CH₄, about 107° for NH₃, 104.5° for H₂O and 180° for CO₂. Water has two lone pairs, which repel the strongest and squeeze the bonding pairs closest.

**A211.** D. lone pair–lone pair > lone pair–bonding pair > bonding pair–bonding pair.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-20 -->

Explanation: Lone pairs are held by one nucleus only and occupy more space, so lone pair repulsions are strongest. Bonding pairs are held between two nuclei, so they repel least.

**A212.** A. tetrahedral and V-shaped.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-21 -->

Explanation: S has four electron pairs (two bonding, two lone), arranged tetrahedrally. Only the atoms are used to name the shape, which is V-shaped.

**A213.** B. NH₃ and PCl₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-22 -->

Explanation: Both NH₃ and PCl₃ have three bonding pairs and one lone pair on the central atom, so both are trigonal pyramidal. CH₄ is tetrahedral and BF₃ is trigonal planar.

**A214.** D. is attracted to only one nucleus and so occupies more space.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-23 -->

Explanation: A lone pair is held by only the central atom's nucleus, so it spreads out more and repels neighbouring pairs more strongly. A bonding pair is shared by two nuclei and is more confined.

**A215.** In CO₂ the carbon atom has two regions of electron density and no lone pairs [1]; these are furthest apart at 180°, so the molecule is linear [1]; in SO₂ the sulfur atom has three regions including one lone pair, so the two bonded atoms are not opposite each other and the shape is V-shaped [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-24 -->

Explanation:

**A216.** Both central atoms have four electron pairs arranged tetrahedrally [1]; water has two lone pairs but ammonia has only one [1]; the extra lone pair repels the bonding pairs more strongly, squeezing the angle further below 109.5° in water [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-25 -->

Explanation:

**A217.** The arrangement of electron pairs includes both bonding and lone pairs; in NH₃ the four pairs are arranged tetrahedrally [1]; the shape describes only the positions of the atoms, so with one lone pair the NH₃ molecule is trigonal pyramidal [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-26 -->

Explanation:

#### Electronegativity and polarity

**A218.** D. H–F
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-1 -->

Explanation: Fluorine is the most electronegative element, so the H–F bond has the largest electronegativity difference (about 1.8). C–H is almost non-polar, with a difference of about 0.4.

**A219.** C. CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-2 -->

Explanation: CO₂ is linear, so the two equal C=O dipoles point in opposite directions and cancel. NH₃ and H₂O are polar because their asymmetrical shapes leave a net dipole, and HCl has only one polar bond.

**A220.** B. Water, H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-3 -->

Explanation: Water molecules are polar, so they orient in the electric field and are attracted towards the rod. Hexane and cyclohexane are non-polar hydrocarbons, and CCl₄ is symmetrical with cancelling dipoles.

**A221.** A. Caesium and fluorine
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-4 -->

Explanation: Caesium has one of the lowest electronegativities and fluorine the highest, giving the largest electronegativity difference and therefore the greatest ionic character. Sodium iodide is also ionic, but with a smaller difference.

**A222.** B. the ability of an atom to attract the shared electrons in a covalent bond.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-5 -->

Explanation: Electronegativity measures how strongly an atom attracts bonding electrons towards itself. The first option defines first ionisation energy, which follows a related but different trend.

**A223.** Cl is more electronegative than C, so each C–Cl bond is polar (Cδ+–Clδ−); the molecule is tetrahedral and symmetrical, so the four equal bond dipoles cancel and there is no overall dipole.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-6 -->

Explanation:

**A224.** O is more electronegative than H, so the shared electrons in each O–H bond are pulled towards O, making each bond polar; O carries a partial negative charge (δ−) and each H a partial positive charge (δ+); the molecule is V-shaped (asymmetrical), so the bond dipoles do not cancel and there is a net dipole.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-7 -->

Explanation:

**A225.** H–Br < H–Cl < H–F; electronegativity decreases down group 17 because the valence shell is further from the nucleus (more shells, same core charge), so the halogen's attraction for the shared electrons, and therefore the electronegativity difference with H, decreases from F to Br.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-8 -->

Explanation:

**A226.** C. Oxygen
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-9 -->

Explanation: Oxygen is small and has a high effective nuclear charge, so it is more electronegative than nitrogen, sulfur and chlorine. Chlorine is tempting because it is a halogen, but its larger atomic radius makes it less electronegative than oxygen.

**A227.** A. Si, P, S, Cl
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-10 -->

Explanation: Electronegativity increases from left to right across a period, so the order across period 3 is Si < P < S < Cl. The reverse order lists the elements by decreasing electronegativity.

**A228.** B. Cl–Cl
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-11 -->

Explanation: Both atoms in Cl–Cl are identical, so they attract the shared electrons equally and the bond is non-polar. C–O and N–H join different elements with different electronegativities, so each is polar.

**A229.** D. Nδ− and Hδ+
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-12 -->

Explanation: Nitrogen is more electronegative than hydrogen, so the shared electrons are drawn towards N, giving it a partial negative charge. Sharing the electrons does not make them equally shared unless the atoms are identical.

**A230.** A. CH₃Cl
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-13 -->

Explanation: In CH₃Cl the C–Cl bond is much more polar than the C–H bonds, so the dipoles do not cancel and there is a net dipole. CH₄, CCl₄ and CO₂ are all symmetrical, so their bond dipoles cancel.

**A231.** C. BF₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-14 -->

Explanation: BF₃ is trigonal planar, so its three equal B–F dipoles cancel. NH₃ and PCl₃ are trigonal pyramidal and H₂S is bent, so their dipoles do not cancel.

**A232.** B. C–H
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-15 -->

Explanation: Carbon and hydrogen have very similar electronegativities, so the C–H bond has the smallest electronegativity difference. F–H has the largest difference of these four.

**A233.** A. both the polarity of its bonds and its shape.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-16 -->

Explanation: A molecule is polar only if it has polar bonds whose dipoles do not cancel because of its shape. Polar bonds alone are not enough, as shown by CO₂ and CCl₄.

**A234.** C. nuclear charge increases while the valence electrons stay in the same shell.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-17 -->

Explanation: Across a period the number of protons rises but the valence shell stays the same, so the nucleus attracts bonding electrons more strongly. Atoms get smaller, not larger, across a period.

**A235.** B. towards the more electronegative atom.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-18 -->

Explanation: The dipole arrow points towards the end of the bond that carries the partial negative charge, which is the more electronegative atom. Pointing it to the δ+ atom is a common reversal.

**A236.** D. the C–H and C≡N bond dipoles are different and do not cancel.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-19 -->

Explanation: For a linear molecule to be non-polar the two end bonds must be identical, as in CO₂. In HCN the two bonds differ, so their dipoles are unequal and leave a net dipole.

**A237.** A. is bent, so its bond dipoles do not cancel.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-20 -->

Explanation: SO₂ has a lone pair on sulfur that makes the molecule bent, so the dipoles add to give a net dipole. CO₂ is linear and its dipoles cancel.

**A238.** C. non-polar covalent to polar covalent to ionic.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-21 -->

Explanation: A difference of zero gives a non-polar bond, a moderate difference a polar covalent bond, and a very large difference approaches ionic bonding. The reverse order describes decreasing difference.

**A239.** B. Sulfur, because it is less electronegative than chlorine
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-22 -->

Explanation: Chlorine is more electronegative than sulfur, so it draws the shared electrons and sulfur becomes δ+. The bond is between different elements, so it is polar.

**A240.** N is more electronegative than H, so each N–H bond is polar with Nδ− and Hδ+ [1]; the molecule is trigonal pyramidal because of the lone pair on N, so it is asymmetrical [1]; the bond dipoles therefore do not cancel and the molecule has a net dipole [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-23 -->

Explanation:

**A241.** The three C–Cl bonds are strongly polar, whereas the C–H bond is almost non-polar [1]; the four bonds are not identical, so the molecule is not symmetrical in its charge distribution [1]; the bond dipoles do not cancel, so there is a net dipole [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-24 -->

Explanation:

**A242.** Na–Cl: 3.2 − 0.9 = 2.3, ionic [1]; H–Cl: 3.2 − 2.2 = 1.0, polar covalent [1]; Cl–Cl: 0, non-polar covalent [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-25 -->

Explanation:

**A243.** S is slightly more electronegative than H, so each S–H bond is slightly polar with Sδ− and Hδ+ [1]; H₂S is bent because of the two lone pairs on S, so it is asymmetrical [1]; the bond dipoles do not cancel, so H₂S is weakly polar [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-26 -->

Explanation:

**A244.** Polarity depends on the shape as well as the bonds, and in a symmetrical molecule such as CO₂ the equal bond dipoles cancel [1]; a diatomic molecule has only one bond, so there is nothing to cancel its dipole [1]; hence a diatomic molecule such as HCl with a polar bond has a net dipole and is polar [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-27 -->

Explanation:

#### Intermolecular forces

**A245.** B. hydrogen bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-1 -->

Explanation: Methanol has an H atom bonded directly to a highly electronegative O atom. This allows hydrogen bonding with lone pairs on neighbouring O atoms, which is stronger than dispersion and other dipole–dipole forces. Covalent bonds act within molecules, not between them.

**A246.** C. the larger number of electrons gives stronger dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-2 -->

Explanation: Noble gases are non-polar monatomic particles, so only dispersion forces act between them. Larger atoms have more electrons, which form larger, more easily formed temporary dipoles, so the attraction is stronger.

**A247.** D. Chloromethane, CH₃Cl
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-3 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen bonding requires H bonded directly to N, O or F. In CH₃Cl the H atoms are bonded to C, so it shows only dipole–dipole and dispersion forces.

**A248.** B. the compact shape of 2,2-dimethylpropane reducing contact between molecules, so it has weaker dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-4 -->

Explanation: Both molecules have the same number of electrons and are non-polar, so only dispersion forces act. The long chain of pentane gives a larger contact area between molecules, so its dispersion forces are stronger. Neither substance can hydrogen bond.

**A249.** A. water molecules form hydrogen bonds with each other.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-5 -->

Explanation: Boiling separates molecules, so it depends on intermolecular forces, not bond strength within molecules. Water forms hydrogen bonds because O is small and highly electronegative, whereas S is not electronegative enough. H₂S actually has more electrons than H₂O and is V-shaped.

**A250.** In ice, each water molecule forms hydrogen bonds to four neighbours in a fixed, open hexagonal lattice; this arrangement holds molecules further apart than in the liquid; when ice melts some hydrogen bonds break and molecules pack more closely, so the same mass occupies a smaller volume in the liquid (ice has a lower density and floats).
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-6 -->

Explanation:

**A251.** (a) HF molecules form hydrogen bonds (H bonded to highly electronegative F), which are much stronger than the intermolecular forces in the other hydrogen halides; (b) HCl, HBr and HI cannot hydrogen bond; from HCl to HI the number of electrons increases, so dispersion forces become stronger, outweighing the decrease in dipole–dipole attraction as bond polarity falls.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-7 -->

Explanation:

**A252.** (a) CH₄: dispersion forces only (non-polar); (b) HCl: dispersion and dipole–dipole forces; (c) NH₃: dispersion, dipole–dipole and hydrogen bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-8 -->

Explanation:

**A253.** D. SnH₄
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-9 -->

Explanation: All four are non-polar and have only dispersion forces. SnH₄ has the most electrons, so its dispersion forces are strongest. CH₄ has the weakest forces despite its strong C–H bonds, because boiling does not break covalent bonds.

**A254.** A. An attraction between an H atom bonded to N in one molecule and the lone pair on the N atom of a neighbouring molecule
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-10 -->

Explanation: A hydrogen bond forms between an H atom covalently bonded to N, O or F and a lone pair on a highly electronegative atom of a neighbouring molecule. Attraction between temporary dipoles describes dispersion forces, not hydrogen bonding.

**A255.** B. dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-11 -->

Explanation: CO₂ is linear and symmetrical, so its polar C=O bond dipoles cancel and the molecule is non-polar. Therefore only dispersion forces act between molecules, even though the bonds themselves are polar.

**A256.** C. Dispersion force, hydrogen bond, covalent bond
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-12 -->

Explanation: Covalent bonds within molecules are much stronger than any intermolecular force. Between molecules, a hydrogen bond is typically stronger than a dispersion force of a similar-sized non-polar molecule.

**A257.** C. ethanol molecules can form hydrogen bonds with each other.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-13 -->

Explanation: Ethanol has an H atom bonded directly to O, so it forms hydrogen bonds. In dimethyl ether all H atoms are bonded to C, so only weaker dipole–dipole and dispersion forces act. Both have the same number of electrons.

**A258.** A. CHCl₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-14 -->

Explanation: CHCl₃ is tetrahedral but the C–H and C–Cl bond dipoles do not cancel, so the molecule is polar. CCl₄, CO₂ and BF₃ are symmetrical, so their bond dipoles cancel and they are non-polar.

**A259.** B. Dispersion forces between I₂ molecules
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-15 -->

Explanation: I₂ is a non-polar molecular substance, so only dispersion forces hold the molecules together in the solid. Changing state does not break the I–I covalent bonds, which remain intact in the gas.

**A260.** D. more electrons and a larger surface area, giving stronger dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-16 -->

Explanation: Both are non-polar hydrocarbons, so only dispersion forces act. The longer octane molecule has more electrons and greater contact with its neighbours, so more energy is needed to separate the molecules.

**A261.** A. H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-17 -->

Explanation: Each water molecule has two H atoms available and two lone pairs, so it can form up to four hydrogen bonds. HF has one H atom, NH₃ has one lone pair and CH₃OH has only one H atom bonded to O, so each can form fewer.

**A262.** B. Hydrogen bonds between molecules are overcome, but the molecules remain H₂O.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-18 -->

Explanation: Boiling is a physical change in which intermolecular forces are overcome and molecules separate. The strong O–H covalent bonds within each molecule are not broken.

**A263.** D. has three –OH groups per molecule, so it forms more hydrogen bonds.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-19 -->

Explanation: Each glycerol molecule has three O–H groups, allowing extensive hydrogen bonding between molecules, which makes them harder to flow past each other. Ethanol has only one O–H group per molecule.

**A264.** A. temporary dipoles caused by momentary uneven distribution of electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-20 -->

Explanation: The electrons in a molecule are constantly moving, so a temporary dipole can form and induce a dipole in a neighbouring molecule. Permanent dipoles cause dipole–dipole forces instead.

**A265.** B. HCl is polar, so it has dipole–dipole forces as well as dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-21 -->

Explanation: With the same number of electrons the dispersion forces are similar. HCl is polar and so has additional dipole–dipole attraction. It cannot hydrogen bond because Cl is not N, O or F, and F₂ is non-polar.

**A266.** C. Ne, HCl, H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-22 -->

Explanation: Ne has only weak dispersion forces, HCl adds dipole–dipole forces, and H₂O forms strong hydrogen bonds, giving the order Ne < HCl < H₂O.

**A267.** A hydrogen bond is an attractive intermolecular force between a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom (N, O or F) in one molecule and a lone pair on a N, O or F atom of a neighbouring molecule [1]; the molecule must therefore contain H bonded directly to N, O or F and the other atom must have a lone pair [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-23 -->

Explanation:

**A268.** These are non-polar molecules, so only dispersion forces act between them [1]; the number of electrons and the molecular size increase from methane to butane [1]; stronger dispersion forces mean more energy is needed to separate the molecules, so the boiling point rises [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-24 -->

Explanation:

**A269.** Both have similar numbers of electrons, so their dispersion forces are of similar strength [1]; ethanol has an H atom bonded directly to O, so its molecules form hydrogen bonds [1]; hydrogen bonds are stronger than the dispersion forces alone in propane, so more energy is needed to boil ethanol [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-25 -->

Explanation:

**A270.** Intramolecular forces (covalent bonds) act within a molecule, whereas intermolecular forces act between separate molecules [1]; when water boils only intermolecular forces (hydrogen bonds and dispersion forces) are overcome, and the O–H covalent bonds stay intact [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-26 -->

Explanation:

**A271.** Each water molecule has two H atoms and two lone pairs, so it can form up to four hydrogen bonds [1]; HF has only one H atom per molecule and NH₃ has only one lone pair, so each can form fewer hydrogen bonds per molecule [1]; more extensive hydrogen bonding in water requires more energy to overcome, giving the highest boiling point [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-27 -->

Explanation:

**A272.** Both are non-polar, so only dispersion forces act between molecules [1]; I₂ has many more electrons and is larger than F₂, so its dispersion forces are much stronger, which holds the molecules in a solid at room temperature [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-28 -->

Explanation:

#### Covalent lattices and carbon allotropes

**A273.** B. covalently bonded to four other carbon atoms in a tetrahedral arrangement.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-1 -->

Explanation: Diamond is a three-dimensional covalent network in which every C forms four single covalent bonds arranged tetrahedrally. Bonding to three atoms in flat layers describes graphite, and C₆₀ is a fullerene.

**A274.** A. each carbon atom has one delocalised electron that can move along the layers.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-2 -->

Explanation: Each C in graphite forms three covalent bonds within its layer. The fourth valence electron is delocalised across the layer and can move when a voltage is applied. If all valence electrons were localised in bonds, as in diamond, graphite would not conduct.

**A275.** C. Silicon dioxide, SiO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-3 -->

Explanation: In SiO₂ (quartz), each Si is covalently bonded to four O atoms and each O to two Si atoms in a continuous three-dimensional network. CO₂ and I₂ are covalent molecular substances, and NaCl is ionic.

**A276.** D. the weak dispersion forces between its layers allow the layers to slide over each other.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-4 -->

Explanation: Within each layer the covalent bonds are very strong, but the layers are held together only by weak dispersion forces, so they slide easily. Graphite actually has a very high sublimation point, above 3600 °C.

**A277.** A. a single layer of carbon atoms arranged in hexagons, as in one layer of graphite.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-5 -->

Explanation: Graphene is a one-atom-thick sheet of hexagonally bonded carbon. It is extremely strong, conducts electricity well and is nearly transparent. C₆₀ is buckminsterfullerene, and the tetrahedral network is diamond.

**A278.** In diamond each C is covalently bonded to four others in a rigid 3D tetrahedral network, so it is very hard, and all valence electrons are localised in bonds so it cannot conduct; in graphite each C bonds to three others in layers held together by weak dispersion forces, so layers slide and it is soft; the fourth electron of each C is delocalised within the layers, so graphite conducts.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A279.** SiO₂ is a covalent network lattice, so melting requires breaking many strong covalent Si–O bonds throughout the structure; CO₂ consists of discrete non-polar molecules, so only weak dispersion forces between molecules must be overcome.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A280.** Use: e.g. reinforcing lightweight sports equipment or composite materials, or as tiny electrical conductors in electronics; property and explanation: very high tensile strength because of the strong covalent bonds in the continuous hexagonal carbon network, or electrical conductivity because of the delocalised electrons in the graphene structure.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-8 -->

Explanation:

**A281.** C. many strong covalent bonds throughout the lattice must be broken.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-9 -->

Explanation: Melting a covalent network lattice requires breaking a huge number of strong covalent bonds, which takes a great deal of energy. Diamond contains no discrete molecules, no ions and no delocalised electrons.

**A282.** A. in hexagonal rings, with each carbon covalently bonded to three others.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-10 -->

Explanation: Each carbon in a graphite layer forms three covalent bonds in a flat hexagonal pattern. Four bonds per carbon in a tetrahedral arrangement describes diamond.

**A283.** D. different structural forms of the same element in the same state.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-11 -->

Explanation: Diamond, graphite and C₆₀ are all pure carbon with different bonding arrangements. Atoms differing in neutron number are isotopes, not allotropes.

**A284.** B. only weak dispersion forces between C₆₀ molecules need to be overcome.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-12 -->

Explanation: C₆₀ is a discrete covalent molecule, so changing state only overcomes weak dispersion forces between molecules. The covalent bonds within each molecule are not broken.

**A285.** C. all four valence electrons of each carbon atom are localised in covalent bonds.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-13 -->

Explanation: Every carbon in diamond uses all four valence electrons in covalent bonds, so no charge carriers are free to move. Graphite is also a non-metal yet conducts, so the last option is wrong.

**A286.** A. four oxygen atoms, and each oxygen atom is bonded to two silicon atoms.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-14 -->

Explanation: Each Si bonds to four O and each O to two Si, which gives the 1 : 2 ratio in SiO₂. The formula is an empirical ratio, not a molecule.

**A287.** D. Silicon carbide
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-15 -->

Explanation: Silicon carbide, SiC, is a compound of silicon and carbon, so it cannot be an allotrope. The other three are all forms of pure carbon.

**A288.** B. Three
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-16 -->

Explanation: Each carbon in C₆₀ is bonded to three other carbons, as in graphite, with the sheet curved into a sphere. The fourth valence electron is delocalised over the molecule.

**A289.** A. a covalent network lattice, so it is very hard.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-17 -->

Explanation: SiC has a three-dimensional network of strong covalent bonds like diamond, making it extremely hard. Covalent molecular substances are soft even when dispersion forces are significant.

**A290.** D. Carbon dioxide, silicon dioxide, diamond
