<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-18 -->

Explanation: CO₂ is molecular with only weak dispersion forces, so it has the lowest value. SiO₂ (about 1700 °C) and diamond (above 3500 °C) are both network lattices, with the stronger C–C network of diamond giving the highest.

**A291.** B. strong covalent bonds within layers make melting difficult, but weak dispersion forces between layers allow sliding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-19 -->

Explanation: Softness relates to the weak forces between layers, while the very high sublimation point relates to the strong covalent bonding within the layers. The atoms within a layer are strongly bonded, so the first option is reversed.

**A292.** B. 2.5 × 10²²
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-20 -->

Explanation: n(C) = 0.50 ÷ 12.0 = 0.0417 mol, and N = 0.0417 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.5 × 10²². The option 3.6 × 10²⁴ comes from multiplying by the molar mass instead of dividing.

**A293.** A. delocalised electrons can move within a layer but not easily from one layer to the next.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-21 -->

Explanation: The delocalised electrons are spread across each layer, and the layers are separated by relatively large gaps. Graphite contains no ions.

**A294.** A. a sheet of graphene rolled into a cylinder.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-22 -->

Explanation: A nanotube is a graphene sheet rolled up, so each carbon still bonds to three others in hexagons. The hollow sphere is C₆₀, and stacked layers with weak forces between them describe graphite.

**A295.** Both are covalent network lattices, with atoms joined by strong covalent bonds in a continuous three-dimensional arrangement [1]; in diamond each C bonds to four C atoms, while in SiO₂ each Si bonds to four O and each O to two Si [1]; shared properties include very high melting point and hardness, and being non-conductors of electricity [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-23 -->

Explanation:

**A296.** C₆₀ is a discrete spherical molecule of 60 carbon atoms arranged in hexagons and pentagons, with each carbon bonded to three others [1]; in the solid, separate C₆₀ molecules are held together only by weak dispersion forces [1]; these are easily overcome, whereas diamond is a rigid network of strong covalent bonds throughout [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-24 -->

Explanation:

**A297.** Each carbon atom in graphene forms three covalent bonds with neighbouring carbons, leaving one valence electron that is not used in bonding [1]; this electron is delocalised across the sheet and is free to move when a voltage is applied, carrying charge [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-25 -->

Explanation:

**A298.** Drill bits use hardness of diamond [1]; because each C is covalently bonded to four others in a rigid three-dimensional tetrahedral lattice [1]; electrodes use the electrical conductivity of graphite [1]; because each C bonds to only three others, leaving one delocalised electron per atom that can move through the layers [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-26 -->

Explanation:

**A299.** The error is that diamond is not held together by dispersion forces between molecules, as it contains no discrete molecules [1]; it is a covalent network lattice and melting requires breaking many strong covalent bonds between carbon atoms [1].
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-27 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 2: Quantifying and classifying materials

#### Relative masses and mass spectrometry

**A300.** D. 77.5%
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-1 -->

Explanation: Let x be the fraction of ³⁵Cl: 35x + 37(1 − x) = 35.45, so 37 − 2x = 35.45 and x = 0.775, i.e. 77.5%. 22.5% is the abundance of ³⁷Cl, not ³⁵Cl.

**A301.** B. Positively charged ions, separated according to their mass-to-charge ratio
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-2 -->

Explanation: The sample is ionised to form positive ions, which are accelerated and deflected; the amount of deflection depends on m/z. Neutral atoms cannot be accelerated or deflected by electric or magnetic fields, so they are not detected.

**A302.** A. one-twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-3 -->

Explanation: The modern scale defines one-twelfth of the mass of a ¹²C atom as exactly 1 unit, so ¹²C has a relative isotopic mass of exactly 12. Hydrogen and oxygen scales were used historically but are not the current standard.

**A303.** B. 69.8
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-4 -->

Explanation: Ar = (69 × 60.1 + 71 × 39.9) ÷ 100 = (4146.9 + 2832.9) ÷ 100 = 69.8. 70.0 would be the simple average, which ignores the higher abundance of ⁶⁹Ga.

**A304.** C. a ⁷⁹Br–⁸¹Br molecule can form in two ways (⁷⁹Br first or ⁸¹Br first), so it is twice as likely as either ⁷⁹Br–⁷⁹Br or ⁸¹Br–⁸¹Br
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-5 -->

Explanation: With a 50 : 50 isotope ratio, the combinations 79–79, 79–81, 81–79 and 81–81 are equally likely, so the mixed molecule (m/z 160) appears twice as often. Ions with m/z 160 are deflected in between the other two, not the least.

**A305.** Ar = (24 × 78.99 + 25 × 10.00 + 26 × 11.01) ÷ 100; = (1895.76 + 250.0 + 286.26) ÷ 100 = 24.32 (no units, as it is a relative value).
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A306.** Relative isotopic mass is the mass of one atom of a particular isotope relative to one-twelfth of the mass of a ¹²C atom; relative atomic mass is the weighted mean of the relative isotopic masses of all naturally occurring isotopes of the element, taking their abundances into account; because most elements are a mixture of isotopes, the weighted mean lies between the isotopic masses and is usually not a whole number.
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A307.** Ar = (63 × 69.2 + 65 × 30.8) ÷ 100; = (4359.6 + 2002.0) ÷ 100 = 63.6; X is copper (Ar = 63.5 in the data book), the closest match.
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A308.** B. 6.92
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-9 -->

Explanation: Ar = (6 × 7.59 + 7 × 92.41) ÷ 100 = (45.54 + 646.87) ÷ 100 = 6.92. 6.50 is the simple average, which ignores that ⁷Li is far more abundant.

**A309.** C. 10.8
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-10 -->

Explanation: Ar = (10 × 19.9 + 11 × 80.1) ÷ 100 = (199 + 881.1) ÷ 100 = 10.8. 10.2 results from swapping the abundances, and 10.5 is the unweighted average.

**A310.** A. It is a ratio of masses, compared with one-twelfth of the mass of a carbon-12 atom
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-11 -->

Explanation: A relative mass is the mass of an atom divided by a standard mass, so the units cancel. It is not a mass in grams or any other unit; an average of masses would still carry units.

**A311.** D. ³⁷Cl⁺ is deflected less because it has the greater mass
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-12 -->

Explanation: For ions of the same charge, the heavier ion has greater inertia and is deflected less. Having the same charge does not mean equal deflection, since deflection also depends on mass.

**A312.** C. the relative abundance of ions of that m/z value
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-13 -->

Explanation: Peak height is proportional to the number of ions detected at that m/z, so it gives relative abundance. The horizontal axis, not the vertical, gives the mass-to-charge ratio.

**A313.** C. 62%
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-14 -->

Explanation: Let x be the fraction of ¹²¹Sb: 121x + 123(1 − x) = 121.76, so 123 − 2x = 121.76 and x = 0.62, i.e. 62%. 38% is the abundance of ¹²³Sb, and 50% would give an Ar of 122.

**A314.** A. Three peaks, at m/z 70, 72 and 74
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-15 -->

Explanation: The possible molecules are ³⁵Cl–³⁵Cl (70), ³⁵Cl–³⁷Cl (72) and ³⁷Cl–³⁷Cl (74). The two ways of forming the mixed molecule give the same m/z, so they produce one peak, not two.

**A315.** D. number of neutrons
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-16 -->

Explanation: Isotopes of an element have the same number of protons (and electrons) but different numbers of neutrons. The same electron configuration means their chemical properties are essentially identical.

**A316.** C. 28.1
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-17 -->

Explanation: Ar = (28 × 92.2 + 29 × 4.7 + 30 × 3.1) ÷ 100 = 28.11. 29.0 is the unweighted mean of the three values, which ignores the dominance of ²⁸Si.

**A317.** B. Silver (Ar = 107.9)
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-18 -->

Explanation: Ar = (107 × 51.8 + 109 × 48.2) ÷ 100 = 107.96, matching silver. Palladium has an Ar below 107, which is impossible for a mixture of isotopes of mass 107 and 109.

**A318.** A. 12
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-19 -->

Explanation: m/z is the mass number divided by the charge: 24 ÷ 2 = 12. Using 24 would ignore the 2+ charge.

**A319.** D. 36 and 38
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-20 -->

Explanation: ¹H³⁵Cl has m/z 36 and ¹H³⁷Cl has m/z 38. The peaks at 35 and 37 would be the Cl⁺ fragments, not the intact HCl molecular ions.

**A320.** B. Slightly above 52, because the heavier isotopes contribute more to the weighted mean than the lighter one
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-21 -->

Explanation: The weighted mean is dominated by the 52 peak, but the 53 and 54 isotopes pull it up more than the 50 isotope pulls it down (Ar ≈ 52.06). An unweighted mean ignores the abundances.

**A321.** A. It always lies between the lightest and heaviest relative isotopic masses of the element
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-22 -->

Explanation: A weighted mean must fall between the smallest and largest values being averaged. It is close to, but generally not equal to, the mass number of the most abundant isotope.

**A322.** Ar = (79 × 50.7 + 81 × 49.3) ÷ 100 [1]; = (4005.3 + 3993.3) ÷ 100 = 79.99 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-23 -->

Explanation:

**A323.** The sample is vaporised [1]; atoms are ionised (by electron bombardment) to form positive ions [1]; the ions are accelerated and deflected by a magnetic (or electric) field, with lighter ions deflected more than heavier ones of the same charge [1]; a detector records the ions at each m/z value, with peak height giving relative abundance [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-24 -->

Explanation:

**A324.** Let x be the fraction of ⁴¹K: 39(1 − x) + 41x = 39.10 [1]; 39 + 2x = 39.10, so x = 0.05 [1]; ⁴¹K = 5% and ³⁹K = 95% [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-25 -->

Explanation:

**A325.** The horizontal axis shows the mass-to-charge ratio (m/z) of the ions [1]; for a ⁶³Cu⁺ ion, m/z = 63 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-26 -->

Explanation:

**A326.** Ar = (20 × 90.5 + 21 × 0.3 + 22 × 9.2) ÷ 100 [1]; = (1810 + 6.3 + 202.4) ÷ 100 [1]; = 20.19 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-27 -->

Explanation:

**A327.** Both are isotopes of chlorine with 17 protons and 17 electrons [1]; they differ in neutron number (18 and 20), so their masses differ [1]; chemical properties depend on the electron configuration, which is the same for both [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-28 -->

Explanation:

#### The mole and molar mass

**A328.** B. 1.51 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-1 -->

Explanation: N = n × NA = 0.250 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.51 × 10²³. 2.41 × 10²⁴ results from dividing NA by n rather than multiplying.

**A329.** B. 164.1 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-2 -->

Explanation: M = 40.1 + 2 × (14.0 + 3 × 16.0) = 40.1 + 124.0 = 164.1 g mol⁻¹. 102.1 g mol⁻¹ results from forgetting the subscript 2 outside the brackets.

**A330.** D. 16.0 g of CH₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-3 -->

Explanation: Each of H₂O, CO₂ and CH₄ is 1.00 mol of molecules, but CH₄ has 5 atoms per molecule (5 mol of atoms) compared with 3 for H₂O and CO₂. 1.0 g of H₂ is 0.50 mol of molecules, or only 1.0 mol of atoms.

**A331.** A. 4.77 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-4 -->

Explanation: M(Na₂CO₃) = 2(23.0) + 12.0 + 3(16.0) = 106.0 g mol⁻¹; m = n × M = 0.0450 × 106.0 = 4.77 g. 4.25 × 10⁻⁴ g comes from dividing n by M.

**A332.** C. 7.2 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-5 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit contains 3 × 4 = 12 O atoms, so n(O) = 12 × 0.10 = 1.2 mol and N = 1.2 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 7.2 × 10²³. 2.4 × 10²³ counts only the 4 O in one sulfate ion.

**A333.** M(C₆H₁₂O₆) = 6(12.0) + 12(1.0) + 6(16.0) = 180.0 g mol⁻¹; n = 9.00 ÷ 180.0 = 0.0500 mol; N = 0.0500 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.01 × 10²² molecules.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A334.** n(H) = 1.20 × 10²⁴ ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.99 mol; each ethanol molecule has 6 H atoms, so n(C₂H₅OH) = 1.99 ÷ 6 = 0.332 mol; M(C₂H₅OH) = 46.0 g mol⁻¹, so m = 0.332 × 46.0 = 15.3 g.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A335.** One mole is the amount of substance containing 6.02 × 10²³ (Avogadro's number of) specified particles; both samples contain the same number of atoms, but each magnesium atom on average has about twice the mass of a carbon atom, so the molar mass (numerically equal to Ar in grams) is larger for magnesium.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-8 -->

Explanation:

**A336.** B. 0.100 mol
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-9 -->

Explanation: M(CO₂) = 12.0 + 2(16.0) = 44.0 g mol⁻¹, so n = 4.40 ÷ 44.0 = 0.100 mol. 10.0 mol results from inverting the formula (M ÷ m).

**A337.** C. 80.0 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-10 -->

Explanation: M = 2(14.0) + 4(1.0) + 3(16.0) = 80.0 g mol⁻¹. 66.0 g mol⁻¹ counts only one nitrogen atom.

**A338.** D. 3.01 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-11 -->

Explanation: N = n × NA = 0.500 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.01 × 10²³. 1.20 × 10²⁴ results from dividing NA by n (6.02 × 10²³ ÷ 0.500) rather than multiplying.

**A339.** A. 6.38 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-12 -->

Explanation: n = 1.20 × 10²³ ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 0.199 mol, and m = 0.199 × 32.0 = 6.38 g. 3.19 g wrongly uses 16.0 g mol⁻¹, the molar mass of O atoms.

**A340.** B. 3.6 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-13 -->

Explanation: n(H) = 3 × 0.20 = 0.60 mol, so N = 0.60 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.6 × 10²³. 1.2 × 10²³ is the number of NH₃ molecules, not H atoms.

**A341.** C. 0.50 mol of Fe₂O₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-14 -->

Explanation: Masses are 18.0 g, 32.0 g, 79.8 g and 25.0 g respectively, so 0.50 mol of Fe₂O₃ (M = 159.6 g mol⁻¹) is greatest. Having the most moles (2.0 mol CH₄) does not mean the greatest mass.

**A342.** D. 0.60 mol
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-15 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit contains 2 Cl, so n(Cl) = 2 × 0.30 = 0.60 mol. 0.90 mol counts all three ions or atoms in the formula.

**A343.** A. 249.6 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-16 -->

Explanation: M = 63.5 + 32.1 + 4(16.0) + 5(18.0) = 249.6 g mol⁻¹. 159.6 g mol⁻¹ is the anhydrous salt, ignoring the water of crystallisation.

**A344.** B. 1.95 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-17 -->

Explanation: M = 27.0 + 3(16.0 + 1.0) = 78.0 g mol⁻¹, so m = 0.0250 × 78.0 = 1.95 g. 0.675 g uses only the mass of aluminium.

**A345.** C. 1.5 mol
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-18 -->

Explanation: n(H₂O) = 9.0 ÷ 18.0 = 0.50 mol, and each molecule has 3 atoms, so n(atoms) = 1.5 mol. 0.50 mol counts molecules only.

**A346.** D. They contain the same number of atoms but have different masses.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-19 -->

Explanation: One mole of any element contains 6.02 × 10²³ atoms, but the molar masses differ (23.0 and 39.1 g mol⁻¹). Heavier atoms do not change the number of atoms in a mole.

**A347.** A. 1.8 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-20 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit gives 2 Na⁺ and 1 SO₄²⁻, i.e. 3 ions, so n(ions) = 0.30 mol and N = 1.8 × 10²³. 4.2 × 10²³ counts all 7 atoms rather than 3 ions.

**A348.** B. 2.99 × 10⁻²³ g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-21 -->

Explanation: Mass of one molecule = M ÷ NA = 18.0 ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.99 × 10⁻²³ g. 18.0 g is the mass of one mole, not one molecule.

**A349.** C. 7.00 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-22 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit has 2 N, so n(N) = 0.500 mol and m = 0.500 × 14.0 = 7.00 g. 3.50 g forgets the 2 N atoms per formula unit.

**A350.** M((NH₄)₂SO₄) = 2(14.0) + 8(1.0) + 32.1 + 4(16.0) = 132.1 g mol⁻¹ [1]; m = n × M = 0.350 × 132.1 = 46.2 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-23 -->

Explanation:

**A351.** M(CH₄) = 12.0 + 4(1.0) = 16.0 g mol⁻¹, so n = 5.00 ÷ 16.0 = 0.313 mol [1]; each molecule contains 5 atoms, so n(atoms) = 5 × 0.3125 = 1.56 mol [1]; N = 1.56 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 9.41 × 10²³ atoms [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-24 -->

Explanation:

**A352.** Relative molecular mass is the mass of a molecule relative to 1/12 of a carbon-12 atom, so it is a ratio with no units [1]; molar mass is the mass of one mole of the substance, in g mol⁻¹, and is numerically equal to the relative molecular mass [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-25 -->

Explanation:

**A353.** Each formula unit contains 6 O atoms, so n(Cu(NO₃)₂) = 0.600 ÷ 6 = 0.100 mol [1]; M = 63.5 + 2(14.0) + 6(16.0) = 187.5 g mol⁻¹ [1]; m = 0.100 × 187.5 = 18.8 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-26 -->

Explanation:

**A354.** n(H₂O) = 10.0 ÷ 18.0 = 0.556 mol [1]; N(H₂O) = 0.556 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.34 × 10²³ [1]; n(CO₂) = 10.0 ÷ 44.0 = 0.227 mol, so N(CO₂) = 0.227 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.37 × 10²³ [1]; the water sample contains more molecules because its molar mass is smaller [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-27 -->

Explanation:

**A355.** n = 3.01 × 10²² ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 0.0500 mol [1]; M = m ÷ n = 3.20 ÷ 0.0500 = 64.0 g mol⁻¹ [1]; this matches SO₂ (64.1 g mol⁻¹), not O₂ (32.0) or N₂ (28.0) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-28 -->

Explanation:

**A356.** n(Al) = 1000 ÷ 27.0 = 37.0 mol [1]; N = 37.0 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.23 × 10²⁵ atoms [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-29 -->

Explanation:

**A357.** M(NaCl) = 23.0 + 35.5 = 58.5 g mol⁻¹, so n = 2.34 ÷ 58.5 = 0.0400 mol [1]; each formula unit gives 2 ions (Na⁺ and Cl⁻), so n(ions) = 0.0800 mol [1]; N = 0.0800 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 4.82 × 10²² ions [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-30 -->

Explanation:

**A358.** M(Na₂CO₃·10H₂O) = 106.0 + 10(18.0) = 286.0 g mol⁻¹, so n = 2.86 ÷ 286.0 = 0.0100 mol [1]; n(H₂O) = 10 × 0.0100 = 0.100 mol [1]; m(H₂O) = 0.100 × 18.0 = 1.80 g [1]; n(H) = 2 × 0.100 = 0.200 mol, so N = 0.200 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.20 × 10²³ atoms [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-31 -->

Explanation:

#### Percentage composition and empirical formulas

**A359.** B. 35.0%
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-1 -->

Explanation: M(NH₄NO₃) = 2(14.0) + 4(1.0) + 3(16.0) = 80.0 g mol⁻¹; %N = 28.0 ÷ 80.0 × 100 = 35.0%. 17.5% counts only one of the two nitrogen atoms.

**A360.** D. CH₂O
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-2 -->

Explanation: In 100 g: C 40.0 ÷ 12.0 = 3.33 mol, H 6.7 ÷ 1.0 = 6.7 mol, O 53.3 ÷ 16.0 = 3.33 mol. Dividing by 3.33 gives 1 : 2 : 1, so CH₂O. CHO would ignore the 2 : 1 ratio of H to C.

**A361.** C. C₆H₁₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-3 -->

Explanation: M(CH₂) = 14.0 g mol⁻¹ and 84.0 ÷ 14.0 = 6, so the molecular formula is (CH₂)₆ = C₆H₁₂. C₆H₁₄ has the right number of C atoms but its empirical formula is C₃H₇, not CH₂.

**A362.** A. Ethyne, C₂H₂, and benzene, C₆H₆
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-4 -->

Explanation: Both C₂H₂ and C₆H₆ reduce to CH. Propane (C₃H₈) and butane (empirical formula C₂H₅) have different simplest ratios even though they are in the same homologous series.

**A363.** C. Some magnesium remained unreacted at the end of heating
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-5 -->

Explanation: Unreacted magnesium means less oxygen combined, so the mass gain (and the calculated amount of O) is too low, giving a ratio below 1 : 1. Absorbing water would increase the final mass and make the O value too high, not too low.

**A364.** In 100 g: n(C) = 85.6 ÷ 12.0 = 7.13 mol and n(H) = 14.4 ÷ 1.0 = 14.4 mol; ratio 7.13 : 14.4 = 1 : 2.02 ≈ 1 : 2, so the empirical formula is CH₂ (M = 14.0 g mol⁻¹); 56.0 ÷ 14.0 = 4, so the molecular formula is C₄H₈.
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A365.** m(H₂O) lost = 2.50 − 1.60 = 0.90 g, so n(H₂O) = 0.90 ÷ 18.0 = 0.050 mol; M(CuSO₄) = 63.5 + 32.1 + 64.0 = 159.6 g mol⁻¹, so n(CuSO₄) = 1.60 ÷ 159.6 = 0.0100 mol; ratio H₂O : CuSO₄ = 0.050 : 0.0100 = 5, so x = 5 (CuSO₄·5H₂O).
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A366.** M(Fe₂O₃) = 2(55.8) + 3(16.0) = 159.6 g mol⁻¹; %Fe = 111.6 ÷ 159.6 × 100 = 69.9%; m(Fe₂O₃) = 0.800 × 500 = 400 kg, so m(Fe) = 0.699 × 400 = 280 kg (2.80 × 10² kg).
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A367.** B. 40.1%
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-9 -->

Explanation: M(CaCO₃) = 40.1 + 12.0 + 3(16.0) = 100.1 g mol⁻¹; %Ca = 40.1 ÷ 100.1 × 100 = 40.1%. 12.0% is the percentage of carbon, not calcium.

**A368.** D. Na₂SO₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-10 -->

Explanation: In 100 g: Na 32.4 ÷ 23.0 = 1.41 mol, S 22.6 ÷ 32.1 = 0.704 mol, O 45.0 ÷ 16.0 = 2.81 mol; dividing by 0.704 gives 2 : 1 : 4, so Na₂SO₄. NaSO₄ results from forgetting to scale the sodium value.

**A369.** A. N₂O₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-11 -->

Explanation: M(NO₂) = 46.0 g mol⁻¹ and 92.0 ÷ 46.0 = 2, so the molecular formula is (NO₂)₂ = N₂O₄. NO₂ is only the empirical formula and has a molar mass of 46.0 g mol⁻¹.

**A370.** A. 23.3 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-12 -->

Explanation: M(urea) = 12.0 + 16.0 + 2(14.0) + 4(1.0) = 60.0 g mol⁻¹; m(N) = 28.0 ÷ 60.0 × 50.0 = 23.3 g. 11.7 g counts only one of the two nitrogen atoms.

**A371.** B. 51.1%
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-13 -->

Explanation: M(MgSO₄·7H₂O) = 120.4 + 126.0 = 246.4 g mol⁻¹; %H₂O = 126.0 ÷ 246.4 × 100 = 51.1%. 104.7% comes from dividing by the mass of the anhydrous salt only, instead of the whole hydrate.

**A372.** C. Al₂O₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-14 -->

Explanation: n(Al) = 5.40 ÷ 27.0 = 0.200 mol and n(O) = 4.80 ÷ 16.0 = 0.300 mol; the ratio 0.200 : 0.300 = 2 : 3 gives Al₂O₃. AlO would come from comparing the masses rather than the amounts.

**A373.** D. It shows the simplest whole-number ratio of ions in the lattice
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-15 -->

Explanation: An ionic lattice has no discrete molecules, so its formula gives only the simplest ratio of ions (1 : 1). A molecule of NaCl does not exist, so the first option is incorrect.

**A374.** A. CH₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-16 -->

Explanation: n(C) = 2.40 ÷ 12.0 = 0.200 mol and n(H) = 0.600 ÷ 1.0 = 0.600 mol, a ratio of 1 : 3, so CH₃. C₂H₆ has the same ratio but is not the simplest whole-number ratio.

**A375.** B. The molar mass of the compound
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-17 -->

Explanation: Dividing the molar mass by the empirical formula mass gives the whole-number multiple needed. Physical properties such as melting point or colour cannot show how many empirical units make up a molecule.

**A376.** C. SO₂ has the higher percentage of sulfur: 50.0% compared with 40.1%
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-18 -->

Explanation: %S in SO₂ = 32.1 ÷ 64.1 × 100 = 50.0%, while %S in SO₃ = 32.1 ÷ 80.1 × 100 = 40.1%. The extra oxygen in SO₃ increases the total mass and so lowers the sulfur percentage.

**A377.** D. 19.1 kg
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-19 -->

Explanation: M((NH₄)₂SO₄) = 132.1 g mol⁻¹, so %N = 28.0 ÷ 132.1 = 21.2% and m(N) = 0.212 × 90.0 = 19.1 kg. 21.2 kg ignores that only 90.0% of the fertiliser is ammonium sulfate.

**A378.** A. x will be too low because the measured mass of water lost is too small
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-20 -->

Explanation: Incomplete heating means the mass loss, and so the calculated amount of water, is too small, giving a value of x that is too low. The ratio is not unchanged because some water is still counted as part of the residue.

**A379.** B. Fe₂O₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-21 -->

Explanation: n(Fe) = 70.0 ÷ 55.8 = 1.25 mol and n(O) = 30.0 ÷ 16.0 = 1.88 mol; the ratio 1 : 1.5 becomes 2 : 3, so Fe₂O₃. Fe₃O₄ would give 1 : 1.33, and FeO would give 1 : 1.

**A380.** C. C₄H₈O₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-22 -->

Explanation: Ratios of 4.54 : 9.1 : 2.28 mol give an empirical formula of C₂H₄O (44.0 g mol⁻¹); 88.0 ÷ 44.0 = 2, so the molecular formula is C₄H₈O₂. C₂H₄O is only the empirical formula.

**A381.** M(Al₂(SO₄)₃) = 2(27.0) + 3(32.1) + 12(16.0) = 342.3 g mol⁻¹ [1]; %O = 192.0 ÷ 342.3 × 100 = 56.1% [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-23 -->

Explanation:

**A382.** n(Pb) = 2.07 ÷ 207 = 0.0100 mol [1]; n(O) = 0.320 ÷ 16.0 = 0.0200 mol [1]; ratio Pb : O = 1 : 2, so the empirical formula is PbO₂ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-24 -->

Explanation:

**A383.** n(C) = n(CO₂) = 2.20 ÷ 44.0 = 0.0500 mol, so m(C) = 0.600 g [1]; n(H) = 2 × 1.35 ÷ 18.0 = 0.150 mol, so m(H) = 0.150 g [1]; m(O) = 1.15 − 0.600 − 0.150 = 0.400 g, so n(O) = 0.400 ÷ 16.0 = 0.0250 mol [1]; ratio C : H : O = 0.0500 : 0.150 : 0.0250 = 2 : 6 : 1, so the empirical formula is C₂H₆O [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-25 -->

Explanation:

**A384.** In 100 g: m(H₂O) = 62.9 g and m(Na₂CO₃) = 37.1 g; n(H₂O) = 62.9 ÷ 18.0 = 3.49 mol [1]; M(Na₂CO₃) = 106.0 g mol⁻¹, so n(Na₂CO₃) = 37.1 ÷ 106.0 = 0.350 mol [1]; ratio H₂O : Na₂CO₃ = 3.49 ÷ 0.350 ≈ 10, so x = 10 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-26 -->

Explanation:

**A385.** M(C₃H₄O₃) = 3(12.0) + 4(1.0) + 3(16.0) = 88.0 g mol⁻¹ [1]; 176 ÷ 88.0 = 2, so the molecular formula is C₆H₈O₆ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A386.** (a) Cleaning removes the layer of magnesium oxide already on the ribbon, so the initial mass is that of magnesium only and the mass of oxygen gained is not underestimated [1]; (b) constant mass shows that the reaction is complete and no more oxygen is being gained, so the mass of oxygen combined is accurate [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A387.** %Fe = 1.40 ÷ 2.20 × 100 = 63.6% [1]; m(S) = 2.20 − 1.40 = 0.80 g, so n(Fe) = 1.40 ÷ 55.8 = 0.0251 mol and n(S) = 0.80 ÷ 32.1 = 0.0249 mol [1]; ratio Fe : S = 1 : 1, so the empirical formula is FeS [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A388.** In 100 g: n(C) = 49.0 ÷ 12.0 = 4.08 mol, n(H) = 2.7 ÷ 1.0 = 2.7 mol, n(Cl) = 48.3 ÷ 35.5 = 1.36 mol [1]; dividing by 1.36 gives C : H : Cl = 3 : 2 : 1, so the empirical formula is C₃H₂Cl [1]; M(C₃H₂Cl) = 36.0 + 2.0 + 35.5 = 73.5 g mol⁻¹ [1]; 147 ÷ 73.5 = 2, so the molecular formula is C₆H₄Cl₂ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Hydrocarbons and IUPAC naming

**A389.** B. 2-methylbutane
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-1 -->

Explanation: The longest chain has 4 carbons (butane) with a methyl branch; numbering from the end nearest the branch gives the lowest locant, 2. 3-methylbutane numbers from the wrong end.

**A390.** A. C₅H₁₀
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-2 -->

Explanation: Alkenes with one C=C bond have the general formula CₙH₂ₙ, which C₅H₁₀ fits. C₅H₁₂ and C₆H₁₄ fit CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ (alkanes), and C₅H₈ fits CₙH₂ₙ₋₂ (alkynes).

**A391.** A. 2
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-3 -->

Explanation: Only butane (unbranched) and 2-methylpropane are possible. Drawing a 'bent' four-carbon chain does not create a new isomer, which is a common source of over-counting.

**A392.** B. pent-2-ene
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-4 -->

Explanation: The chain has 5 carbons with a C=C between C2 and C3 when numbered to give the double bond the lowest locant, so it is pent-2-ene. Pent-3-ene numbers from the wrong end; '-yne' indicates a triple bond.

**A393.** D. the increasing strength of dispersion forces as the molecules become larger
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-5 -->

Explanation: Larger molecules have more electrons and greater surface contact, giving stronger dispersion forces that need more energy to overcome. Boiling does not break covalent bonds, and alkanes cannot hydrogen bond.

**A394.** (a) pentane; (b) but-1-yne; (c) 2-methylpropane (methylpropane is also accepted).
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A395.** Both are non-polar, so the only intermolecular forces are dispersion forces, and both have the same number of electrons; pentane is a linear (unbranched) molecule, so its molecules have a larger surface area of contact and can approach more closely; this gives stronger dispersion forces in pentane, needing more energy to separate the molecules, hence a higher boiling point than the compact, spherical 2,2-dimethylpropane.
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A396.** (a) C₃H₈(g) + 5O₂(g) → 3CO₂(g) + 4H₂O(l); (b) the chain should be numbered from the end that gives the branch the lowest locant; the correct name is 2-methylbutane.
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A397.** C. C₇H₁₆
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-9 -->

Explanation: Alkanes follow CₙH₂ₙ₊₂, so n = 7 gives C₇H₁₆. C₇H₁₄ is the formula of an alkene (CₙH₂ₙ).

**A398.** B. 3-methylpentane
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-10 -->

Explanation: The longest chain has 5 carbons (pentane) with a methyl branch on carbon 3. Naming it 2-ethylbutane picks a chain that is not the longest.

**A399.** A. C₃H₈
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-11 -->

Explanation: C₃H₈ fits CₙH₂ₙ₊₂, so it contains only C–C single bonds and is saturated. C₂H₄ and C₂H₂ contain C=C and C≡C bonds respectively, and C₄H₆ is also unsaturated.

**A400.** C. pent-2-yne
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-12 -->

Explanation: The chain has 5 carbons and a triple bond between C2 and C3 when numbered from the nearest end, giving pent-2-yne. Pent-3-yne numbers from the wrong end.

**A401.** D. C(CH₃)₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-13 -->

Explanation: Propane has 3 carbons, and two methyl groups on carbon 2 give a central carbon bonded to four CH₃ groups, C(CH₃)₄. The first option is pentane and the second is 2-methylbutane.

**A402.** B. 6
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-14 -->

Explanation: But-2-yne is CH₃C≡CCH₃, C₄H₆, fitting CₙH₂ₙ₋₂ for alkynes. Eight hydrogens would be butene and ten would be butane.

**A403.** A. but-1-ene and 2-methylpropene
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-15 -->

Explanation: Both but-1-ene and 2-methylpropene are C₄H₈ with different arrangements of atoms. Butane (C₄H₁₀) and but-1-ene (C₄H₈) have different molecular formulas, so they are not isomers.

**A404.** D. 2,3-dimethylbutane
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-16 -->

Explanation: The longest chain is butane, with methyl groups on carbons 2 and 3. 2-methylpentane would have only one branch and a 5-carbon chain.

**A405.** B. structural isomers
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-17 -->

Explanation: Both have the formula C₆H₁₂ but different structures (a ring versus a chain with C=C), so they are structural isomers. They are in different homologous series, so they cannot be homologues.

**A406.** B. Only dispersion forces act between the molecules.
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-18 -->

Explanation: Hexane is non-polar, so only weak dispersion forces act between its molecules. Hydrogen bonding requires H bonded to N, O or F, which hexane does not have.

**A407.** D. hexane
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-19 -->

Explanation: Hexane has a boiling point of about 69 °C and so is a liquid at 25 °C. Ethane, propane and butane have boiling points below 25 °C and are gases.

**A408.** A. CO and H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-20 -->

Explanation: With limited oxygen, carbon monoxide (or carbon) forms along with water. Carbon dioxide and water only are the products of complete combustion.

**A409.** B. 2-methylbut-1-ene
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-21 -->

Explanation: The longest chain containing C=C has 4 carbons, with the double bond at C1 and a methyl on C2. 3-methylbut-1-ene places the branch on the wrong carbon, and a double bond at C3 does not give the lowest locant.

**A410.** Saturated hydrocarbons contain only single C–C bonds, e.g. propane [1]; unsaturated hydrocarbons contain at least one C=C or C≡C bond, e.g. ethene [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-22 -->

Explanation:

**A411.** Alkanes CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ [1]; alkenes CₙH₂ₙ [1]; alkynes CₙH₂ₙ₋₂ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-23 -->

Explanation:

**A412.** (a) but-1-ene [1]; (b) 2-methylpentane [1]; (c) but-2-yne [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-24 -->

Explanation:

**A413.** (a) CH₃C(CH₃)=CHCH₃ [1]; (b) HC≡CCH(CH₃)CH₂CH₃ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A414.** Hexane is non-polar and has only weak dispersion forces between its molecules [1]; water molecules are held by strong hydrogen bonds [1]; the forces that hexane could form with water are much weaker than the hydrogen bonds that would be broken, so hexane does not dissolve [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A415.** n(C₃H₈) = 11.0 ÷ 44.0 = 0.250 mol [1]; n(CO₂) = 3 × 0.250 = 0.750 mol [1]; m(CO₂) = 0.750 × 44.0 = 33.0 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A416.** Add bromine water (a few drops, shaking) to a sample of each [1]; but-1-ene decolourises the orange-brown bromine water quickly because it is unsaturated [1]; butane causes no colour change in the dark because it is saturated [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-28 -->

Explanation:

#### Functional groups and homologous series

**A417.** C. Carboxyl group, –COOH
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-1 -->

Explanation: Carboxylic acids contain the carboxyl group, –COOH (a C=O and an –OH on the same carbon). Although an –OH is present, the compound is not classified as an alcohol.

**A418.** A. propan-1-ol
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-2 -->

Explanation: It is a three-carbon alcohol with the –OH on carbon 1, so propan-1-ol. Propan-2-ol would have the –OH on the middle carbon, CH₃CH(OH)CH₃.

**A419.** D. Successive members differ by a –CH₂– unit and have similar chemical properties
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-3 -->

Explanation: A homologous series shares a general formula and functional group, with successive members differing by CH₂; their physical properties (e.g. boiling point) change gradually. Isomers have the same molecular formula, which homologues do not.

**A420.** B. CH₃CH₂Cl
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-4 -->

Explanation: Chloroethane, CH₃CH₂Cl, has a halogen atom replacing a hydrogen in an alkane. CH₃OH is an alcohol and CH₃COOH is a carboxylic acid.

**A421.** C. Propan-1-ol, C₃H₇OH (60.0 g mol⁻¹)
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-5 -->

Explanation: Propan-1-ol molecules can form hydrogen bonds through their –OH group, which are much stronger than the dipole–dipole and dispersion forces in chloroethane or the dispersion forces in butane. Chloroethane has the highest molar mass but no hydrogen bonding.

**A422.** Ethanol has a polar –OH group with H bonded to highly electronegative O; it can form hydrogen bonds with water molecules, which release enough energy to compensate for breaking hydrogen bonds between water molecules, so ethanol mixes with water; ethane is non-polar with only dispersion forces, so it cannot form hydrogen bonds with water and would disrupt the water hydrogen-bond network without compensating attractions, so it does not dissolve.
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A423.** Fifth member has n = 5: pentan-1-ol, C₅H₁₁OH (C₅H₁₂O); M = 5(12.0) + 12(1.0) + 16.0 = 88.0 g mol⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A424.** (a) carboxyl group, propanoic acid; (b) bromo (halo) group, 2-bromopropane; (c) carbon–carbon double bond, propene (prop-1-ene).
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A425.** B. Ester group, –COO–
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-9 -->

Explanation: Methyl ethanoate has a C=O bonded to an O that is itself bonded to a carbon chain, which is the ester linkage, –COO–. A carboxyl group would have a hydrogen on that oxygen (–COOH).

**A426.** A. C₅H₁₀
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-10 -->

Explanation: Alkenes with one C=C have the general formula CₙH₂ₙ, so five carbons gives C₅H₁₀. C₅H₁₂ fits an alkane (CₙH₂ₙ₊₂), and C₅H₈ has too few hydrogens for one double bond.

**A427.** C. Amino group, –NH₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-11 -->

Explanation: Amines contain the amino group, –NH₂, bonded to a carbon chain. An amide would also have a C=O attached to the nitrogen, which ethylamine does not have.

**A428.** A. CH₃OH and CH₃CH₂OH
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-12 -->

Explanation: Methanol and ethanol are both alkanols and differ by one –CH₂– unit. Ethanol and ethanoic acid have the same number of carbons but different functional groups, so they are in different series.

**A429.** D. Dispersion forces increase as the molecules have more electrons
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-13 -->

Explanation: Alkanes are non-polar, so the only intermolecular forces are dispersion forces, which strengthen as molecular size and electron number increase. Covalent bonds are not broken when a molecular substance boils.

**A430.** B. 74.0 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-14 -->

Explanation: Propanoic acid is C₃H₆O₂, so M = 3(12.0) + 6(1.0) + 2(16.0) = 74.0 g mol⁻¹. 60.0 g mol⁻¹ is the value for ethanoic acid, which has one fewer carbon.

**A431.** A. butanoic acid
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-15 -->

Explanation: The chain has four carbons including the carboxyl carbon, so the name is butanoic acid. Propanoic acid has three carbons, and butan-1-ol is an alcohol with an –OH group instead.

**A432.** B. 2-methylpropan-1-ol
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-16 -->

Explanation: The longest chain containing the carbon bonded to –OH has three carbons, with the –OH on carbon 1 and a methyl branch on carbon 2. Butan-1-ol has an unbranched four-carbon chain, so it is a different isomer.

**A433.** C. C₆H₁₂O₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-17 -->

Explanation: Hexanoic acid is CH₃(CH₂)₄COOH, which has six carbons, twelve hydrogens and two oxygens. C₅H₁₀O₂ is pentanoic acid, which has one fewer carbon.

**A434.** D. 1,2-dibromoethane
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-18 -->

Explanation: There is one bromine on each of the two carbons, so the locants are 1 and 2 and the prefix is di-. 1,1-dibromoethane would have both bromines on the same carbon.

**A435.** B. Amino and carboxyl
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-19 -->

Explanation: The molecule has an –NH₂ amino group and a –COOH carboxyl group. It has no C=O bonded to the nitrogen, so it does not contain an amide group.

**A436.** CH₃CH₂CH₂Cl is 1-chloropropane [1]; CH₃CHClCH₃ is 2-chloropropane [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-20 -->

Explanation:

**A437.** General formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₂, so 2n + 2 = 22 and n = 10, giving C₁₀H₂₂ [1]; the name is decane [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-21 -->

Explanation:

**A438.** Alkanes contain only C–C single bonds whereas alkenes contain a C=C double bond [1]; hex-1-ene decolourises the orange/brown bromine water [1]; hexane does not react so the bromine water stays orange/brown [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-22 -->

Explanation:

**A439.** Both are non-polar, so the only intermolecular forces are dispersion forces [1]; butane has more electrons (larger molecules), so stronger dispersion forces that require more energy to overcome [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-23 -->

Explanation:

**A440.** Hydroxyl group, –OH [1]; carboxyl group, –COOH [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-24 -->

Explanation:

**A441.** Molecular formula is C₄H₉Cl [1]; M = 4(12.0) + 9(1.0) + 35.5 = 92.5 g mol⁻¹ [1]; %Cl = 35.5 ÷ 92.5 × 100 = 38.4% [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-25 -->

Explanation:

**A442.** Ester group, –COO– [1]; carboxylic acid is propanoic acid [1]; alkanol is methanol [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-26 -->

Explanation:

**A443.** The –OH group forms hydrogen bonds with water, so methanol, with a small non-polar part, mixes with water [1]; decan-1-ol has a long non-polar hydrocarbon chain that forms only dispersion forces with water [1]; the energy released by hydrogen bonding is not enough to disrupt the hydrogen bonds between water molecules, so decan-1-ol is insoluble [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-27 -->

Explanation:

#### Addition polymers

**A444.** A. A carbon–carbon double bond
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-1 -->

Explanation: In addition polymerisation one bond of the C=C breaks, allowing monomers to join into a chain with no other product. A halogen atom alone is not enough; chloroethene polymerises because of its C=C, not its Cl.

**A445.** D. –CH₂–CHCl–
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-2 -->

Explanation: The C=C opens and each monomer contributes a –CH₂–CHCl– unit with only single bonds in the chain. –CH=CCl– wrongly retains a double bond and has lost hydrogen.

**A446.** B. propene
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-3 -->

Explanation: Restoring the C=C between the two chain carbons gives CH₂=CHCH₃, propene. Propane has no double bond, so it cannot undergo addition polymerisation.

**A447.** C. has little chain branching, so chains pack closely and have stronger dispersion forces between them
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-4 -->

Explanation: Both are made from ethene; HDPE chains are largely unbranched, so they align closely in crystalline regions, increasing density and the strength of dispersion forces. Polyethene has no cross-links and no hydrogen bonding.

**A448.** B. 1.00 × 10⁴
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-5 -->

Explanation: M(C₂H₄) = 28.0 g mol⁻¹, and 2.80 × 10⁵ ÷ 28.0 = 1.00 × 10⁴. Because no atoms are lost in addition polymerisation, the chain mass is simply the number of units × 28.0.

**A449.** Monomer is tetrafluoroethene, CF₂=CF₂; repeating unit is –CF₂–CF₂–; the C=C double bond of each monomer breaks and monomers join end to end with no other product (no small molecule such as water is lost), so it is addition polymerisation.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-6 -->

Explanation:

**A450.** Longer chains have more electrons and greater contact area between chains, so the dispersion forces between chains are stronger in total; more energy is needed for the chains to slide past each other, so the polymer softens at a higher temperature.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-7 -->

Explanation:

**A451.** n CH₂=CH(C₆H₅) → –[CH₂–CH(C₆H₅)]ₙ–; the repeating unit shows the double bond opened and a benzene ring (phenyl group) on every second carbon; e.g. it is rigid/brittle at room temperature because the bulky phenyl groups restrict chain movement (or it is a thermoplastic that softens on heating because there are no cross-links).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-8 -->

Explanation:

**A452.** A. The polymer is the only product; no small molecule is released
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-9 -->

Explanation: The C=C of each monomer opens and the monomers join directly, so all atoms end up in the polymer. Release of water is characteristic of condensation polymerisation, not addition polymerisation.

**A453.** B. Hexane
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-10 -->

Explanation: Hexane is a saturated alkane with only single bonds, so there is no C=C to open. The others each contain a C=C, even though they carry halogen atoms or a methyl group.

**A454.** C. Chloroethene
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-11 -->

Explanation: PVC is made from CH₂=CHCl, which is chloroethene (one chlorine on an ethene skeleton). Chloroethane is saturated and cannot polymerise by addition.

**A455.** D. 14.0 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-12 -->

Explanation: No atoms are lost in addition polymerisation, so mass is conserved and the polymer has the same mass as the monomer used. Doubling to 28.0 g would wrongly use the molar mass instead of the sample mass.

**A456.** B. produce a reactive radical that attacks the C=C and starts the chain
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-13 -->

Explanation: The initiator breaks down to form radicals, and a radical reacts with the C=C of a monomer to begin chain growth. It does not become a cross-linking agent or supply extra atoms to the chain.

**A457.** D. the weak dispersion forces between chains are overcome while the covalent bonds within chains stay intact
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-14 -->

Explanation: Heating supplies enough energy to overcome the intermolecular forces so chains can move past each other. The strong C–C covalent bonds along the chain are not broken, and there are no C=C bonds left in the polymer.

**A458.** A. –CH₂–CH(CN)–
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-15 -->

Explanation: The C=C opens, leaving a –CH₂–CH(CN)– unit with the CN group as a side group on every second carbon. A repeating unit containing C=C is wrong because the polymer chain is fully saturated.

**A459.** B. 1000
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-16 -->

Explanation: Each ethene unit contributes two carbon atoms to the chain, so 500 × 2 = 1000. Counting one carbon per unit confuses the monomer with the number of carbon atoms it supplies.

**A460.** C. n CH₂=CH₂ → –[CH₂–CH₂]ₙ–
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-17 -->

Explanation: All atoms of the monomer end up in the saturated polymer, so there is no second product. Equations that release H₂ wrongly treat the reaction as one that loses small molecules.

**A461.** D. polar C–Cl bonds, giving dipole–dipole forces in addition to dispersion forces between chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-18 -->

Explanation: The C–Cl bonds are polar, adding dipole–dipole attractions to the dispersion forces and so making the chains harder to separate. Cl bonded to carbon cannot form hydrogen bonds, and PVC chains contain no C=C bonds.

**A462.** A. The polyethene does not decolourise the bromine, but ethene does
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-19 -->

Explanation: Ethene has a C=C that reacts with bromine, decolourising it. In polyethene the C=C bonds were consumed during polymerisation, so the chain is saturated and does not react.

**A463.** B. –CH₂–CH(CH₂CH₃)–
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-20 -->

Explanation: Only the two carbons of the C=C form the chain backbone, and the remaining ethyl group becomes a side group. Putting all four carbons in the backbone ignores that the side group stays attached.

**A464.** C. its strong, non-polar saturated C–C and C–H backbone is not readily broken down by microorganisms
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-21 -->

Explanation: The saturated non-polar backbone of strong covalent bonds resists chemical attack and microbial breakdown. Polyethene does not contain C=C bonds, so this cannot be the cause.

**A465.** A monomer is a small molecule that can join with many others to form a polymer [1]. A polymer is a very large molecule made of many repeating units joined together [1]. Addition polymerisation requires a carbon–carbon double bond (C=C) in the monomer [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-22 -->

Explanation:

**A466.** n CH₂=CH(CH₃) → –[CH₂–CH(CH₃)]ₙ– [1] with the double bond opened and a methyl group on every second carbon of the chain, with no other product formed [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-23 -->

Explanation:

**A467.** Repeating unit is –CH₂–CHCl– (same formula as chloroethene, C₂H₃Cl) [1]. M(C₂H₃Cl) = 2 × 12.0 + 3 × 1.0 + 35.5 = 62.5 g mol⁻¹ [1]. M(polymer) = 2500 × 62.5 = 1.56 × 10⁵ g mol⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-24 -->

Explanation:

**A468.** (a) No atoms are lost in addition polymerisation, so mass of polyethene = 5.60 kg [1]. (b) n(C₂H₄) = 5600 ÷ 28.0 = 200 mol [1]; each chain uses 4.00 × 10³ monomer units, so n(chains) = 200 ÷ 4.00 × 10³ [1] = 0.0500 mol [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-25 -->

Explanation:

#### Thermoplastics, thermosets and the circular economy

**A469.** D. Chains are held together only by intermolecular forces, so it softens on heating and can be remoulded
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-1 -->

Explanation: Heating a thermoplastic overcomes the relatively weak intermolecular forces between chains, letting them slide and be reshaped. The cross-linked description in option A is a thermoset.

**A470.** B. It has extensive covalent cross-links between polymer chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-2 -->

Explanation: Covalent cross-links lock thermoset chains in place, so heating cannot make them flow; strong heating eventually breaks covalent bonds and the material decomposes. Raw material source does not define thermosets.

**A471.** C. Designing products so their materials can be reused, repaired or recycled and kept in use for as long as possible
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-3 -->

Explanation: A circular economy aims to design out waste and keep materials in use. The other options follow the linear 'take–make–dispose' model or simply move waste elsewhere.

**A472.** A. Polylactic acid (PLA)
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-4 -->

Explanation: PLA is made from lactic acid obtained by fermenting plant sugars, and is compostable under industrial conditions. PVC, PTFE and polystyrene are normally made from fossil fuel-derived monomers.

**A473.** D. Its covalent cross-links prevent the chains from flowing, and heating strongly enough to break them decomposes the polymer
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-5 -->

Explanation: Melting requires chains to move past each other, which cross-links prevent; the material chars instead. Thermosets are carbon-based and do not dissolve in water.

**A474.** In a linear economy, fossil fuels are extracted to make PET, bottles are used once and then sent to landfill or become litter (take–make–dispose); in a circular economy, bottles are designed for recovery, collected (e.g. through container deposit schemes) and mechanically or chemically recycled into new bottles; this reduces demand for new raw materials, energy use and waste, keeping the material in use for longer.
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A475.** The resin chains are joined by covalent cross-links forming a rigid three-dimensional network; heating gives the chains energy, but the strong covalent cross-links prevent them from sliding past one another, so the material does not soften or melt; only at very high temperatures do covalent bonds break, causing decomposition (charring) rather than melting.
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A476.** Bio-based means the carbon comes from a renewable biological feedstock (sugarcane); however, bio-PE is chemically identical to fossil-derived polyethene, so microorganisms cannot readily break it down and it is not biodegradable; an advantage is that it reduces use of finite fossil fuels, and the CO₂ absorbed by the sugarcane during growth can offset some of the emissions (and it can be recycled with ordinary PE).
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A477.** B. Polyethene
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-9 -->

Explanation: Polyethene consists of separate chains held together by dispersion forces, so it softens on heating and can be remoulded. Bakelite, vulcanised rubber and melamine–formaldehyde are cross-linked thermosets.

**A478.** C. Forms covalent sulfur cross-links between the polymer chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-10 -->

Explanation: Sulfur atoms form covalent bridges between neighbouring chains, making the rubber stronger and more elastic. The chains are joined to each other rather than broken apart or simply lengthened.

**A479.** A. It can be shredded, melted and reshaped because there are no covalent cross-links between its chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-11 -->

Explanation: Only weak intermolecular forces hold the chains together, so heating lets the chains flow and be remoulded. HDPE does not depolymerise or dissolve in water on gentle heating.

**A480.** D. It becomes harder and more rigid and is less able to soften on heating
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-12 -->

Explanation: More covalent cross-links restrict movement of the chains, giving a harder, more rigid material that resists softening. Greater flexibility and easier softening are features of fewer cross-links.

**A481.** B. Reducing consumption so that less waste is created
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-13 -->

Explanation: Avoiding and reducing waste at the source is the top priority because no material or energy is needed to process it. Recycling is better than disposal but ranks below reduction and reuse.

**A482.** A. Breaking the polymer down into its monomers or small molecules that are used to make new polymer
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-14 -->

Explanation: Chemical recycling breaks bonds in the polymer (for example by hydrolysis) to recover monomers for repolymerisation. Melting and remoulding is mechanical recycling, and burning is energy recovery.

**A483.** D. It can be broken down by microorganisms into substances such as carbon dioxide, water and biomass
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-15 -->

Explanation: Biodegradability refers to breakdown by microorganisms, and depends on the polymer's structure and conditions, not its raw material. Some bio-based polymers are not biodegradable, and some biodegradable polymers come from fossil fuels.

**A484.** B. It is a thermoplastic with no extensive covalent cross-links between chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-16 -->

Explanation: Repeated softening and hardening shows the chains can move past each other when heated, so they are not covalently cross-linked. A cross-linked thermoset would char rather than soften.

**A485.** A. A thermoset, because its cross-links stop it softening
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-17 -->

Explanation: A thermoset keeps its shape at high temperature because covalent cross-links hold the chains in place. A low-melting thermoplastic would soften and deform.

**A486.** C. It encourages return of used containers, providing a collected, relatively clean stream of material for recycling
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-18 -->

Explanation: A refundable deposit gives people an incentive to return containers, so they are collected and recycled rather than littered or landfilled. Burning is lower on the waste hierarchy than recycling.

**A487.** D. 72%
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-19 -->

Explanation: Recovery = 180 ÷ 250 × 100 = 72%. The 28% figure is the proportion lost, and 139% results from dividing the wrong way round.

**A488.** A. Different polymers have different melting points and mix poorly, so a mixture gives a weak, inconsistent product
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-20 -->

Explanation: Different polymers melt at different temperatures and are often immiscible, so blends have poor properties. Plastics differ in density, and sorting does not change their chemical structure.

**A489.** B. Dispersion forces
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-21 -->

Explanation: Polyethene chains are non-polar hydrocarbons, so only weak dispersion forces act between them. Covalent cross-links are found in thermosets, and polyethene has no O–H or N–H groups for hydrogen bonding.

**A490.** D. It destroys the material, so new raw materials are needed, and releases carbon dioxide from the carbon in the plastic
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-22 -->

Explanation: Combustion recovers some energy but the material is lost and CO₂ is released, whereas recycling keeps material in use. Plastics do release energy when burned, so that is not the drawback.

**A491.** Polyethene is a thermoplastic whose chains are held together only by weak dispersion forces [1]; heating overcomes these forces so the chains can slide and the material can be melted and remoulded [1]; vulcanised rubber has covalent sulfur cross-links between chains, which hold the chains in place, so it cannot melt and decomposes when heated strongly [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-23 -->

Explanation:

**A492.** Mechanical recycling involves sorting, cleaning, shredding and melting the PET so that it can be reshaped into new products without changing the polymer chemically [1]; chemical recycling breaks the polymer into monomers or small molecules (e.g. by hydrolysis) which are purified and repolymerised [1]; chemical recycling can give material equal in quality to new PET, whereas mechanical recycling often gives a lower-quality product because chains shorten and contaminants build up [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-24 -->

Explanation:

**A493.** Order: reducing consumption, then recycling, then landfill [1]; reducing consumption is best because less material and energy is used and no waste is created [1]; recycling keeps materials in use and saves raw materials, whereas landfill loses the material permanently and uses land [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-25 -->

Explanation:

**A494.** Mass of PET in waste = 0.65 × 12.0 [1] = 7.80 t [1]; mass recovered = 0.70 × 7.80 = 5.46 t [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-26 -->

Explanation:

**A495.** Make the tubs from a thermoplastic such as polypropene or PET instead [1]; a thermoplastic can be melted and remoulded because it has no covalent cross-links, so it can be mechanically recycled [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-27 -->

Explanation:

**A496.** Landfill is cool and has little oxygen and moisture, so conditions are unsuitable for the microorganisms that degrade PLA, which needs the high temperatures and moisture of industrial composting [1]; composting allows PLA to break down into carbon dioxide, water and biomass, which can be returned to soil rather than being buried [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-28 -->

Explanation:

**A497.** Downcycling is recycling a material into a product of lower quality or value than the original [1]; during melting and reprocessing, polymer chains can shorten and impurities or mixed polymers contaminate the plastic [1]; this reduces strength and clarity, so the material is used for less demanding products such as park benches rather than new food-grade bottles [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-29 -->

Explanation:

**A498.** Benefit: the refund encourages people to return bottles, increasing collection rates and reducing litter and landfill [1]; benefit: returned bottles form a clean, single-type stream that is easier to recycle into food-grade material, reducing demand for new fossil-fuel-based PET [1]; limitation: it covers only certain containers and relies on consumer participation, and collection and transport cost energy and money [1]; overall it keeps materials in use for longer but should be combined with reduction and reuse strategies [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-30 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 1: Chemicals and water

#### Properties of water and hydrogen bonding

**A499.** C. extensive hydrogen bonding between molecules absorbs energy that would otherwise raise the kinetic energy of the molecules
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-1 -->

Explanation: Much of the energy supplied is used to disrupt hydrogen bonds, so a larger energy input is needed to raise the temperature. Heating water does not break covalent O–H bonds, and water is polar.

**A500.** A. 62.7 kJ
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-2 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 250 × 4.18 × 60.0 = 6.27 × 10⁴ J = 62.7 kJ. 20.9 kJ uses the initial temperature (20.0 °C) instead of ΔT; 6.27 × 10⁴ kJ forgets to convert J to kJ.

**A501.** D. hydrogen bonding holds the molecules in an open hexagonal lattice, so ice is less dense than liquid water
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-3 -->

Explanation: In ice each molecule hydrogen-bonds to four neighbours in a regular, open arrangement with more space between molecules than in the liquid. The molar mass of H₂O is the same in both states.

**A502.** B. H₂O, because its molecules form hydrogen bonds
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-4 -->

Explanation: Water boils at 100 °C, far higher than the others, because O is highly electronegative and small, allowing strong hydrogen bonds. H₂Te has the strongest dispersion forces of the group but these are weaker than water's hydrogen bonding.

**A503.** C. 20.4 kJ
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-5 -->

Explanation: n(H₂O) = 9.00 ÷ 18.0 = 0.500 mol; energy = 0.500 × 40.7 = 20.4 kJ. 366 kJ results from multiplying by the mass in grams rather than the amount in mol.

**A504.** Water has a high specific heat capacity; this is because energy is used to disrupt the extensive hydrogen bonding between water molecules, so a large amount of energy is needed to change its temperature; large bodies of water such as Port Phillip Bay therefore absorb heat during the day and release it at night with only small temperature changes, moderating the air temperature of nearby land.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-6 -->

Explanation:

**A505.** A hydrogen bond is an attraction between a hydrogen atom bonded to a highly electronegative atom (O) in one molecule and a lone pair on an O atom of a neighbouring molecule; the large electronegativity difference makes H strongly δ+ and O strongly δ−; each water molecule has two H atoms (each can bond to another molecule) and two lone pairs on O (each can accept a hydrogen bond), giving up to four hydrogen bonds.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-7 -->

Explanation:

**A506.** ΔT = q ÷ (mc) = 15 000 ÷ (200 × 4.18) = 17.9 °C; final temperature = 18.0 + 17.9 = 35.9 °C.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-8 -->

Explanation:

**A507.** C. NH₃ and H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-9 -->

Explanation: NH₃ has an H atom bonded to N and a lone pair on N, and H₂O has H bonded to O and lone pairs on O, so hydrogen bonds can form between them. HCl is tempting because it is polar, but its H is bonded to Cl, not to N, O or F, and CH₄ and CH₃Cl have no H bonded to N, O or F either.

**A508.** A. two bonding pairs and two lone pairs of electrons, which repel to give a bent shape
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-10 -->

Explanation: Four electron pairs around O (two bonding, two lone) repel to a tetrahedral arrangement, but the shape of the molecule is bent because only two of the pairs are bonded. With no lone pairs the molecule would be linear, but O in water has two.

**A509.** B. hydrogen bonds pulling surface molecules inwards towards neighbouring molecules
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-11 -->

Explanation: Molecules at the surface have no neighbours above them, so they are pulled inwards and sideways by hydrogen bonds, creating a tight 'skin'. The covalent bonds are within molecules and are not what holds the surface together.

**A510.** D. Hydrogen bonds between water molecules are broken, but each molecule remains intact
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-12 -->

Explanation: Boiling is a physical change in which the intermolecular hydrogen bonds are overcome so molecules can separate as steam. The covalent O–H bonds are much stronger and stay intact, so no hydrogen or oxygen gas forms.

**A511.** A. 15.7 kJ
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-13 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 150 × 4.18 × 25.0 = 15 675 J = 15.7 kJ. 0.627 kJ results from leaving out the temperature change, and 3.75 kJ from leaving out the specific heat capacity.

**A512.** C. 120 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-14 -->

Explanation: m = q ÷ (cΔT) = 5000 ÷ (4.18 × 10.0) = 120 g. 0.120 g comes from using 5.00 J rather than converting kJ to J, and 2.09 × 10⁵ g from multiplying by c instead of dividing.

**A513.** B. evaporating sweat absorbs a large amount of energy from the skin to overcome hydrogen bonds
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-15 -->

Explanation: Water has a high heat of vaporisation because many hydrogen bonds must be broken for it to evaporate, so evaporation removes a lot of energy from the body. Water's specific heat capacity is high, not low, and breaking bonds absorbs rather than releases energy.

**A514.** D. water molecules form hydrogen bonds, whereas methane has only weak dispersion forces
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-16 -->

Explanation: Water's molecules are held by strong hydrogen bonds, whereas non-polar methane has only weak dispersion forces, so far less energy is needed to separate methane molecules. The molar masses are similar, so dispersion forces cannot explain the 260 °C difference.

**A515.** A. 44.2 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-17 -->

Explanation: n = 100 ÷ 40.7 = 2.46 mol; m = 2.46 × 18.0 = 44.2 g. 2.46 g stops at the amount in mol without converting to mass.

**A516.** C. the δ+ hydrogen of one molecule and a lone pair on the oxygen of a neighbouring molecule
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-18 -->

Explanation: The hydrogen bond is an attraction between a δ+ H on one molecule and a lone pair on the δ− O of another. The attraction between H and O within a molecule is the covalent bond, not a hydrogen bond.

**A517.** B. ethanol, because its molecules form hydrogen bonds
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-19 -->

Explanation: Ethanol has an H atom bonded to O, so its molecules form hydrogen bonds, which are stronger than the dipole–dipole forces in dimethyl ether or the dispersion forces in propane. Similar molar mass only means similar dispersion forces, not similar total intermolecular forces.

**A518.** A. The ethanol temperature rises by more, because it has a lower specific heat capacity
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-20 -->

Explanation: ΔT = q ÷ (mc), so for the same q and m the substance with the lower c has the larger temperature rise. Water's high c, caused by hydrogen bonding, means it needs more energy per degree, so it warms less.

**A519.** C. 109 mL
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-21 -->

Explanation: Mass is conserved: m = 100 g, so V(ice) = 100 ÷ 0.92 = 109 mL. 92.0 mL multiplies by the density instead of dividing, which would wrongly suggest the water contracts on freezing.

**A520.** B. Hydrogen bonds are weaker than the covalent bonds within molecules but stronger than dispersion forces between similar-sized non-polar molecules
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-22 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen bonds are an intermolecular force, much weaker than covalent bonds, which is why boiling does not decompose water, yet they are stronger than the dispersion forces in non-polar substances of similar size. Dispersion forces in small non-polar molecules are weaker than hydrogen bonds.

**A521.** Water molecules at the surface are attracted inwards and sideways by hydrogen bonds to neighbouring molecules, producing high surface tension [1]; this forms a skin that can support the small mass of the insect without the surface breaking [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-23 -->

Explanation:

**A522.** Nitrogen is small and highly electronegative, so the N–H bonds are very polar and NH₃ molecules form hydrogen bonds between them [1]; phosphorus is larger and less electronegative, so PH₃ cannot form hydrogen bonds and is held only by weaker dispersion forces and weak dipole–dipole attractions [1]; hydrogen bonds are stronger, so more energy is needed to separate NH₃ molecules, giving ammonia the higher boiling point despite its smaller molar mass [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-24 -->

Explanation:

**A523.** Heating: q = mcΔT = 500 × 4.18 × 85.0 = 1.78 × 10⁵ J [1]; = 178 kJ [1]; n(H₂O) = 500 ÷ 18.0 = 27.8 mol, vaporisation energy = 27.8 × 40.7 = 1.13 × 10³ kJ [1]; total = 178 + 1130 = 1.31 × 10³ kJ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-25 -->

Explanation:

**A524.** q = mcΔT = 1500 × 4.18 × 78.0 [1] = 4.89 × 10⁵ J [1]; time = 4.89 × 10⁵ ÷ 2000 = 245 s (about 4.1 minutes) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-26 -->

Explanation:

**A525.** In ice, hydrogen bonds hold the molecules in a rigid, open lattice with more space between molecules than in liquid water [1]; so ice has a lower density than liquid water and floats at the surface [1]; the floating layer of ice insulates the water below, so it remains liquid and aquatic life can survive [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-27 -->

Explanation:

**A526.** Oxygen is much more electronegative than hydrogen, so each O–H bond is polar with O partially negative (δ−) and H partially positive (δ+) [1]; the molecule is bent (about 104.5°) because of the two lone pairs on oxygen [1]; because of the bent shape, the bond dipoles do not cancel, so the molecule has a net dipole [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-28 -->

Explanation:

**A527.** Covalent O–H bonds are strong bonds within a molecule formed by sharing of electrons, whereas hydrogen bonds are much weaker intermolecular attractions between δ+ H and a lone pair on O of a neighbouring molecule [1]; when water evaporates only the hydrogen bonds between molecules are broken, and the covalent bonds remain intact [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-29 -->

Explanation:

**A528.** Energy lost by the hot water equals energy gained by the cold water [1]; 100 × 4.18 × (80.0 − T) = 200 × 4.18 × (T − 20.0) [1]; 8000 − 100T = 200T − 4000, so T = 40.0 °C [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Solubility and precipitation reactions

**A529.** B. AgCl
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-1 -->

Explanation: Silver chloride is a classic insoluble chloride. All sodium, potassium, ammonium and nitrate compounds are soluble.

**A530.** C. Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-2 -->

Explanation: The ionic equation shows only the ions that form the precipitate, omitting spectator ions. Option B is the full (molecular) equation, not the ionic equation; NaCl and BaCl₂ are soluble.

**A531.** A. Na⁺ and NO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-3 -->

Explanation: Ag⁺ and Cl⁻ combine to form AgCl(s); Na⁺ and NO₃⁻ remain in solution unchanged. Option D wrongly includes Cl⁻, which is consumed in the precipitate.

**A532.** D. 78 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-4 -->

Explanation: At 60 °C 110 g is dissolved; at 20 °C only 32 g can stay dissolved, so 110 − 32 = 78 g crystallises. 32 g is the amount that stays in solution.

**A533.** B. Pb(NO₃)₂(aq) and KI(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-5 -->

Explanation: Pb²⁺ and I⁻ form insoluble yellow PbI₂(s). In the other pairs every possible combination of ions (e.g. NaNO₃, KCl, KNO₃) is soluble.

**A534.** Water molecules are polar; the δ− oxygen ends are attracted to Na⁺ ions and the δ+ hydrogen ends to Cl⁻ ions at the surface of the lattice (ion–dipole attractions); these attractions are strong enough to overcome the ionic bonding, so ions are pulled from the lattice and become surrounded by water molecules (hydrated), dispersing through the solution as Na⁺(aq) and Cl⁻(aq).
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A535.** Cu²⁺(aq) + 2OH⁻(aq) → Cu(OH)₂(s); a pale blue precipitate forms (and the blue colour of the solution fades); the spectator ions are Na⁺ and SO₄²⁻.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A536.** The ion is Ba²⁺; barium sulfate is insoluble, so Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s) explains the white precipitate; if Ag⁺ were present, adding chloride would have formed a white precipitate of AgCl, but none formed (BaCl₂ is soluble).
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-8 -->

Explanation:

**A537.** A. PbCl₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-9 -->

Explanation: Pb²⁺ and Cl⁻ combine in a 1 : 2 ratio to form insoluble PbCl₂. NaNO₃ is soluble, so it stays in solution and is not the precipitate.

**A538.** C. Fe³⁺(aq) + 3OH⁻(aq) → Fe(OH)₃(s)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-10 -->

Explanation: The ionic equation shows only the ions that form the precipitate, balanced for charge and atoms. The first option is the full equation, and the last is wrong because NaNO₃ is soluble.

**A539.** D. KOH(aq) and NaNO₃(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-11 -->

Explanation: The possible products, KNO₃ and NaOH, are both soluble, so no reaction occurs. The other pairs form AgBr, ZnS and Ca₃(PO₄)₂ respectively, which are all insoluble.

**A540.** A. 1.17 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-12 -->

Explanation: n(BaCl₂) = 0.0500 × 0.100 = 0.00500 mol, and Ba²⁺ : BaSO₄ is 1 : 1, so m = 0.00500 × 233.4 = 1.17 g. Using 0.0100 mol (2.33 g) wrongly doubles the amount.

**A541.** D. Na₂SO₄(s) → 2Na⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-13 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit releases two Na⁺ ions and one SO₄²⁻ ion, and the equation must balance in atoms and charge. The second option is not balanced, and the third gives too many sulfate ions.

**A542.** C. It contains the maximum amount of solute that can dissolve at that temperature, so any extra solute remains undissolved.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-14 -->

Explanation: A saturated solution cannot dissolve any more solute at that temperature. It need not be highly concentrated, because a sparingly soluble salt can form a saturated solution that is very dilute.

**A543.** B. A saturated solution forms with about 12 g of solid remaining undissolved.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-15 -->

Explanation: Only 88 g can dissolve at this temperature, so 100 − 88 = 12 g remains as solid in contact with the saturated solution. The 88 g figure is the amount that dissolves, not the amount left over.

**A544.** C. CO₃²⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-16 -->

Explanation: Magnesium, calcium and barium carbonates are all insoluble. Chloride and nitrate salts of these ions are soluble, and MgSO₄ is soluble so sulfate would not remove Mg²⁺.

**A545.** B. 2Ag⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → Ag₂CO₃(s)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-17 -->

Explanation: Two Ag⁺ ions are needed to balance the charge of one CO₃²⁻ ion, giving Ag₂CO₃. The first option has unbalanced charges, and sodium salts are soluble so they do not precipitate.

**A546.** A. The ionic bonds in the lattice are stronger than the ion–dipole attractions that water molecules could form with the ions.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-18 -->

Explanation: A compound dissolves only if hydration of its ions releases enough energy to overcome the lattice attractions. Water is polar, so the second option is wrong, and CaCO₃ is ionic.

**A547.** D. 3Mg²⁺(aq) + 2PO₄³⁻(aq) → Mg₃(PO₄)₂(s)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-19 -->

Explanation: Three Mg²⁺ (+6) balance two PO₄³⁻ (−6), giving Mg₃(PO₄)₂. The other options have unbalanced charges, usually from swapping or ignoring the ion charges.

**A548.** A. Ba(OH)₂(aq) and CuSO₄(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-20 -->

Explanation: Barium hydroxide with copper(II) sulfate gives both BaSO₄ and Cu(OH)₂, which are insoluble. NaOH with CuSO₄ gives only Cu(OH)₂, and BaCl₂ with Na₂SO₄ gives only BaSO₄.

**A549.** B. The δ− oxygen ends point towards the Na⁺ ion.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-21 -->

Explanation: The positive Na⁺ ion is attracted to the partially negative oxygen atoms of water. The δ+ hydrogen ends point towards anions such as Cl⁻.

**A550.** Ca²⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → CaCO₃(s) [1] A white precipitate forms [1] The spectator ions are Na⁺ and Cl⁻ [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-22 -->

Explanation:

**A551.** n(AgNO₃) = 0.0250 × 0.200 = 0.00500 mol [1] Ag⁺ : AgCl = 1 : 1, so n(AgCl) = 0.00500 mol [1] m(AgCl) = 0.00500 × 143.4 = 0.717 g [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-23 -->

Explanation:

**A552.** Add barium nitrate solution to a sample of each solution [1] The one that forms a white precipitate is sodium sulfate, because Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s) [1] Add silver nitrate solution to samples of the other two solutions [1] The one that forms a white precipitate (AgCl) is sodium chloride and the one with no precipitate is sodium nitrate [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-24 -->

Explanation:

**A553.** For an ionic compound to dissolve, the energy released by ion–dipole attractions between the ions and water molecules must be enough to overcome the attractions between ions in the lattice [1] In AgCl the attractions between the Ag⁺ and Cl⁻ ions in the lattice are stronger than the ion–dipole attractions with water, so it stays solid, whereas for NaCl the ion–dipole attractions are sufficient so it dissolves [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-25 -->

Explanation:

**A554.** Mass dissolved at 60 °C = 46 × 2.5 = 115 g [1] Mass that can stay dissolved at 20 °C = 34 × 2.5 = 85 g [1] Mass crystallising = 115 − 85 = 30 g [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-26 -->

Explanation:

**A555.** Add sodium carbonate solution so that carbonate ions react with lead(II) ions: Pb²⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → PbCO₃(s) [1] Lead(II) carbonate is insoluble so the lead is removed from solution as a precipitate, while the Na⁺ ions remain in solution as spectator ions [1] The precipitate is separated from the water by filtration [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-27 -->

Explanation:

**A556.** Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s) [1] Ba²⁺ and SO₄²⁻ are each 0.00500 mol, so they react completely with none in excess [1] n(Na⁺) = 2 × 0.00500 = 0.0100 mol and n(Cl⁻) = 2 × 0.00500 = 0.0100 mol, in a total volume of 0.100 L [1] [Na⁺] = [Cl⁻] = 0.0100 ÷ 0.100 = 0.100 mol L⁻¹ [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-28 -->

Explanation:

**A557.** Al(NO₃)₃(aq) + 3KOH(aq) → Al(OH)₃(s) + 3KNO₃(aq) [1] Ionic form: Al³⁺(aq) + 3NO₃⁻(aq) + 3K⁺(aq) + 3OH⁻(aq) → Al(OH)₃(s) + 3K⁺(aq) + 3NO₃⁻(aq) [1] Cancelling spectator ions K⁺ and NO₃⁻ gives Al³⁺(aq) + 3OH⁻(aq) → Al(OH)₃(s) [1]
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Brønsted-Lowry acids and bases

**A558.** A. proton donor
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-1 -->

Explanation: A Brønsted–Lowry acid donates H⁺ (a proton) and a base accepts it. Producing OH⁻ in water is the older Arrhenius description of a base.

**A559.** C. HPO₄²⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-2 -->

Explanation: A conjugate base has one fewer H⁺ than its acid: H₂PO₄⁻ − H⁺ = HPO₄²⁻. H₃PO₄ is its conjugate acid.

**A560.** B. HCO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-3 -->

Explanation: HCO₃⁻ can donate a proton (forming CO₃²⁻) or accept one (forming H₂CO₃). CO₃²⁻ has no H to donate, and Cl⁻ is an extremely weak base that does not act as an acid.

**A561.** D. an acid, because it donates a proton to NH₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-4 -->

Explanation: H₂O loses H⁺ to become OH⁻, so it is the acid; NH₃ is the base. OH⁻ is the conjugate base of water, not the other way round.

**A562.** C. H₂SO₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-5 -->

Explanation: Sulfuric acid can donate two protons per molecule. Ethanoic acid has four H atoms but only the one in –COOH is ionisable, so it is monoprotic; H₃PO₄ is triprotic.

**A563.** (a) HCO₃⁻(aq) + OH⁻(aq) → CO₃²⁻(aq) + H₂O(l); (b) HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq) → H₂CO₃(aq) + H₂O(l) (or → CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l)); an amphiprotic species can act as either a proton donor (acid) or a proton acceptor (base) depending on what it reacts with.
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A564.** CH₃COOH / CH₃COO⁻ (acid / conjugate base); H₃O⁺ / H₂O (conjugate acid / base).
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A565.** (a) MgCO₃(s) + 2HCl(aq) → MgCl₂(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g); (b) Zn(s) + 2HCl(aq) → ZnCl₂(aq) + H₂(g); in both, bubbles of gas (effervescence) are seen and the solid dissolves.
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-8 -->

Explanation:

**A566.** A. NH₄⁺
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-9 -->

Explanation: NH₃ accepts a proton to form NH₄⁺, its conjugate acid. NH₂⁻ would be the conjugate base, formed by losing a proton.

**A567.** B. OH⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-10 -->

Explanation: Removing one H⁺ from H₂O gives OH⁻. H₃O⁺ is the conjugate acid, formed by gaining a proton.

**A568.** C. HCO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-11 -->

Explanation: The conjugate acid has one more H⁺ than the base: CO₃²⁻ + H⁺ gives HCO₃⁻. H₂CO₃ would require two protons to be added.

**A569.** D. HCl
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-12 -->

Explanation: HCl donates one proton per molecule. H₂CO₃ and H₂S are diprotic and H₃PO₄ is triprotic.

**A570.** A. H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-13 -->

Explanation: Water can donate H⁺ (forming OH⁻) or accept H⁺ (forming H₃O⁺), so it is amphiprotic. NO₃⁻ and SO₄²⁻ have no H to donate.

**A571.** B. HSO₄⁻ and SO₄²⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-14 -->

Explanation: HSO₄⁻ and SO₄²⁻ differ by one proton. HSO₄⁻ and H₃O⁺ are both acids, so they cannot be a conjugate pair.

**A572.** C. H₃O⁺
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-15 -->

Explanation: A hydronium ion is a water molecule that has gained a proton, H₂O + H⁺ = H₃O⁺. OH⁻ is the hydroxide ion formed when water loses a proton.

**A573.** D. Ca(NO₃)₂, H₂O and CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-16 -->

Explanation: Acid plus carbonate gives a salt, water and carbon dioxide. Hydrogen gas forms only when an acid reacts with a reactive metal.

**A574.** B. CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-17 -->

Explanation: Carbon dioxide reacts with limewater to form insoluble calcium carbonate, which makes it cloudy. Hydrogen would instead burn with a squeaky pop.

**A575.** C. ionises almost completely in water
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-18 -->

Explanation: Strength refers to the extent of ionisation, not concentration. A dilute solution of a strong acid is still a strong acid.

**A576.** D. a very weak base
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-19 -->

Explanation: A strong acid donates protons so readily that its conjugate base has almost no tendency to accept one back. It is not a strong base.

**A577.** A. HS⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-20 -->

Explanation: H₂S loses one proton to form HS⁻. S²⁻ would need two protons to be lost, and H₃O⁺ is the conjugate acid of water.

**A578.** B. an acid, because it donates a proton
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-21 -->

Explanation: H₂PO₄⁻ loses H⁺ to OH⁻, so in this reaction it is the acid. It is amphiprotic in general, but here it only donates.

**A579.** C. 0.0250 mol
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-22 -->

Explanation: n(CaCO₃) = 2.50 ÷ 100.1 = 0.0250 mol, and the equation gives a 1:1 ratio to CO₂. The 0.0500 mol option wrongly uses a 1:2 ratio, which applies to HCl.

**A580.** A Brønsted–Lowry base is a proton (H⁺) acceptor [1]. CO₃²⁻(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ HCO₃⁻(aq) + OH⁻(aq) [1]. The conjugate acid formed is HCO₃⁻ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-23 -->

Explanation:

**A581.** HNO₃(aq) + H₂O(l) → H₃O⁺(aq) + NO₃⁻(aq) [1]. It is a strong acid because it ionises (donates protons to water) essentially completely, so the equation has a single arrow [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-24 -->

Explanation:

**A582.** CH₃COOH(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ CH₃COO⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq) [1]. Only a small fraction of molecules ionise, so the reaction does not go to completion and an equilibrium with mostly unionised CH₃COOH is established [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-25 -->

Explanation:

**A583.** (a) H₂SO₄(aq) + 2NaOH(aq) → Na₂SO₄(aq) + 2H₂O(l) [1]; (b) H₂SO₄(aq) + CuO(s) → CuSO₄(aq) + H₂O(l) [1]; the black solid dissolves and the solution turns blue [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-26 -->

Explanation:

**A584.** 2CH₃COOH(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → 2CH₃COO⁻(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g) [1]; CH₃COOH is the proton donor (acid) and CO₃²⁻ is the proton acceptor (base) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-27 -->

Explanation:

**A585.** H₂SO₄(aq) + H₂O(l) → HSO₄⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq) [1]; HSO₄⁻(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ SO₄²⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-28 -->

Explanation:

**A586.** HCl(aq) + NaOH(aq) → NaCl(aq) + H₂O(l) [1]; n(NaOH) = 0.100 × 0.0185 = 1.85 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; n(HCl) = 1.85 × 10⁻³ mol (1:1 ratio) [1]; c(HCl) = 1.85 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.0250 = 0.0740 M [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-29 -->

Explanation:

**A587.** 2H₂O(l) ⇌ H₃O⁺(aq) + OH⁻(aq) [1]; one water molecule donates a proton (acts as an acid) while the other accepts it (acts as a base) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-30 -->

Explanation:

**A588.** HCl is a strong acid and ionises completely, giving a high concentration of ions [1]; ethanoic acid is weak and only partly ionises, so fewer mobile ions are present to carry charge [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-31 -->

Explanation:

#### pH and strong vs weak acids

**A589.** B. 2.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-1 -->
