Explanation: HCl is a strong acid and ionises completely, so [H₃O⁺] = 0.010 mol L⁻¹ and pH = −log₁₀(0.010) = 2.0. 12.0 would be the pH of 0.010 mol L⁻¹ NaOH.

**A590.** D. 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-2 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ = 10⁻⁴·⁰ = 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹. 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁰ mol L⁻¹ is the hydroxide ion concentration at 25 °C.

**A591.** A. The HCl solution has the lower pH because HCl ionises completely in water, whereas CH₃COOH only partially ionises
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-3 -->

Explanation: Strength refers to the extent of ionisation: HCl is strong, so [H₃O⁺] ≈ 0.10 mol L⁻¹, while ethanoic acid is weak and produces a much smaller [H₃O⁺]. Equal concentrations do not mean equal pH.

**A592.** C. 3.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-4 -->

Explanation: The solution is diluted 100-fold, so [H₃O⁺] falls from 0.10 to 1.0 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹ and pH = 3.0. Each tenfold dilution of a strong acid raises the pH by 1, not by 10.

**A593.** D. 12.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-5 -->

Explanation: Each Ba(OH)₂ releases 2 OH⁻, so [OH⁻] = 0.010 mol L⁻¹; [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 0.010 = 1.0 × 10⁻¹² mol L⁻¹, pH = 12.0. 11.7 ignores the factor of 2.

**A594.** Strength describes the extent to which an acid ionises (donates protons) in water: a strong acid such as HCl ionises completely, while a weak acid such as CH₃COOH ionises only partially; concentration describes the amount of acid dissolved per litre of solution (mol L⁻¹); so an acid can be strong but dilute (e.g. 0.001 mol L⁻¹ HCl) or weak but concentrated (e.g. 10 mol L⁻¹ CH₃COOH).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-6 -->

Explanation:

**A595.** n(HCl) = 0.100 × 0.0200 = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol and n(NaOH) = 0.100 × 0.0150 = 1.50 × 10⁻³ mol, so H₃O⁺ is in excess by 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol; total volume = 35.0 mL, so [H₃O⁺] = 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ ÷ 0.0350 = 1.43 × 10⁻² mol L⁻¹; pH = −log₁₀(1.43 × 10⁻²) = 1.85.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-7 -->

Explanation:

**A596.** [OH⁻] = 0.025 mol L⁻¹, so [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 0.025 = 4.0 × 10⁻¹³ mol L⁻¹; pH = −log₁₀(4.0 × 10⁻¹³) = 12.40 (12.4).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-8 -->

Explanation:

**A597.** C. An acid that ionises completely (donates its protons almost entirely) in water
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-9 -->

Explanation: A strong acid ionises essentially completely in water, regardless of how concentrated it is. A high concentration describes the amount dissolved, not the strength.

**A598.** A. 3.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-10 -->

Explanation: HNO₃ is a strong monoprotic acid, so [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹ and pH = 3.0. 11.0 would be the pH of a 0.0010 mol L⁻¹ strong base such as NaOH.

**A599.** B. 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-11 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ mol L⁻¹, so [OH⁻] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ = 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ mol L⁻¹. 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ mol L⁻¹ is the hydronium concentration, not the hydroxide concentration.

**A600.** D. CH₃COOH
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-12 -->

Explanation: Ethanoic acid ionises only partially in water, so it is a weak acid. HNO₃, HCl and HBr are all strong acids that ionise completely.

**A601.** B. It decreases by a factor of 100
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-13 -->

Explanation: Each pH unit is a tenfold change in [H₃O⁺], so a rise of 2 units means a 10² = 100-fold decrease. A change of 2 in pH is not a halving or a change of 2 mol L⁻¹.

**A602.** A. HCl has the higher conductivity because it forms more ions
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-14 -->

Explanation: Complete ionisation of HCl gives a higher concentration of mobile ions than the partial ionisation of HF, so conductivity is higher. Equal concentrations of acid do not mean equal concentrations of ions.

**A603.** D. 11.60
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-15 -->

Explanation: Each Ca(OH)₂ releases 2 OH⁻, so [OH⁻] = 0.0040 mol L⁻¹, pOH = 2.40 and pH = 11.60. 11.30 results from forgetting the factor of 2.

**A604.** C. 1000 times greater
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-16 -->

Explanation: The pH differs by 3 units, and each unit is a factor of 10, so [H₃O⁺] in X is 10³ = 1000 times greater. The pH scale is logarithmic, so a difference of 3 units is not a factor of 3.

**A605.** B. HF + H₂O ⇌ H₃O⁺ + F⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-17 -->

Explanation: A weak acid donates a proton to water in a reversible reaction, shown with an equilibrium arrow. A single forward arrow would imply complete ionisation, as for a strong acid.

**A606.** A. 1.30
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-18 -->

Explanation: The volume doubles, so [H₃O⁺] = 0.050 mol L⁻¹ and pH = −log₁₀(0.050) = 1.30. A pH of 1.00 would be the undiluted solution.

**A607.** D. Both need the same volume of NaOH
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-19 -->

Explanation: Both acids are monoprotic and contain the same amount in moles, and the weak acid's ionisation shifts to replace H₃O⁺ as it is consumed. Strength affects the pH, not the amount of base needed.

**A608.** C. The water is neutral because [H₃O⁺] = [OH⁻]
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-20 -->

Explanation: Neutrality means [H₃O⁺] = [OH⁻]; at higher temperature Kw increases, so the neutral pH falls below 7. A pH of 7 is neutral only at 25 °C.

**A609.** A. NH₃ has a lower pH because it only partially ionises, giving a lower [OH⁻]
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-21 -->

Explanation: NaOH dissociates completely, whereas NH₃ reacts only partially with water, so [OH⁻] and pH are lower for NH₃. NH₃ is still basic, with pH above 7.

**A610.** C. HA
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-22 -->

Explanation: A weak acid is only slightly ionised, so most of the acid remains as HA molecules while H₃O⁺ and A⁻ are present in small and equal amounts. H₃O⁺ would be highest only for a strong acid.

**A611.** A strong acid is one that ionises (donates protons to water) completely in aqueous solution [1]; HNO₃ + H₂O → H₃O⁺ + NO₃⁻ (single forward arrow) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-23 -->

Explanation:

**A612.** n(HCl) = 0.50 × 0.0100 = 5.0 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; c = 5.0 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.250 = 0.020 mol L⁻¹, so [H₃O⁺] = 0.020 mol L⁻¹ as HCl is strong [1]; pH = −log₁₀(0.020) = 1.70 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-24 -->

Explanation:

**A613.** [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ = 10⁻³·⁴⁰ [1]; = 4.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-25 -->

Explanation:

**A614.** CH₃COOH + H₂O ⇌ CH₃COO⁻ + H₃O⁺ [1]; the reversible arrow shows ionisation is incomplete, with the equilibrium lying well to the left [1]; so most ethanoic acid remains as molecules and [H₃O⁺] is much lower than the acid concentration [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-26 -->

Explanation:

**A615.** n(NaOH) = 2.50 × 10⁻³ mol and n(HCl) = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; OH⁻ is in excess by 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol [1]; total volume = 45.0 mL, so [OH⁻] = 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ ÷ 0.0450 = 1.11 × 10⁻² mol L⁻¹ [1]; [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 1.11 × 10⁻² = 9.0 × 10⁻¹³ mol L⁻¹, pH = 12.05 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-27 -->

Explanation:

**A616.** [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻²·⁷⁸ = 1.66 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹ [1]; fraction ionised = 1.66 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.15 = 0.0111 [1]; so about 1.1% ionised, confirming it is a weak acid [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-28 -->

Explanation:

**A617.** n(HNO₃) = 1.26 ÷ 63.0 = 0.0200 mol [1]; c = 0.0200 ÷ 0.250 = 0.0800 mol L⁻¹, so [H₃O⁺] = 0.0800 mol L⁻¹ as HNO₃ is strong [1]; pH = −log₁₀(0.0800) = 1.10 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Redox reactions and half-equations

**A618.** B. Cu²⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-1 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ gains electrons (is reduced) to form Cu, so it is the oxidising agent. Zn(s) loses electrons and is the reducing agent, not the oxidising agent.

**A619.** D. +7
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-2 -->

Explanation: Each O is −2, giving −8 in total. The ion's charge is −1, so Mn + (−8) = −1 and Mn = +7. +2 is the oxidation number of Mn in Mn²⁺, the usual reduction product.

**A620.** C. Cr₂O₇²⁻(aq) + 14H⁺(aq) + 6e⁻ → 2Cr³⁺(aq) + 7H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-3 -->

Explanation: Balance Cr (2), O with 7 H₂O, H with 14 H⁺, then charge: left side 14 − 2 = +12, right side +6, so 6 e⁻ are added to the left. Electrons on the product side would make it an oxidation, and 8 e⁻ leaves the charge unbalanced.

**A621.** B. AgNO₃(aq) + NaCl(aq) → AgCl(s) + NaNO₃(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-4 -->

Explanation: The precipitation of AgCl involves no change in oxidation numbers (Ag stays +1, Cl stays −1), so it is not redox. In the other reactions, elements change oxidation number, e.g. Fe goes from +3 to 0.

**A622.** D. is oxidised, from +4 to +6
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-5 -->

Explanation: In SO₂, S = +4; in SO₄²⁻, S + 4(−2) = −2 so S = +6. An increase in oxidation number is oxidation. An increase in oxidation number can never be reduction.

**A623.** Oxidation: Al(s) → Al³⁺(aq) + 3e⁻; Reduction: Cu²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → Cu(s); Overall (electrons balanced at 6): 2Al(s) + 3Cu²⁺(aq) → 2Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cu(s).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A624.** Reduction: MnO₄⁻(aq) + 8H⁺(aq) + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺(aq) + 4H₂O(l); Oxidation: Fe²⁺(aq) → Fe³⁺(aq) + e⁻; Multiply the oxidation by 5 so electrons cancel; Overall: MnO₄⁻(aq) + 8H⁺(aq) + 5Fe²⁺(aq) → Mn²⁺(aq) + 5Fe³⁺(aq) + 4H₂O(l).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A625.** Cl in Cl₂ has oxidation number 0; in Cl⁻ it is −1, so some chlorine is reduced; in OCl⁻ it is +1, so some chlorine is oxidised; the same species is both oxidised and reduced (disproportionation), and H, O and Na oxidation numbers do not change.
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A626.** B. loss of electrons
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-9 -->

Explanation: Oxidation is the loss of electrons and is accompanied by an increase in oxidation number. Gain of electrons is reduction, which is why it is a tempting but incorrect choice.

**A627.** A. −3
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-10 -->

Explanation: Each H is +1, giving +4 in total. The ion's charge is +1, so N + 4 = +1 and N = −3. +3 results from forgetting that the overall charge is +1 rather than 0 and adding the signs the wrong way.

**A628.** D. −1
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-11 -->

Explanation: Each H is +1, so the two O atoms must total −2, giving −1 each. −2 is the usual oxidation number of oxygen but peroxides are an exception.

**A629.** C. Mg(s)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-12 -->

Explanation: Mg loses electrons (0 to +2), so it is oxidised and is the reducing agent. O₂ gains electrons and is the oxidising agent, which is the common mix-up.

**A630.** C. H₂O₂(aq) + 2H⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → 2H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-13 -->

Explanation: Two H⁺ are needed to balance hydrogen (4 each side) and two electrons to balance charge (0 on both sides). Placing the electrons on the product side would describe oxidation, and omitting the H⁺ leaves H and charge unbalanced.

**A631.** B. 2
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-14 -->

Explanation: Each Cl atom goes from 0 to −1, gaining one electron, so a Cl₂ molecule gains two. Answering 1 considers only a single atom rather than the whole molecule.

**A632.** A. MnO₄⁻ → Mn²⁺
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-15 -->

Explanation: Mn goes from +7 to +2, a decrease in oxidation number, so this is reduction. The other three each involve an increase in oxidation number (Fe +2 to +3, S +4 to +6, Cl −1 to 0) and are oxidations.

**A633.** D. 2I⁻(aq) → I₂(aq) + 2e⁻
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-16 -->

Explanation: Two iodide ions each lose one electron, so two electrons appear on the product side with I balanced. I⁻ → I₂ + e⁻ is unbalanced in iodine atoms, and electrons on the reactant side would describe reduction.

**A634.** B. Carbon is oxidised from −4 to +4
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-17 -->

Explanation: Carbon is −4 in CH₄ and +4 in CO₂, an increase, so it is oxidised. Oxygen goes from 0 to −2, which is reduction, and hydrogen stays at +1.

**A635.** A. Zn(s) + 2Ag⁺(aq) → Zn²⁺(aq) + 2Ag(s)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-18 -->

Explanation: Zn loses 2 electrons and each Ag⁺ gains only 1, so two Ag⁺ are needed. Equations with Zn + Ag⁺ in a 1:1 ratio are unbalanced in charge.

**A636.** C. SO₃²⁻(aq) + H₂O(l) → SO₄²⁻(aq) + 2H⁺(aq) + 2e⁻
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-19 -->

Explanation: One H₂O supplies the extra oxygen, 2H⁺ balance hydrogen, and charge requires 2e⁻ on the product side (−2 on both sides). Putting the electrons on the left describes reduction, and a single electron leaves charge unbalanced.

**A637.** D. H⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-20 -->

Explanation: H in H⁺ goes from +1 to 0, a decrease, so H⁺ is reduced. Fe is oxidised (0 to +2); H₂ is the product of reduction, not the species reduced.

**A638.** B. +5
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-21 -->

Explanation: Three O atoms contribute −6 and the ion's charge is −1, so Cl = −1 + 6 = +5. +7 is the value in perchlorate, ClO₄⁻.

**A639.** A. The solution turns blue and a silver-coloured solid forms
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-22 -->

Explanation: Cu is oxidised to blue Cu²⁺(aq) while Ag⁺ is reduced to solid silver: Cu + 2Ag⁺ → Cu²⁺ + 2Ag. A brown solid would be copper forming, which is the reverse reaction.

**A640.** C. 3
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-23 -->

Explanation: Left side charge is −1 + 4 = +3 and the right side is 0, so 3 electrons are required. This matches N changing from +5 to +2.

**A641.** H₂S: S = −2 as each H is +1 [1]. S₈: S = 0 as it is an uncombined element [1]. SO₂: S = +4 as each O is −2 [1]. SO₄²⁻: S = +6 since S + 4(−2) = −2 [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-24 -->

Explanation:

**A642.** Oxidation: SO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l) → SO₄²⁻(aq) + 4H⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ [1]. Reduction: MnO₄⁻(aq) + 8H⁺(aq) + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺(aq) + 4H₂O(l) [1]. Multiply oxidation by 5 and reduction by 2 so 10 electrons cancel [1]. Overall: 5SO₂(g) + 2MnO₄⁻(aq) + 2H₂O(l) → 5SO₄²⁻(aq) + 2Mn²⁺(aq) + 4H⁺(aq) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-25 -->

Explanation:

**A643.** Oxidation: Mg(s) → Mg²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ [1]. Reduction: 2H⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → H₂(g) [1]. Overall: Mg(s) + 2H⁺(aq) → Mg²⁺(aq) + H₂(g) [1]. Mg is the species oxidised (0 to +2) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-26 -->

Explanation:

**A644.** Fe changes from +3 to 0, so it is reduced [1]. Al changes from 0 to +3, so it is oxidised [1]. Oxidising agent is Fe₂O₃ [1]. Reducing agent is Al [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A645.** In the zinc reaction Zn changes from 0 to +2 and H changes from +1 to 0, so oxidation numbers change and it is redox [1]. In the NaOH and HCl reaction every element keeps its oxidation number (Na +1, O −2, H +1, Cl −1), so it is not redox [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A646.** n(Mg) = 0.486 ÷ 24.3 = 0.0200 mol [1]. The mole ratio Mg : Cu is 1 : 1, so n(Cu) = 0.0200 mol [1]. m(Cu) = 0.0200 × 63.5 = 1.27 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A647.** Electrons lost in oxidation cannot exist freely in solution, so they must be gained by another species in reduction [1]. The number of electrons lost by the reducing agent equals the number gained by the oxidising agent [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Reactivity series and metal displacement

**A648.** C. Zinc
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-1 -->

Explanation: Zinc is a stronger reducing agent than iron (Zn²⁺/Zn lies below Fe²⁺/Fe in the electrochemical series), so it reduces Fe²⁺ to Fe. Lead is a weaker reductant than iron, so it does not react, and neither do Cu or Ag.

**A649.** B. Grey-silver crystals form on the copper and the solution turns blue
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-2 -->

Explanation: Cu is a stronger reductant than Ag, so Cu(s) + 2Ag⁺(aq) → Cu²⁺(aq) + 2Ag(s). Silver deposits form and blue Cu²⁺ ions appear. Copper is more reactive than silver, so 'no change' is wrong.

**A650.** A. Mg(s)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-3 -->

Explanation: Mg²⁺/Mg has the most negative E° (−2.37 V) of these, so Mg(s) loses electrons most readily. Ag(s) is the weakest reductant of the four.

**A651.** D. X > Y > Z
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-4 -->

Explanation: X displaces Y, so X is more reactive than Y. Z cannot displace either, so Z is the least reactive. Putting Z first reverses what the 'no reaction' results mean.

**A652.** B. potassium reacts vigorously with the water itself, producing hydrogen gas and hydroxide ions
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-5 -->

Explanation: Potassium is such a strong reductant that it reduces water (2K + 2H₂O → 2K⁺ + 2OH⁻ + H₂) rather than the metal ions, often violently. K⁺ is actually a very weak oxidising agent.

**A653.** A reaction occurs; Pb²⁺(aq) (E° = −0.13 V) is a stronger oxidising agent than Zn²⁺(aq) (E° = −0.76 V), so Zn(s) is a stronger reductant than Pb(s) and can reduce Pb²⁺; Zn(s) + Pb²⁺(aq) → Zn²⁺(aq) + Pb(s) (grey crystals of lead form as zinc dissolves).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A654.** Iron is a stronger reducing agent than copper (Fe²⁺/Fe is below Cu²⁺/Cu in the electrochemical series), so iron is oxidised and the container corrodes while copper metal deposits; Fe(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Fe²⁺(aq) + Cu(s).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A655.** (a) A > C > B > D, since each metal displaces only those below it; (b) D²⁺ is the strongest oxidising agent; because D is the weakest reductant, its ion most readily gains electrons (every other metal can reduce D²⁺).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-8 -->

Explanation:

**A656.** A. Sodium
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-9 -->

Explanation: Sodium is a very strong reducing agent and reduces water: 2Na + 2H₂O → 2Na⁺ + 2OH⁻ + H₂. Iron reacts only very slowly with water (as rusting), and copper and lead do not react.

**A657.** C. No reaction occurs, because Ag is a weaker reducing agent than Cu
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-10 -->

Explanation: Ag(s) is a weaker reductant than Cu(s), so it cannot reduce Cu²⁺ and there is no reaction. Copper deposition would require silver to be the more reactive metal, which is the reverse of the actual order.

**A658.** D. A reddish-brown coating forms on the nail and the blue colour fades to a pale green
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-11 -->

Explanation: Fe(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Fe²⁺(aq) + Cu(s): copper deposits as a reddish-brown layer and the blue Cu²⁺ is replaced by pale green Fe²⁺. 'No change' is wrong because iron is the stronger reductant.

**A659.** A. Cu²⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-12 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ gains electrons (is reduced), so it is the oxidising agent. Zn(s) is oxidised and so is the reducing agent.

**A660.** C. Lead metal in silver nitrate solution
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-13 -->

Explanation: Pb is a stronger reducing agent than Ag, so Pb(s) + 2Ag⁺(aq) → Pb²⁺(aq) + 2Ag(s). In each other option the metal is less reactive than the metal ion's parent metal, so there is no reaction.

**A661.** D. 5.58 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-14 -->

Explanation: Fe + Cu²⁺ → Fe²⁺ + Cu is a 1 : 1 ratio, so n(Fe) = 0.100 mol and m = 0.100 × 55.8 = 5.58 g. 6.35 g is the mass of copper formed, not iron dissolved.

**A662.** A. between zinc and copper in the reactivity series
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-15 -->

Explanation: M reduces Cu²⁺, so it is more reactive than copper. It cannot reduce Zn²⁺, so it is less reactive than zinc. Hence it lies between zinc and copper.

**A663.** C. Copper
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-16 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺/Cu (+0.34 V) lies above H⁺/H₂ (0.00 V), so Cu is a weaker reductant than H₂ and cannot reduce H⁺. Mg, Zn and Fe all lie below hydrogen and react.

**A664.** B. has a thin, unreactive coating of aluminium oxide that protects the metal beneath
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-17 -->

Explanation: A tightly adhering Al₂O₃ layer forms on the surface and prevents contact with water and oxygen. The reactivity series is unchanged, so aluminium is still the stronger reductant than zinc.

**A665.** C. 10.8 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-18 -->

Explanation: Cu + 2Ag⁺ → Cu²⁺ + 2Ag, so n(Ag) = 2 × 0.0500 = 0.100 mol and m = 0.100 × 107.9 = 10.8 g. 5.40 g comes from ignoring the 1 : 2 mole ratio.

**A666.** C. loses electrons to the less reactive metal ion, which is reduced to metal
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-19 -->

Explanation: The more reactive metal is the stronger reductant, so it transfers electrons to the ion, which is reduced to the solid metal. Gaining electrons more readily describes the weaker reductant's ion, not the reactive metal.

**A667.** A reddish-brown solid (copper) deposits on the magnesium [1]; the blue colour of the solution fades [1]; Mg(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Mg²⁺(aq) + Cu(s) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-20 -->

Explanation:

**A668.** Place a strip of each metal into a solution of each of the other metals' nitrates (or a systematic set of pairs) and observe for deposits of metal [1]; a reaction (metal deposit on the strip) shows the strip is more reactive than the metal in solution [1]; zinc displaces all three others, silver displaces none [1]; order from most to least reactive: Zn > Pb > Cu > Ag [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-21 -->

Explanation:

**A669.** Oxidation: Al(s) → Al³⁺(aq) + 3e⁻ [1]; reduction: Cu²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → Cu(s) [1]; overall: 2Al(s) + 3Cu²⁺(aq) → 2Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cu(s) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-22 -->

Explanation:

**A670.** n(Zn) = 3.27 ÷ 65.4 = 0.0500 mol [1]; Zn + Cu²⁺ → Zn²⁺ + Cu, so n(Cu) = n(Zn) = 0.0500 mol [1]; m(Cu) = 0.0500 × 63.5 = 3.18 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-23 -->

Explanation:

**A671.** Zinc lies below hydrogen in the reactivity series, so Zn is a stronger reducing agent than H₂ and can reduce H⁺ to H₂ [1]; copper lies above hydrogen, so it is a weaker reducing agent than H₂ and cannot reduce H⁺ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-24 -->

Explanation:

**A672.** Iron is a weaker reducing agent than magnesium (Fe²⁺/Fe lies above Mg²⁺/Mg in the electrochemical series) [1]; so Fe(s) cannot transfer electrons to Mg²⁺ and no displacement occurs [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-25 -->

Explanation:

**A673.** A reaction in which a more reactive metal transfers electrons to the ion of a less reactive metal, so the more reactive metal is oxidised and the ion is reduced to the metal [1]; e.g. Mg(s) + 2Ag⁺(aq) → Mg²⁺(aq) + 2Ag(s) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-26 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 2: Measuring and analysing chemicals

#### Concentration units and dilution

**A674.** C. 0.400 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-1 -->

Explanation: n = 5.85 ÷ 58.5 = 0.100 mol; c = 0.100 ÷ 0.2500 L = 0.400 mol L⁻¹. 0.100 mol L⁻¹ forgets to divide by the volume in litres.

**A675.** A. 0.100 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-2 -->

Explanation: c₂ = c₁V₁ ÷ V₂ = 2.00 × 25.0 ÷ 500.0 = 0.100 mol L⁻¹. The amount of HCl is unchanged by dilution; only the volume increases twenty-fold.

**A676.** B. 15 mg L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-3 -->

Explanation: 1 ppm = 1 mg per kg, and 1 L of dilute aqueous solution has a mass of about 1 kg, so 15 ppm = 15 mg L⁻¹. 15 µg L⁻¹ would be 15 ppb.

**A677.** D. 0.600 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-4 -->

Explanation: Each Al₂(SO₄)₃ releases 3 SO₄²⁻ ions, so [SO₄²⁻] = 3 × 0.200 = 0.600 mol L⁻¹. 0.400 mol L⁻¹ is the Al³⁺ concentration.

**A678.** B. 4.00 % (m/v)
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-5 -->

Explanation: % (m/v) = grams of solute per 100 mL: 12.0 g ÷ 300 mL × 100 = 4.00 %. 0.0400 omits the ×100 conversion.

**A679.** V₁ = c₂V₂ ÷ c₁ = 0.250 × 2.00 ÷ 10.0 = 0.0500 L = 50.0 mL of stock; measure 50.0 mL accurately (pipette/burette), transfer to a 2.00 L volumetric flask and make up to the mark with deionised water, then mix.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A680.** (a) 0.48 mg ÷ 0.200 L = 2.4 mg L⁻¹ = 2.4 ppm; (b) M(NO₃⁻) = 14.0 + 3 × 16.0 = 62.0 g mol⁻¹; c = 2.4 × 10⁻³ g L⁻¹ ÷ 62.0 g mol⁻¹ = 3.9 × 10⁻⁵ mol L⁻¹ (2 significant figures).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A681.** First dilution: 0.500 × 10.0 ÷ 100.0 = 0.0500 mol L⁻¹; second dilution: 0.0500 × 5.00 ÷ 50.0 = 0.00500 mol L⁻¹ (5.00 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹); n(Cu²⁺) = 0.00500 × 0.0500 L = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol (equal to the moles in the 5.00 mL aliquot).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-8 -->

Explanation:

**A682.** A. 25 ppb
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-9 -->

Explanation: 1 ppb is 1 µg per litre (1 µg per kg of dilute aqueous solution), so 25 µg L⁻¹ is 25 ppb. 25 000 ppb would be 25 mg L⁻¹ (25 ppm).

**A683.** B. 0.200 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-10 -->

Explanation: n = 4.00 ÷ 40.0 = 0.100 mol; c = 0.100 ÷ 0.5000 L = 0.200 mol L⁻¹. 2.00 mol L⁻¹ results from using 50.0 mL instead of 500.0 mL.

**A684.** C. 0.200 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-11 -->

Explanation: c₂ = 0.800 × 50.0 ÷ 200.0 = 0.200 mol L⁻¹, since the volume has increased four-fold. 3.20 mol L⁻¹ comes from multiplying by the dilution factor instead of dividing.

**A685.** D. 0.300 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-12 -->

Explanation: CaCl₂ → Ca²⁺ + 2Cl⁻, so [Cl⁻] = 2 × 0.150 = 0.300 mol L⁻¹. 0.150 mol L⁻¹ is the Ca²⁺ concentration.

**A686.** A. 60.0 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-13 -->

Explanation: V₂ = 1.00 × 20.0 ÷ 0.250 = 80.0 mL final volume, so 80.0 − 20.0 = 60.0 mL of water is added. 80.0 mL is the final volume, not the volume of water added.

**A687.** B. Volumetric flask
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-14 -->

Explanation: A volumetric flask is calibrated to contain one precise volume when filled to its mark. A measuring cylinder is graduated but much less accurate.

**A688.** C. 2.65 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-15 -->

Explanation: n = 0.100 × 0.2500 = 0.0250 mol; m = 0.0250 × 106.0 = 2.65 g. 10.6 g is the mass in one litre of 0.100 mol L⁻¹ solution, ignoring the actual volume.

**A689.** D. 6.00 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-16 -->

Explanation: % (v/v) = 15.0 ÷ 250.0 × 100 = 6.00 %. 16.7 % results from inverting the ratio (250.0 ÷ 15.0).

**A690.** A. 25.0
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-17 -->

Explanation: Dilution factor = V₂ ÷ V₁ = 250.0 ÷ 10.0 = 25.0, so the concentration falls to one twenty-fifth of the original. 0.0400 is the inverse, the fraction of the original concentration remaining.

**A691.** B. 10 mg
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-18 -->

Explanation: 2.0 ppm = 2.0 mg L⁻¹, so 5.0 L contains 2.0 × 5.0 = 10 mg. 10 g confuses mg with g.

**A692.** C. 7.90 g L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-19 -->

Explanation: Mass concentration = c × M = 0.0500 × 158.0 = 7.90 g L⁻¹. 3.16 × 10⁻⁴ results from dividing by the molar mass instead of multiplying.

**A693.** D. The amount of solute, in mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-20 -->

Explanation: Adding solvent does not add or remove solute particles, so n(solute) is constant, which is why c₁V₁ = c₂V₂. Concentration and volume both change.

**A694.** A. 0.250 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-21 -->

Explanation: n = 0.0200 + 0.0300 = 0.0500 mol in 0.2000 L, so c = 0.250 mol L⁻¹. 0.500 mol L⁻¹ simply adds the two concentrations without accounting for the larger volume.

**A695.** B. 1.00 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-22 -->

Explanation: c = 0.0400 g L⁻¹ ÷ 40.0 g mol⁻¹ = 1.00 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹. 1.60 mol L⁻¹ results from multiplying by the molar mass instead of dividing.

**A696.** C. 25.0 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-23 -->

Explanation: V₁ = c₂V₂ ÷ c₁ = 0.600 × 500.0 ÷ 12.0 = 25.0 mL. 250 mL is out by a factor of ten from a decimal slip.

**A697.** n(KCl) = c × V = 0.100 × 0.5000 = 0.0500 mol [1]; m = n × M = 0.0500 × 74.6 = 3.73 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-24 -->

Explanation:

**A698.** Mass concentration = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹ × 35.5 g mol⁻¹ = 8.875 × 10⁻³ g L⁻¹ [1]; = 8.88 mg L⁻¹ = 8.88 ppm (assuming density 1.00 g mL⁻¹) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-25 -->

Explanation:

**A699.** Na₃PO₄(s) → 3Na⁺(aq) + PO₄³⁻(aq) [1]; [Na⁺] = 3 × 0.250 = 0.750 mol L⁻¹ [1]; [PO₄³⁻] = 0.250 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-26 -->

Explanation:

**A700.** n(HNO₃) = 0.600 × 0.0200 = 0.0120 mol [1]; final volume = 20.0 + 30.0 = 50.0 mL [1]; c = 0.0120 ÷ 0.0500 = 0.240 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-27 -->

Explanation:

**A701.** A volumetric flask is calibrated to hold one precise volume when filled to its mark, whereas a beaker's graduations are only approximate [1]; the solute is first dissolved in less water and then made up to the mark so that the total final volume of solution (not the volume of water added) is accurately known, giving an accurate concentration [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-28 -->

Explanation:

**A702.** Use c₁V₁ = c₂V₂ (concentration in ppm is directly proportional to the amount of solute per volume) [1]; V₁ = 25.0 × 100.0 ÷ 1000 = 2.50 mL [1]; pipette 2.50 mL of stock into a 100.0 mL volumetric flask, then add deionised water to the mark and mix by inverting [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-29 -->

Explanation:

**A703.** c(MgCl₂) = 0.400 × 50.0 ÷ 200.0 = 0.100 mol L⁻¹ [1]; [Mg²⁺] = 0.100 mol L⁻¹ [1]; [Cl⁻] = 2 × 0.100 = 0.200 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Stoichiometry and limiting reactants

**A704.** B. 22 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-1 -->

Explanation: n(CH₄) = 8.0 ÷ 16.0 = 0.50 mol; the mole ratio is 1:1, so n(CO₂) = 0.50 mol; m = 0.50 × 44.0 = 22 g. 44 g assumes 1 mol of methane.

**A705.** B. O₂ is the limiting reactant
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-2 -->

Explanation: n(H₂) = 2.0 mol and n(O₂) = 0.500 mol. 2.0 mol H₂ needs 1.0 mol O₂, but only 0.500 mol is present, so O₂ is limiting. Comparing masses (4.0 g vs 16.0 g) wrongly suggests H₂ is limiting.

**A706.** D. 0.0250 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-3 -->

Explanation: n(Zn) = 0.0500 mol and n(HCl) = 0.0500 mol. Zn needs 0.100 mol HCl, so HCl is limiting; n(H₂) = ½ × 0.0500 = 0.0250 mol. 0.0500 mol assumes Zn is limiting.

**A707.** C. 75.0 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-4 -->

Explanation: Percentage yield = actual ÷ theoretical × 100 = 9.00 ÷ 12.0 × 100 = 75.0 %. 133 % comes from inverting the ratio, and a yield above 100 % signals an error.

**A708.** A. 0.90 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-5 -->

Explanation: n(CO) = 3/2 × n(Fe) = 1.5 × 0.60 = 0.90 mol. 0.40 mol uses the ratio upside down (2/3).

**A709.** n(Ag⁺) = 0.200 × 0.0500 = 0.0100 mol; n(Cl⁻) = 0.150 × 0.0300 = 0.00450 mol; the ratio is 1:1, so Cl⁻ (NaCl) is limiting; m(AgCl) = 0.00450 × 143.4 = 0.645 g; excess Ag⁺ = 0.0100 − 0.00450 = 0.00550 mol.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A710.** n(CaCO₃) = 2.50 ÷ 100.1 = 0.0250 mol; n(HCl) = 1.00 × 0.0400 = 0.0400 mol; 0.0250 mol CaCO₃ would need 0.0500 mol HCl, so HCl is limiting; n(CO₂) = ½ × 0.0400 = 0.0200 mol, m = 0.0200 × 44.0 = 0.880 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A711.** Any two of: the reaction may not go to completion (e.g. it is reversible or too slow); side reactions form other products; product is lost during transfer, filtration or washing; reactants are impure, so fewer moles are present than assumed.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-8 -->

Explanation:

**A712.** A. 1.0 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-9 -->

Explanation: n(NH₃) = 2/3 × n(H₂) = 2/3 × 1.5 = 1.0 mol. 2.25 mol comes from using the ratio upside down (3/2).

**A713.** B. 8.06 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-10 -->

Explanation: n(Mg) = 4.86 ÷ 24.3 = 0.200 mol; the ratio Mg:MgO is 1:1, so m(MgO) = 0.200 × 40.3 = 8.06 g. 4.03 g wrongly halves the amount using the coefficient of O₂.

**A714.** C. 2.48 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-11 -->

Explanation: n(O₂) = ½ × 0.200 = 0.100 mol; V = 0.100 × 24.8 = 2.48 L. 4.96 L assumes a 1:1 ratio between H₂O₂ and O₂.

**A715.** D. The reactant that is completely used up first, so no more product can form
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-12 -->

Explanation: The limiting reactant is the one fully consumed first, and it sets the maximum amount of product. Initial mass or coefficient alone does not identify it, because the amounts in mol and the ratio must be compared.

**A716.** A. 21.0 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-13 -->

Explanation: Actual = 84.0 ÷ 100 × 25.0 = 21.0 g. 29.8 g comes from dividing by 0.840 instead of multiplying, which would give more than the theoretical maximum.

**A717.** B. 0.20 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-14 -->

Explanation: 0.30 mol Al needs 0.45 mol Cl₂, but only 0.30 mol is present, so Cl₂ is limiting: n(AlCl₃) = 2/3 × 0.30 = 0.20 mol. 0.30 mol wrongly takes Al as limiting.

**A718.** C. 1.0 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-15 -->

Explanation: O₂ is limiting and uses 2 × 0.50 = 1.0 mol of Mg, leaving 2.0 − 1.0 = 1.0 mol Mg. 1.5 mol results from subtracting 0.50 mol directly, ignoring the 2:1 ratio.

**A719.** D. 32.0 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-16 -->

Explanation: n(H₂SO₄) = 0.200 × 0.0200 = 0.00400 mol; n(NaOH) = 2 × 0.00400 = 0.00800 mol; V = 0.00800 ÷ 0.250 = 0.0320 L = 32.0 mL. 16.0 mL ignores the 1:2 ratio.

**A720.** A. 4.67 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-17 -->

Explanation: Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s) is a 1:1 ratio, so n(BaSO₄) = 0.0200 mol and m = 0.0200 × 233.4 = 4.67 g. 9.34 g wrongly doubles the amount.

**A721.** B. 50 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-18 -->

Explanation: Equal volumes of gases at the same temperature and pressure contain equal amounts, so volume ratios match mole ratios: 10 × 5 = 50 L. 30 L uses the coefficient of CO₂ instead of O₂.

**A722.** C. The precipitate was not fully dried and still contained water or impurities
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-19 -->

Explanation: A yield above 100 % means more mass was measured than is theoretically possible, which happens when the product is wet or impure. The other options would all lower the measured mass.

**A723.** A. Amounts in moles of reactants and products
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-20 -->

Explanation: Coefficients give the mole ratio in which substances react and form. They do not give mass ratios, because molar masses differ.

**A724.** B. 1.24 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-21 -->

Explanation: n(Mg) = 0.0500 mol; the Mg:H₂ ratio is 1:1, so V = 0.0500 × 24.8 = 1.24 L. 2.48 L wrongly doubles the amount using the coefficient of HCl.

**A725.** The limiting reactant is the reactant that is completely consumed first [1]; once it is used up the reaction stops, so no more product can form regardless of how much of the other reactants (in excess) remains [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-22 -->

Explanation:

**A726.** n(Al) = 5.40 ÷ 27.0 = 0.200 mol [1]; n(Al₂O₃) = 2/4 × 0.200 = 0.100 mol [1]; m(Al₂O₃) = 0.100 × 102.0 = 10.2 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-23 -->

Explanation:

**A727.** n(Al) = 0.540 ÷ 27.0 = 0.0200 mol [1]; n(HCl) = 1.00 × 0.0500 = 0.0500 mol [1]; 0.0200 mol Al needs 0.0600 mol HCl, so HCl is limiting [1]; n(H₂) = 3/6 × 0.0500 = 0.0250 mol, V = 0.0250 × 24.8 = 0.620 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-24 -->

Explanation:

**A728.** n(CuSO₄) = 0.500 × 0.0200 = 0.0100 mol, so n(Cu(OH)₂) = 0.0100 mol (1:1) [1]; theoretical mass = 0.0100 × 97.5 = 0.975 g [1]; percentage yield = 0.850 ÷ 0.975 × 100 = 87.2 % [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-25 -->

Explanation:

**A729.** The limiting reactant depends on the amounts in mol available compared with the mole ratio in the balanced equation, not on mass [1]; substances have different molar masses and coefficients, so each mass must be converted to moles and compared with the stoichiometric ratio [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-26 -->

Explanation:

**A730.** n(C₂H₅OH) = 2.30 ÷ 46.0 = 0.0500 mol [1]; n(O₂) = 3 × 0.0500 = 0.150 mol [1]; V(O₂) = 0.150 × 24.8 = 3.72 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-27 -->

Explanation:

**A731.** n(Pb²⁺) = 0.400 × 0.0250 = 0.0100 mol; n(I⁻) = 0.600 × 0.0250 = 0.0150 mol; 0.0100 mol Pb²⁺ needs 0.0200 mol I⁻, so I⁻ (KI) is limiting [1]; n(PbI₂) = ½ × 0.0150 = 0.00750 mol [1]; m = 0.00750 × 461.0 = 3.46 g [1]; Pb²⁺ remaining = 0.0100 − 0.00750 = 0.00250 mol in 0.0500 L, so c = 0.0500 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-28 -->

Explanation:

**A732.** Theoretical mass of CaO = 5.61 ÷ 0.800 = 7.01 g, so n(CaO) = 7.01 ÷ 56.1 = 0.125 mol [1]; n(CaCO₃) = 0.125 mol (1:1) [1]; m(CaCO₃) = 0.125 × 100.1 = 12.5 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-29 -->

Explanation:

**A733.** n(Na) = 4.60 ÷ 23.0 = 0.200 mol, which needs only 0.100 mol Cl₂ [1]; Cl₂ (0.150 mol) is in excess, so Na is the limiting reactant [1]; n(NaCl) = 0.200 mol, m = 0.200 × 58.5 = 11.7 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Gas laws and molar volume

**A734.** B. 6.20 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-1 -->

Explanation: V = n × Vm = 0.250 × 24.8 = 6.20 L. 5.60 L uses 22.4 L mol⁻¹, the molar volume at STP (0 °C), not at SLC.

**A735.** D. 400 kPa
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-2 -->

Explanation: Boyle's law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂, so P₂ = 100 × 2.0 ÷ 0.50 = 400 kPa. Reducing the volume to a quarter quadruples the pressure; 25 kPa wrongly assumes pressure falls.

**A736.** A. 0.120 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-3 -->

Explanation: n = PV ÷ RT = (150 kPa × 2.00 L) ÷ (8.31 × 300) = 300 ÷ 2493 = 0.120 mol. kPa × L gives J, so no extra unit conversion is needed. 0.0120 mol comes from a units slip.

**A737.** C. 667 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-4 -->

Explanation: Charles' law needs kelvin: V₂ = 500 × 400 K ÷ 300 K = 667 mL. 2350 mL comes from using Celsius temperatures (500 × 127 ÷ 27).

**A738.** B. number of molecules
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-5 -->

Explanation: Avogadro's hypothesis: equal volumes of gases at the same T and P contain equal numbers of particles (molecules). The atom count differs because H₂ is diatomic and CO₂ is triatomic, and the masses differ too.

**A739.** n(CO₂) = V ÷ Vm = 3.10 ÷ 24.8 = 0.125 mol; m = 0.125 × 44.0 = 5.50 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A740.** T = 298 K; n(N₂) = PV ÷ RT = (120 × 60.0) ÷ (8.31 × 298) = 2.91 mol; n(NaN₃) = 2/3 × 2.91 = 1.94 mol; M(NaN₃) = 23.0 + 3 × 14.0 = 65.0 g mol⁻¹, so m = 1.94 × 65.0 = 126 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A741.** Heating increases the average kinetic energy of the gas particles, so they move faster; they collide with the container walls more often and with greater force; because the volume is fixed, the force per unit area of wall (pressure) increases.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-8 -->

Explanation:

**A742.** A. 0.500 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-9 -->

Explanation: n = V ÷ Vm = 12.4 ÷ 24.8 = 0.500 mol. 0.554 mol comes from using 22.4 L mol⁻¹, the molar volume at STP, instead of the SLC value.

**A743.** B. 44.8 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-10 -->

Explanation: V = n × Vm = 2.00 × 22.4 = 44.8 L. 49.6 L uses the SLC molar volume of 24.8 L mol⁻¹, and 11.2 L divides instead of multiplying.

**A744.** C. 200 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-11 -->

Explanation: Charles' law with kelvin temperatures: V₂ = 300 × 200 K ÷ 300 K = 200 mL. Using Celsius values gives a meaningless negative volume, and 450 mL inverts the temperature ratio.

**A745.** A. 4.0 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-12 -->

Explanation: V₂ = V₁ × (P₁ ÷ P₂) × (T₂ ÷ T₁) = 4.0 × ½ × 2 = 4.0 L. The halving from doubled pressure is cancelled by the doubling from doubled temperature.

**A746.** C. 1.00 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-13 -->

Explanation: n = 5.60 ÷ 22.4 = 0.250 mol, so m = 0.250 × 4.00 = 1.00 g. 0.903 g comes from using 24.8 L mol⁻¹, which applies at SLC rather than STP.

**A747.** D. 1.29 g L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-14 -->

Explanation: Density = M ÷ Vm = 32.0 ÷ 24.8 = 1.29 g L⁻¹. 0.775 g L⁻¹ inverts the ratio, and 32.0 is the molar mass, not a density.

**A748.** A. 5.0 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-15 -->

Explanation: At the same T and P, gas volumes are in the same ratio as the mole ratio, so V(O₂) = ½ × 10 = 5.0 L. Assuming equal volumes (10 L) ignores the 2 : 1 coefficients.

**A749.** B. 0.0201 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-16 -->

Explanation: Convert 500 mL to 0.500 L: n = PV ÷ RT = (100 × 0.500) ÷ (8.31 × 300) = 0.0201 mol. Using 500 directly for the volume gives 20.1 mol, which is 1000 times too large.

**A750.** B. V against 1 ÷ P
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-17 -->

Explanation: Boyle's law gives PV = constant, so V is directly proportional to 1 ÷ P. A plot of P against V is a curve (hyperbola), not a straight line.

**A751.** D. The temperature is higher, so the gas particles move faster and spread further apart
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-18 -->

Explanation: A higher temperature (25 °C rather than 0 °C) gives more energetic collisions, so a mole of gas occupies a larger volume. A mole always contains the same number of particles, and particle size and mass do not change with temperature.

**A752.** A. 2.48 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-19 -->

Explanation: n(CaCO₃) = 10.0 ÷ 100.1 = 0.0999 mol, equal to n(CO₂) (1 : 1), so V = 0.0999 × 24.8 = 2.48 L. 2.24 L results from using the STP molar volume.

**A753.** B. 3.0 L of carbon dioxide
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-20 -->

Explanation: By Avogadro's hypothesis, the number of molecules is proportional to volume at the same T and P, so the largest volume (3.0 L) has the most. The identity, mass or molecular size of the gas does not matter.

**A754.** C. 327 °C
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-21 -->

Explanation: At constant volume P ∝ T (in kelvin), so doubling P gives T = 600 K = 327 °C. 600 °C wrongly doubles a Celsius value.

**A755.** n(CH₄) = m ÷ M = 8.00 ÷ 16.0 = 0.500 mol [1]; V = n × Vm = 0.500 × 24.8 = 12.4 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-22 -->

Explanation:

**A756.** Boyle's law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂ [1]; P₂ = 150 × 3.0 ÷ 1.2 = 375 kPa [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-23 -->

Explanation:

**A757.** Temperatures in kelvin: T₁ = 288 K and T₂ = 318 K [1]; V₂ = 250 × 318 ÷ 288 = 276 mL [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-24 -->

Explanation:

**A758.** Convert to kelvin: T₁ = 300 K, T₂ = 400 K [1]; combined gas law: P₁V₁ ÷ T₁ = P₂V₂ ÷ T₂ [1]; V₂ = 2.50 × (200 ÷ 100) × (400 ÷ 300) = 6.67 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-25 -->

Explanation:

**A759.** n(H₂) = 2.48 ÷ 24.8 = 0.100 mol [1]; n(Mg) = n(H₂) = 0.100 mol, from the 1 : 1 ratio [1]; m(Mg) = 0.100 × 24.3 = 2.43 g [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-26 -->

Explanation:

**A760.** T = 300 K; n = PV ÷ RT = (100 × 1.00) ÷ (8.31 × 300) = 0.0401 mol [1]; M = m ÷ n [1]; M = 1.50 ÷ 0.0401 = 37.4 g mol⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-27 -->

Explanation:

**A761.** With a smaller volume, the gas particles strike the container walls more frequently, as they have less distance to travel between collisions [1]; the average kinetic energy is unchanged (constant temperature), so the greater collision rate per unit area increases the pressure [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-28 -->

Explanation:

**A762.** Gas particles are very far apart, so the volume of the gas is mostly empty space and the size of individual particles is negligible [1]; the volume of a gas therefore depends only on the number of particles (at fixed T and P), not on their identity (Avogadro's hypothesis) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-29 -->

Explanation:

**A763.** At the same T and P, gas volumes are in the same ratio as the coefficients, so C₃H₈ : O₂ : CO₂ = 1 : 5 : 3 [1]; V(O₂) = 5 × 4.0 = 20 L [1]; V(CO₂) = 3 × 4.0 = 12 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Volumetric analysis and titration

**A764.** B. Pipette
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-1 -->

Explanation: A pipette delivers a single fixed, accurate volume (the aliquot). The burette delivers the titrant and measures the titre. A measuring cylinder is not accurate enough.

**A765.** C. too high, because the diluted titrant gives a larger titre
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-2 -->

Explanation: Water left in the burette dilutes the titrant, so a larger volume is needed to reach the end point. The calculation uses the original (higher) concentration, which overestimates the moles of titrant and hence the analyte concentration.

**A766.** A. 0.0920 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-3 -->

Explanation: n(HCl) = 0.100 × 0.01840 = 1.84 × 10⁻³ mol = n(NaOH) (1:1 ratio); c = 1.84 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.0920 mol L⁻¹. 0.109 mol L⁻¹ comes from inverting the volumes.

**A767.** D. 0.0675 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-4 -->

Explanation: n(NaOH) = 0.150 × 0.02250 = 3.375 × 10⁻³ mol; n(H₂SO₄) = ½ × that = 1.6875 × 10⁻³ mol; c = 1.6875 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02500 = 0.0675 mol L⁻¹. 0.135 mol L⁻¹ ignores the 1:2 ratio.

**A768.** B. 21.35 mL, 21.40 mL, 21.30 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-5 -->

Explanation: The range of 21.30 to 21.40 mL is 0.10 mL, so these three are concordant. The first set contains 22.10 mL, which is often a rough first titre and should be left out of the mean.

**A769.** n(NaOH) = 0.1050 × 0.01685 = 1.769 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CH₃COOH) in the aliquot (1:1); c(diluted) = 1.769 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.08846 mol L⁻¹; c(original) = 0.08846 × 10 (dilution factor 250.0/25.00) = 0.885 mol L⁻¹; 0.885 × 60.0 = 53.1 g L⁻¹ = 5.31 % (m/v).
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A770.** Phenolphthalein; at equivalence the solution contains ethanoate ions, a weak base, so the equivalence point pH is above 7 (about 8–9); the indicator must change colour within the steep pH change at equivalence, which phenolphthalein does, whereas methyl orange would change colour well before equivalence, giving a titre that is too small.
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A771.** Any three: high purity; known, stable chemical formula; does not absorb water or react with air (not hygroscopic or deliquescent); high molar mass to reduce weighing error; readily soluble; NaOH is unsuitable because it absorbs water from the air and reacts with atmospheric CO₂ (forming Na₂CO₃), so its mass does not reliably match the amount of NaOH.
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A772.** A. A solution whose concentration is accurately known
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-9 -->

Explanation: A standard solution has an accurately known concentration, so it can be used to determine the concentration of an unknown. It can be prepared from a primary standard or standardised against one, and it need not be neutral or 1.00 L.

**A773.** B. At the bottom of the meniscus, with the eye level with the meniscus
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-10 -->

Explanation: The reading is taken at the bottom of the meniscus with the eye at the same level, which avoids parallax error. Reading from above or below makes the reading appear higher or lower than it really is.

**A774.** C. The equivalence point is where the reactants are in exact stoichiometric ratio; the end point is where the indicator changes colour
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-11 -->

Explanation: The equivalence point is the stoichiometric point calculated from the equation, while the end point is the observed colour change of the indicator. A good indicator makes the two very close, but they are not defined as identical.

**A775.** D. 0.0656 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-12 -->

Explanation: n(NaOH) = 0.0800 × 0.02050 = 1.64 × 10⁻³ mol = n(HCl) (1:1); c(HCl) = 1.64 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02500 = 0.0656 mol L⁻¹. 0.0976 mol L⁻¹ comes from dividing by the titre instead of the aliquot volume.

**A776.** A. No effect, because the amount of analyte in the flask is unchanged
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-13 -->

Explanation: The extra water does not change the number of moles of analyte in the aliquot, so the same volume of titrant is needed to reach the end point. Dilution in the flask therefore does not affect the result.

**A777.** B. too low, because the aliquot contains fewer moles of analyte
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-14 -->

Explanation: Residual water dilutes the aliquot, so it contains fewer moles of analyte than expected and the titre is smaller. The calculation assumes the original aliquot concentration, so the result is too low.

**A778.** C. Too high, because the measured titre is larger than the volume of titrant actually delivered
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-15 -->

Explanation: Some of the volume read off the burette was air, so the recorded titre is larger than the volume of liquid delivered. This overestimates the moles of titrant and gives an analyte concentration that is too high.

**A779.** D. 0.0600 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-16 -->

Explanation: n(HCl) = 0.100 × 0.02400 = 2.40 × 10⁻³ mol; n(Na₂CO₃) = ½ × 2.40 × 10⁻³ = 1.20 × 10⁻³ mol; c = 1.20 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.0600 mol L⁻¹. 0.120 mol L⁻¹ ignores the 1:2 mole ratio.

**A780.** A. Anhydrous sodium carbonate
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-17 -->

Explanation: Anhydrous sodium carbonate is pure, stable, non-hygroscopic and has a known formula. Sodium hydroxide absorbs water and CO₂, and concentrated hydrochloric acid loses HCl gas, so neither has a reliably known amount.

**A781.** B. 0.0500 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-18 -->

Explanation: n = 1.325 ÷ 106.0 = 0.0125 mol; c = 0.0125 ÷ 0.2500 = 0.0500 mol L⁻¹. 0.0125 is the number of moles, not the concentration, because the volume was not used.

**A782.** D. Methyl orange, because the solution at equivalence contains NH₄⁺ and is acidic
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-19 -->

Explanation: At equivalence the solution contains NH₄⁺, a weak acid, so the pH is below 7 and methyl orange (pH 3.1–4.4) changes colour in the steep region. Phenolphthalein would change colour well before the equivalence point is reached.

**A783.** A. 22.45 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-20 -->

Explanation: The rough titre is discarded and the concordant titres (22.45, 22.40, 22.50 mL, all within 0.10 mL) are averaged to give 22.45 mL. 22.74 mL results from including the rough titre.

**A784.** B. 0.0780 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-21 -->

Explanation: n(H₂SO₄) = 0.0500 × 0.01560 = 7.80 × 10⁻⁴ mol; n(KOH) = 2 × 7.80 × 10⁻⁴ = 1.56 × 10⁻³ mol; c = 1.56 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.0780 mol L⁻¹. 0.0390 mol L⁻¹ ignores the 2:1 ratio.

**A785.** C. Indicators are weak acids or bases, so a larger amount would react with the titrant and alter the titre
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-22 -->

Explanation: Most indicators are themselves weak acids or bases, so excess indicator consumes some titrant and gives a titre that is too large. The amount is kept small to make this effect negligible.

**A786.** Burette is rinsed with a small amount of the titrant (standard solution) [1]; pipette is rinsed with a small amount of the analyte solution it will measure [1]; conical flask is rinsed with deionised water only, because the water does not change the moles of analyte in the aliquot, whereas rinsing with analyte would add extra moles and make the titre too large [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-23 -->

Explanation:

**A787.** The rough titre gives an approximate end point so that later titrations can add titrant dropwise near the end point for accuracy [1]; concordant titres (within 0.10 mL) show that the results are reproducible and that random errors are small, so the mean is reliable [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-24 -->

Explanation:

**A788.** Accuracy is how close the result is to the true value [1]; precision is how close repeated measurements (titres) are to each other [1]; a systematic error, such as a burette not rinsed with titrant or a consistently misread meniscus, shifts all titres the same way so they are concordant (precise) but the result is wrong (inaccurate) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-25 -->

Explanation:

**A789.** n(HCl) = 0.0950 × 0.02130 = 2.0235 × 10⁻³ mol = n(NH₃) in the aliquot (1:1) [1]; c(diluted) = 2.0235 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02500 = 0.0809 mol L⁻¹ [1]; c(original) = 0.08094 × 50 (dilution factor 500.0/10.00) = 4.05 mol L⁻¹ [1]; 4.047 × 17.0 = 68.8 g L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-26 -->

Explanation:

**A790.** The equivalence point of a strong acid with a strong base is at pH 7, which lies within the range of bromothymol blue [1]; the pH changes very steeply (from about 3 to 11) near equivalence, so a drop of titrant moves the pH through the whole range of the indicator [1]; therefore several indicators (such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange) give a nearly identical titre, whereas a weak acid has a smaller, higher-pH steep region so only some indicators are suitable [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-27 -->

Explanation:

**A791.** n(H₂C₂O₄·2H₂O) = 0.6300 ÷ 126.0 = 5.000 × 10⁻³ mol, so c = 5.000 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.1000 = 0.0500 mol L⁻¹ [1]; n(H₂C₂O₄) in aliquot = 0.0500 × 0.02000 = 1.000 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; n(NaOH) = 2 × 1.000 × 10⁻³ = 2.000 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; c(NaOH) = 2.000 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.01940 = 0.103 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-28 -->

Explanation:

**A792.** Accurately weigh the required mass of the primary standard (for example by difference) [1]; dissolve it in a beaker with deionised water, then transfer the solution to the volumetric flask, rinsing the beaker and funnel into the flask [1]; add deionised water until the bottom of the meniscus sits on the calibration mark at eye level [1]; stopper and invert the flask several times to mix thoroughly [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Gravimetric analysis

**A793.** C. ensure all water has been removed from the precipitate
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-1 -->

Explanation: Heating to constant mass confirms that no more water is being lost, so the recorded mass is of dry precipitate only. Removing soluble impurities is the job of washing, not drying.

**A794.** A. ensure that all chloride ions are precipitated as AgCl
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-2 -->

Explanation: An excess of Ag⁺ makes chloride the limiting reactant, so all of it is precipitated and the mass of AgCl reflects the total chloride. Na⁺ does not react with Ag⁺.

**A795.** B. 3.55 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-3 -->

Explanation: m(Cl) = 0.287 × 35.5 ÷ 143.4 = 0.0711 g; % = 0.0711 ÷ 2.00 × 100 = 3.55 %. 14.4 % takes the mass of AgCl as if it were all chloride.

**A796.** D. It will be too high, because the dried precipitate mass includes NaCl
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-4 -->

Explanation: Impurities that dry onto the precipitate add mass, which is wrongly counted as BaSO₄, so the sulfate result is too high. NaCl being soluble does not help unless it is washed off.

**A797.** C. 0.192 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-5 -->

Explanation: n(BaSO₄) = 0.466 ÷ 233.4 = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol = n(SO₄²⁻); m = 2.00 × 10⁻³ × 96.1 = 0.192 g. 0.466 g is the mass of the whole precipitate, including barium.

**A798.** m(H₂O) = 2.50 − 1.60 = 0.90 g, n(H₂O) = 0.90 ÷ 18.0 = 0.050 mol; n(CuSO₄) = 1.60 ÷ 159.6 = 0.0100 mol; ratio H₂O : CuSO₄ = 0.050 ÷ 0.0100 ≈ 5, so x = 5 (CuSO₄·5H₂O).
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-6 -->

Explanation:

**A799.** n(BaSO₄) = 0.0583 ÷ 233.4 = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol; n(SO₄²⁻) = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol (1:1); m(SO₄²⁻) = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ × 96.1 = 0.0240 g = 24.0 mg; concentration = 24.0 mg ÷ 0.2000 L = 1.20 × 10² mg L⁻¹ = 120 ppm.
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-7 -->

Explanation:

**A800.** Any two with effect: the precipitate is not dried to constant mass, so the mass is too high and the result is overestimated; fine precipitate passes through the filter paper, or some precipitate stays dissolved, so the mass is too low and the result is underestimated; soluble impurities are not washed off or are co-precipitated, so the result is too high; not enough precipitating agent is added, so precipitation is incomplete and the result is too low.
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-8 -->

Explanation:

**A801.** B. converting it to a pure solid of known formula and measuring its mass
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-9 -->

Explanation: In gravimetric analysis the analyte is isolated as a pure, stable solid and weighed, and its amount is calculated from that mass. Measuring a volume of reagent describes titration, not gravimetric analysis.

**A802.** C. barium chloride
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-10 -->

Explanation: Ba²⁺ forms insoluble BaSO₄ with sulfate ions. Sodium nitrate, potassium chloride and hydrochloric acid contain no cation that forms an insoluble sulfate.

**A803.** D. filtrate
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-11 -->

Explanation: The filtrate is the liquid that passes through the filter paper. The precipitate (residue) is the solid that is retained on the paper.

**A804.** B. 0.100 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-12 -->

Explanation: n(CaCO₃) = 0.250 ÷ 100.1 = 2.50 × 10⁻³ mol = n(Ca²⁺); m(Ca) = 2.50 × 10⁻³ × 40.1 = 0.100 g. 0.250 g is the mass of the whole precipitate, which includes carbonate.

**A805.** C. 0.585 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-13 -->

Explanation: n(AgCl) = 1.434 ÷ 143.4 = 0.0100 mol = n(NaCl); m(NaCl) = 0.0100 × 58.5 = 0.585 g. 0.355 g is the mass of chloride only, not NaCl.

**A806.** D. It is soluble in water so that it is easy to wash
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-14 -->

Explanation: The precipitate must be insoluble so that essentially none is lost in the filtrate or during washing. Being soluble in water would make it unsuitable.

**A807.** A. the crucible mass does not change during the experiment
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-15 -->

Explanation: A crucible at constant mass will not gain or lose mass (for example, by losing moisture) during heating, so any later mass change comes from the precipitate. It has nothing to do with dissolving the precipitate.

**A808.** D. too low, because the measured mass of precipitate is less than the actual mass
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-16 -->

Explanation: Lost precipitate means a smaller mass is weighed, so the calculated amount of analyte is too low. The reaction still produced the full amount, but it was not all weighed.

**A809.** B. 27.5 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-17 -->

Explanation: n(BaSO₄) = 0.2334 ÷ 233.4 = 1.000 × 10⁻³ mol = n(Ba); m(Ba) = 0.1373 g; % = 0.1373 ÷ 0.500 × 100 = 27.5 %. 58.8 % is the percentage of barium in pure BaSO₄, not in the sample.

**A810.** A. add a few more drops of barium chloride to the filtrate and see whether more precipitate forms
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-18 -->

Explanation: If no further precipitate forms when more Ba²⁺ is added to the filtrate, no sulfate remains and precipitation was complete. Weighing or boiling the filtrate does not show whether sulfate ions remain.

**A811.** B. 0.0200 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-19 -->

Explanation: n(PbI₂) = 0.461 ÷ 461.0 = 1.00 × 10⁻³ mol = n(Pb²⁺); c = 1.00 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.0500 = 0.0200 mol L⁻¹. Doubling the answer wrongly applies a 2:1 ratio, but Pb²⁺ and PbI₂ are in a 1:1 ratio.

**A812.** A. The precipitate has reached constant mass and 0.497 g should be used
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-20 -->

Explanation: The last two masses agree, which shows all the water has been removed, so 0.497 g is the dry mass. Averaging includes the earlier masses of a still-damp precipitate and gives a value that is too high.

**A813.** C. 18.0 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-21 -->

Explanation: Mass of water lost = 5.00 − 4.10 = 0.90 g; % = 0.90 ÷ 5.00 × 100 = 18.0 %. 22.0 % divides by the mass of the anhydrous solid instead of the original hydrated sample.

**A814.** Washing removes soluble impurities (such as excess precipitating reagent or spectator ions) that are stuck to the surface of the precipitate [1]; if left, these would dry onto the precipitate, adding mass and making the calculated amount of analyte too high [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-22 -->

Explanation:

**A815.** Accurately weigh the sample and dissolve it in distilled water [1]; add excess silver nitrate solution so that all chloride ions precipitate as AgCl [1]; filter the mixture and wash the precipitate with distilled water [1]; dry the precipitate to constant mass, weigh it and use the mole ratio n(Cl⁻) = n(AgCl) to calculate the percentage of chloride [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-23 -->

Explanation:

**A816.** n(AgCl) = 2.868 ÷ 143.4 = 0.02000 mol [1]; n(NaCl) = n(AgCl) = 0.02000 mol (1:1 ratio) [1]; m(NaCl) = 0.02000 × 58.5 = 1.170 g [1]; % NaCl = 1.170 ÷ 1.250 × 100 = 93.6 % [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-24 -->

Explanation:

**A817.** n(CaCO₃) = 0.350 ÷ 100.1 = 3.50 × 10⁻³ mol [1]; n(Ca²⁺) = n(CaCO₃) = 3.50 × 10⁻³ mol (1:1) [1]; c(Ca²⁺) = 3.50 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.0250 = 0.140 mol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-25 -->

Explanation:

**A818.** m(H₂O) = 4.92 − 2.40 = 2.52 g, so n(H₂O) = 2.52 ÷ 18.0 = 0.140 mol [1]; n(MgSO₄) = 2.40 ÷ 120.4 = 0.0199 mol [1]; ratio H₂O : MgSO₄ = 0.140 ÷ 0.0199 ≈ 7, so x = 7 (MgSO₄·7H₂O) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-26 -->

Explanation:

**A819.** Not all of the analyte ions are precipitated, because the precipitating agent is the limiting reactant [1]; the mass of precipitate is lower than it should be, so the calculated mass of analyte is too low [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-27 -->

Explanation:

**A820.** The mass is still decreasing, so the precipitate has not reached constant mass and still contains water [1]; the student should reheat the precipitate and cool it in a desiccator to prevent water being reabsorbed [1]; then reweigh, repeating until two successive masses agree [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-28 -->

Explanation:

**A821.** It must be very insoluble, so that almost none is lost in the filtrate or during washing [1]; it must have a known formula (composition), so that the amount of analyte can be calculated from its mass [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-29 -->

Explanation:

**A822.** n(AgCl) = 0.0287 ÷ 143.4 = 2.00 × 10⁻⁴ mol [1]; n(Cl⁻) = n(AgCl) = 2.00 × 10⁻⁴ mol (1:1) [1]; m(Cl⁻) = 2.00 × 10⁻⁴ × 35.5 = 7.10 × 10⁻³ g = 7.10 mg [1]; concentration = 7.10 mg ÷ 0.2500 L = 28.4 mg L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Colorimetry and UV-visible spectroscopy

**A823.** B. Orange light, because the solution absorbs it most strongly
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-1 -->

Explanation: A solution looks blue because it transmits blue light and absorbs the complementary colour, orange. Measuring at the wavelength absorbed most strongly gives the greatest sensitivity. Blue light would mostly pass straight through.

**A824.** A. 2.6 mg L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-2 -->

Explanation: Absorbance is proportional to concentration: c = 0.26 ÷ 0.40 × 4.0 = 2.6 mg L⁻¹. 6.2 mg L⁻¹ comes from inverting the ratio.

**A825.** D. set absorbance to zero, so that any absorbance by the solvent and cuvette is not counted
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-3 -->

Explanation: The blank sets the baseline (A = 0), so measured absorbances come only from the analyte. It does not set the wavelength or measure a concentration.

**A826.** C. dilute the sample by a known factor, remeasure, and multiply by the dilution factor
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-4 -->

Explanation: Results are only reliable within the range of the standards, because the absorbance–concentration relationship may stop being linear. Diluting by a known factor brings the reading within range. Extrapolation assumes linearity that may not hold.

**A827.** B. electrons to move to higher energy levels
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-5 -->

Explanation: UV and visible photons have the right energy to promote electrons to higher energy levels. Bond vibrations are caused by infrared radiation, and fragmentation into ions describes mass spectrometry.

**A828.** The calibration is linear with slope 0.11 ÷ 2.0 = 0.055 per mg L⁻¹; diluted sample c = 0.15 ÷ 0.055 = 2.7 mg L⁻¹; dilution factor 50.0 ÷ 5.0 = 10, so original = 27 mg L⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-6 -->

Explanation:

**A829.** At λmax the absorbance changes most for a given change in concentration, giving the greatest sensitivity and the lowest detection limit; the absorption peak is flattest there, so small shifts in wavelength have little effect on readings, which improves precision (it may also reduce interference from other species).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-7 -->

Explanation:

**A830.** Absorbance depends on the instrument, wavelength, cuvette path length and the analyte, so the relationship between absorbance and concentration must be found under the same conditions; the curve converts sample absorbance into concentration; the relationship is only known to be linear over the range measured, and at high concentrations it may stop being linear, so extrapolating beyond the standards is unreliable.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-8 -->

Explanation:

**A831.** B. 400–700 nm
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-9 -->

Explanation: Visible light spans roughly 400–700 nm. Wavelengths below 400 nm are ultraviolet, and those above 700 nm are infrared.

**A832.** A. 0.40
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-10 -->

Explanation: Diluting by a factor of 2 halves the concentration, and absorbance is proportional to concentration, so A = 0.40. 1.6 comes from multiplying instead of dividing by the dilution factor.

**A833.** C. It has no units
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-11 -->

Explanation: Absorbance is a logarithm of a ratio of light intensities, so it is dimensionless. Wavelength is measured in nm, which is a different quantity.

**A834.** D. Quartz is transparent to UV radiation, whereas glass and most plastics absorb it
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-12 -->

Explanation: Glass and most plastics absorb UV radiation, so they would block the beam and give false absorbances. Quartz transmits UV and visible light.

**A835.** D. A spectrophotometer can select any wavelength across the UV and visible range, whereas a colorimeter uses a few fixed filters in the visible range
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-13 -->

Explanation: A spectrophotometer uses a monochromator to select any wavelength, including UV, while a colorimeter typically uses filters or LEDs for a few visible wavelengths. Both measure absorbance and both use calibration curves.

**A836.** B. Absorbance on the y-axis and concentration of the standards on the x-axis
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-14 -->

Explanation: Absorbance (measured response) is plotted against the known concentration of the standards. Absorbance against wavelength is an absorption spectrum, not a calibration curve.

**A837.** C. Zn²⁺ (colourless)
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-15 -->

Explanation: Zn²⁺(aq) does not absorb visible light, so a reagent must be added to make a coloured complex. The other ions already absorb strongly in the visible region.

**A838.** A. the sample absorbs no more light at that wavelength than the blank does
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-16 -->

Explanation: An absorbance of 0 means the same intensity is transmitted as for the blank, so the analyte absorbs no measurable light. Total absorption would give a very large absorbance, not zero.

**A839.** D. Absorbance against wavelength
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-17 -->

Explanation: An absorption spectrum shows how absorbance varies with wavelength, and the peak gives λmax. Absorbance against concentration is a calibration curve.

**A840.** B. It will be too high, because the measured absorbance includes the other species
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-18 -->

Explanation: Absorbances from different species at the same wavelength add together, so the reading is higher than the analyte alone would give. This overestimates the concentration.

**A841.** C. about double the true value
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-19 -->

Explanation: Absorbance is proportional to path length, so doubling the path doubles the absorbance and the concentration read from the curve. Halving would occur only if the path were shorter.

**A842.** A. Fingerprints on the clear faces absorb and scatter light, giving a falsely high absorbance
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-20 -->

Explanation: Marks, smudges and fingerprints on the clear faces scatter and absorb the beam, adding to the absorbance. They do not change the chemistry of the analyte.

**A843.** D. shorter wavelength and higher energy per photon
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-21 -->

Explanation: UV radiation has wavelengths below about 400 nm, and energy is inversely related to wavelength, so each photon carries more energy. Higher energy is why UV can excite electrons in many colourless compounds.

**A844.** Use c₁V₁ = c₂V₂: V₁ = 20 × 100.0 ÷ 100 = 20 mL of stock required [1]. Measure 20.0 mL of the stock accurately using a pipette (or burette) and transfer it to a 100.0 mL volumetric flask [1]. Add deionised water up to the mark, stopper and invert several times to mix thoroughly [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-22 -->

Explanation:

**A845.** The calibration is linear through the origin with a slope of 0.24 per mmol L⁻¹ (0.12 ÷ 0.50) [1]. c = 0.30 ÷ 0.24 = 1.25 mmol L⁻¹ [1]. n = 1.25 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹ × 0.0250 L = 3.1 × 10⁻⁵ mol [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-23 -->

Explanation:

**A846.** The reagent reacts with the analyte to form a coloured species that absorbs in the visible region, so absorbance can be measured [1]. The reagent is added in excess/fixed amounts so that all the analyte reacts and the colour intensity (absorbance) is proportional to the analyte concentration [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-24 -->

Explanation:

**A847.** The absorbance is no longer proportional to concentration at high concentrations, so the calibration is unreliable beyond the linear range and concentrations cannot be read accurately [1]. The sample should be diluted by a known factor so that its absorbance falls within the linear range, then the dilution factor is applied to the result [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-25 -->

Explanation:

**A848.** Concentration of diluted solution = 0.48 ÷ 0.040 = 12 mg L⁻¹ [1]. Dilution factor = 100.0 ÷ 1.00 = 100, so the 250.0 mL solution is 1200 mg L⁻¹ [1]. Mass of Cu = 1200 mg L⁻¹ × 0.2500 L = 300 mg [1]. % Cu = 300 ÷ 500 × 100 = 60% [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-26 -->

Explanation:

**A849.** Rinse the cuvette with a small amount of the solution to be measured, and avoid air bubbles in the light path [1]. Wipe the clear faces clean and handle only by the frosted sides; place the cuvette in the same orientation each time [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-27 -->

Explanation:

**A850.** White light contains all visible wavelengths, and the solution absorbs light of some wavelengths (mainly green, around 530 nm) as electrons are promoted to higher energy levels [1]. The remaining wavelengths (red and blue/violet) are transmitted [1]. The transmitted light combines to give the complementary colour, so the solution appears purple [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-28 -->

Explanation:

**A851.** Absorbance is directly proportional to the concentration of the absorbing species [1]. Absorbance is also directly proportional to the path length of the cuvette [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Greenhouse gases and ocean acidification

**A852.** C. Nitrogen, N₂
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-1 -->

Explanation: N₂ is a symmetrical diatomic molecule that does not absorb infrared radiation, so it is not a greenhouse gas. Water vapour is the most abundant greenhouse gas.

**A853.** B. absorb infrared radiation emitted from Earth's surface and re-emit some of it back towards the surface
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-2 -->

Explanation: Earth's surface emits infrared radiation; greenhouse gases absorb it and re-radiate energy in all directions, including back down. UV absorption is mainly due to ozone, and ozone depletion is a separate issue.

**A854.** D. It decreases, because the extra H₃O⁺ reacts with CO₃²⁻ to form HCO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-3 -->

Explanation: Dissolved CO₂ forms H₂CO₃, which donates protons; the extra H₃O⁺ reacts with carbonate (H₃O⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → HCO₃⁻ + H₂O), lowering [CO₃²⁻]. Buffering slows the pH change but does not stop carbonate being used up.

**A855.** A. Organisms that build calcium carbonate shells or skeletons, such as corals and molluscs
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-4 -->

Explanation: Lower carbonate ion concentration makes it harder to form CaCO₃ and can dissolve existing structures, which threatens reef-building corals (e.g. on the Great Barrier Reef), molluscs and some plankton. The other groups are affected only indirectly.

**A856.** C. 26 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-5 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ, so the ratio is 10⁻⁸·¹ ÷ 10⁻⁸·² = 10⁰·¹ ≈ 1.26, an increase of about 26 %. pH is logarithmic, so a 0.1 drop is not a 1 % change.

**A857.** CO₂(g) ⇌ CO₂(aq); CO₂(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ H₂CO₃(aq); H₂CO₃(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq), and the rise in [H₃O⁺] lowers the pH (some HCO₃⁻ also ionises further to CO₃²⁻ and H₃O⁺).
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-6 -->

Explanation:

**A858.** Shells form by Ca²⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → CaCO₃(s), which needs a good supply of carbonate ions; extra H₃O⁺ from dissolved CO₂ converts carbonate to hydrogencarbonate (H₃O⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₂O(l)), so less CO₃²⁻ is available and shell building costs organisms more energy; at lower pH existing shells can dissolve: CaCO₃(s) + H₃O⁺(aq) → Ca²⁺(aq) + HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₂O(l).
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-7 -->

Explanation:

**A859.** The natural greenhouse effect is warming from naturally occurring greenhouse gases that keeps Earth's average temperature about 33 °C warmer than it would be otherwise; the enhanced greenhouse effect is the additional warming caused by human activities (fossil fuel combustion, land clearing, agriculture) raising greenhouse gas concentrations; methane absorbs infrared radiation far more effectively per molecule than CO₂ (a global warming potential of roughly 25–30 times CO₂ over 100 years), so emissions from livestock, landfill and gas leaks have a large effect.
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-8 -->

Explanation:

**A860.** A. −18 °C
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-9 -->

Explanation: Greenhouse gases raise the average from about −18 °C to about 15 °C. 15 °C is the actual present average, not the value without the effect.

**A861.** C. combustion of fossil fuels and land clearing
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-10 -->

Explanation: Burning coal, oil and gas and clearing forests release CO₂ faster than natural sinks remove it. Photosynthesis removes CO₂, so more of it would lower, not raise, the concentration.

**A862.** D. 44.0 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-11 -->

Explanation: n(CH₄) = 16.0 ÷ 16.0 = 1.00 mol, the mole ratio is 1 : 1, so n(CO₂) = 1.00 mol and m = 1.00 × 44.0 = 44.0 g. Giving 16.0 g wrongly assumes mass is conserved between methane and CO₂.

**A863.** A. 176 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-12 -->

Explanation: n(C₈H₁₈) = 57.0 ÷ 114.0 = 0.500 mol; the ratio is 2 : 16, so n(CO₂) = 4.00 mol and m = 4.00 × 44.0 = 176 g. Forgetting the 8 : 1 ratio gives only 0.500 mol of CO₂ (22.0 g).

**A864.** B. 7.9 × 10⁻⁹ M
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-13 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻⁸·¹ = 7.9 × 10⁻⁹ M. The pH value is a negative logarithm, so it cannot be written directly as the exponent coefficient.

**A865.** B. pH is falling, so seawater is becoming less basic (moving towards neutral)
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-14 -->

Explanation: Seawater is still slightly basic (pH above 7), but its pH is decreasing as [H₃O⁺] rises. Dissolved CO₂ forms only a weak acid, and the ocean has not become acidic.

**A866.** A. more CO₂ dissolves in seawater, because the system partly opposes the increase in CO₂(g)
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-15 -->

Explanation: By Le Chatelier's principle the system shifts right to consume some added CO₂(g). K depends only on temperature, so it does not change.

**A867.** B. decreases as temperature rises
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-16 -->

Explanation: Gas solubility falls as temperature rises, so warming oceans can absorb less CO₂. Saying it is independent of temperature ignores the endothermic loss of gas from solution on heating.

**A868.** C. Use of nitrogen-based fertilisers on farmland
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-17 -->

Explanation: Microbial processes in soil acting on nitrogen fertilisers release N₂O. Livestock and rice paddies are mainly methane sources, and coal combustion mainly releases CO₂.

**A869.** D. increases, because carbonic acid ionises and carbonate reacts with H₃O⁺
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-18 -->

Explanation: Both H₂CO₃ ionising and CO₃²⁻ reacting with H₃O⁺ produce HCO₃⁻, so [HCO₃⁻] rises while [CO₃²⁻] falls. The shift is in the opposite direction to the carbonate change.

**A870.** A. 12.4 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-19 -->

Explanation: n = 22.0 ÷ 44.0 = 0.500 mol, so V = 0.500 × 24.8 = 12.4 L. Using 22.0 as the number of litres confuses mass with moles.

**A871.** C. the surface is much cooler than the Sun
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-20 -->

Explanation: The wavelengths emitted by an object depend on its temperature, so the cool Earth emits infrared while the hot Sun emits mostly visible light. Greenhouse gases absorb that infrared rather than creating it.

**A872.** D. 56 t
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-21 -->

Explanation: CO₂-e = 2.0 × 28 = 56 t. Dividing by 28 reverses the relationship and gives 0.071 t.

**A873.** [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ, so initial [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻⁸·² = 6.3 × 10⁻⁹ M [1]; final [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻⁷·⁹ = 1.3 × 10⁻⁸ M [1]; the ratio is 10⁰·³ ≈ 2.0, so [H₃O⁺] doubles [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-22 -->

Explanation:

**A874.** CO₂: burning fossil fuels (or land clearing) [1]; CH₄: livestock digestion, landfill or rice paddies (or gas leaks) [1]; N₂O: use of nitrogen-based fertilisers (or some industrial processes) [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-23 -->

Explanation:

**A875.** C₃H₈(g) + 5O₂(g) → 3CO₂(g) + 4H₂O(l) [1]; n(C₃H₈) = 5.00 ÷ 44.0 = 0.114 mol [1]; n(CO₂) = 3 × 0.1136 = 0.341 mol [1]; V(CO₂) = 0.341 × 24.8 = 8.45 L [1].
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-24 -->

Explanation:

## VCE Units 3 & 4

### Fuels & thermochemistry

#### Fossil fuels and biofuels

**A876.** A. Biogas produced by anaerobic digestion of food waste
<!-- id: chem-1-1-1 -->

Explanation: Biogas comes from biomass that can be replaced within a human lifetime; the others are fossil fuels formed over millions of years.

**A877.** B. C₆H₁₂O₆(aq) → 2C₂H₅OH(aq) + 2CO₂(g)
<!-- id: chem-1-1-2 -->

Explanation: Anaerobic fermentation converts glucose into ethanol and carbon dioxide; the second equation is cellular respiration and the others are unbalanced.

**A878.** C. reacting a triglyceride with methanol in the presence of a strong base catalyst
<!-- id: chem-1-1-3 -->

Explanation: Transesterification of plant oils or animal fats with methanol, catalysed by KOH or NaOH, produces fatty acid methyl esters (biodiesel).

**A879.** Fossil fuels are burnt to power farm machinery, make fertilisers, transport the crop and heat the distillation process, so the total CO₂ released exceeds the CO₂ absorbed by the crop during photosynthesis.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-4 -->

Explanation: Award marks for identifying at least one fossil-fuel energy input in the production chain and linking it to net CO₂ emissions.

**A880.** D. Glycerol
<!-- id: chem-1-1-5 -->

Explanation: Each triglyceride releases its glycerol backbone (propane-1,2,3-triol) as the fatty acid chains are converted into methyl esters.

**A881.** A. methane
<!-- id: chem-1-1-6 -->

Explanation: Biogas is typically 50–70% methane, with most of the remainder being carbon dioxide.

**A882.** B. are generally larger and contain polar ester groups, giving stronger intermolecular forces
<!-- id: chem-1-1-7 -->

Explanation: Long-chain fatty acid methyl esters have strong dispersion forces plus dipole–dipole attractions from the ester group; esters have no O–H so cannot hydrogen bond to each other.

**A883.** Fatty acid methyl esters (biodiesel) and glycerol; the methyl esters are the biodiesel fuel.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-8 -->

Explanation: Glycerol is a by-product; both products must be named.

**A884.** C. the oxygen atoms in its ester groups can form hydrogen bonds with water molecules
<!-- id: chem-1-1-9 -->

Explanation: The lone pairs on ester oxygen atoms accept hydrogen bonds from water, making biodiesel hygroscopic; petrodiesel alkanes cannot do this.

**A885.** D. the CO₂ released when it burns is approximately equal to the CO₂ absorbed by the crop during photosynthesis
<!-- id: chem-1-1-10 -->

Explanation: In the idealised model, CO₂ taken up by photosynthesis balances the CO₂ released in fermentation and combustion.

**A886.** A. It formed from plant material compressed and heated over millions of years
<!-- id: chem-1-1-11 -->

Explanation: Coal is a fossil fuel formed from ancient plant matter under heat and pressure over geological time, so it is non-renewable.

**A887.** Biodiesel contains ester functional groups whose oxygen atoms can accept hydrogen bonds from water molecules, so it attracts and absorbs water. Petrodiesel consists of non-polar hydrocarbons that cannot hydrogen bond with water.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-12 -->

Explanation: Link the ester oxygen atoms to hydrogen bonding with water; mention that alkanes cannot.

**A888.** B. Liquefied petroleum gas (LPG)
<!-- id: chem-1-1-13 -->

Explanation: LPG (mainly propane and butane) is obtained from natural gas processing and crude oil refining, both fossil sources.

**A889.** C. Wheat straw left after harvest
<!-- id: chem-1-1-14 -->

Explanation: Crop residues like straw are lignocellulosic waste not used as human food, so using them avoids the food-versus-fuel conflict.

**A890.** D. a lower energy content per gram
<!-- id: chem-1-1-15 -->

Explanation: Biodiesel molecules are already partly oxidised (they contain oxygen), so they release less energy per gram (about 37–38 kJ g⁻¹ compared with about 45 kJ g⁻¹).

**A891.** Advantage: ethanol is renewable and can lower net greenhouse emissions (it also burns more cleanly). Disadvantage: ethanol releases less energy per gram than petrol, so fuel consumption increases (or it absorbs water, or crops compete with food).
<!-- id: chem-1-1-16 -->

Explanation: Any valid advantage and any valid disadvantage, each clearly explained.

**A892.** A. they form over millions of years, far more slowly than they are consumed
<!-- id: chem-1-1-17 -->

Explanation: Renewability depends on the rate of replenishment; crude oil cannot be replaced within a human timescale.

**A893.** B. Yeast, anaerobic conditions, about 30–40 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-1-18 -->

Explanation: Yeast enzymes ferment sugars in the absence of oxygen; temperatures that are too high denature the enzymes.

**A894.** C. fractional distillation
<!-- id: chem-1-1-19 -->

Explanation: Ethanol (b.p. 78 °C) and water (b.p. 100 °C) are miscible liquids with different boiling points, so they are separated by fractional distillation.

**A895.** A renewable fuel can be replaced by natural processes within a relatively short (human) timescale, e.g. biogas from a Gippsland dairy farm. A non-renewable fuel is used faster than it can be replaced, taking millions of years to form, e.g. Latrobe Valley brown coal.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-20 -->

Explanation: The key idea is the rate of replenishment compared with the rate of use.
