**A1568.** D. methyl orange, because the equivalence point is acidic
<!-- id: chem-6-4-18 -->

Explanation: The ammonium ion formed is a weak acid, so the equivalence point pH is below 7. Methyl orange changes colour in an acidic range.

**A1569.** c = (4200 ÷ 3000) × 20.0 = 28.0 mg/L
<!-- id: chem-6-4-19 -->

Explanation: Because the calibration is linear through the origin, concentration scales directly with peak area.

**A1570.** n(NaOH) = 0.1000 × 0.02150 = 2.150 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CH₃COOH). Diluted c = 2.150 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02500 = 0.0860 M; original c = 0.0860 × 10 = 0.860 M. Mass in 100 mL = 0.0860 mol × 60.05 = 5.16 g, so 5.16% (w/v).
<!-- id: chem-6-4-20 -->

Explanation: Check the 1:1 ratio, the dilution factor of 10, and conversion to grams per 100 mL.

**A1571.** Solid NaOH is hygroscopic (it absorbs water from the air) and reacts with atmospheric CO₂. Its mass therefore does not reflect the true amount of NaOH, so a solution of accurately known concentration cannot be made by weighing it.
<!-- id: chem-6-4-21 -->

Explanation: Mention absorption of water and/or CO₂.

**A1572.** Each compound has a different balance of attraction to the stationary phase and solubility in the mobile phase (for example, due to differences in polarity). The compound more strongly adsorbed to the stationary phase moves more slowly and has a longer retention time.
<!-- id: chem-6-4-22 -->

Explanation: Answers must refer to both stationary and mobile phase interactions.

**A1573.** The water left in the pipette dilutes the aliquot, so it contains fewer moles of analyte. The titre is smaller, and the calculated concentration is lower than the true value.
<!-- id: chem-6-4-23 -->

Explanation: Link fewer moles to a smaller titre and an underestimated result.

**A1574.** n(MnO₄⁻) = 0.0150 × 0.01240 = 1.86 × 10⁻⁴ mol; n(Fe²⁺) = 5 × 1.86 × 10⁻⁴ = 9.30 × 10⁻⁴ mol; m = 9.30 × 10⁻⁴ × 55.85 = 0.0519 g = 51.9 mg.
<!-- id: chem-6-4-24 -->

Explanation: The 1 : 5 mole ratio is the key step; KMnO₄ acts as its own indicator (the first permanent pink colour).

**A1575.** Small particles give the stationary phase a very large surface area, which improves separation and resolution. They also pack tightly and resist flow, so high pressure is needed to push the mobile phase through the column at a useful rate.
<!-- id: chem-6-4-25 -->

Explanation: Credit both the surface area/resolution idea and the pressure requirement.

**A1576.** B. 0.45
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-1 -->

Explanation: Rf = distance travelled by component ÷ distance travelled by solvent front = 3.6 ÷ 8.0 = 0.45. Rf values must lie between 0 and 1, which rules out 2.2.

**A1577.** C. ethanoic acid
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-2 -->

Explanation: Ethanoic acid is the most polar (small, with a highly polar COOH group), so it is least attracted to the non-polar stationary phase and most soluble in the polar mobile phase. It therefore elutes first. The longer, less polar molecules adsorb more strongly and are retained longer.

**A1578.** C. Components separate because of differences in their relative attraction to the stationary and mobile phases
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-3 -->

Explanation: Separation depends on the balance between adsorption to the stationary phase and solubility in (desorption into) the mobile phase. Strongly adsorbed components move more slowly. The relative polarity of the phases can be chosen either way.

**A1579.** C. 500 mg L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-4 -->

Explanation: The calibration is linear at 75 area units per mg L⁻¹, so an area of 3750 corresponds to 50 mg L⁻¹ in the diluted sample. Multiplying by the dilution factor of 10 gives 500 mg L⁻¹ in the original cola. 50 mg L⁻¹ forgets the dilution.

**A1580.** A. decreases, because the compound is now more soluble in the mobile phase
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-5 -->

Explanation: A less polar mobile phase dissolves a non-polar compound better, so it spends more time in the mobile phase and less adsorbed on the non-polar stationary phase, eluting sooner. The stationary phase itself is unchanged.

**A1581.** (a) Retention time is used for qualitative identification: a component is identified by comparing its retention time with that of a known standard run under identical conditions (same column, mobile phase, flow rate, temperature); (b) peak area is proportional to the amount/concentration of the component; it is used quantitatively by comparing with a calibration curve made from standards of known concentration.
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1582.** Different compounds can have the same (or very similar) retention time under a given set of conditions, so a match is not proof of identity; confirmation could be obtained by spiking the sample with the standard and checking that only the single peak increases (no new peak), by repeating with a different column or mobile phase, or by coupling HPLC to a mass spectrometer.
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1583.** Concentration in original drink = 12.4 × (50.0 ÷ 10.0) = 62.0 mg L⁻¹; mass in 0.600 L = 62.0 × 0.600 = 37.2 mg.
<!-- id: chem34a3-chr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1584.** C. 0.875 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-1 -->

Explanation: n(NaOH) = 0.100 × 0.01750 = 1.750 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CH₃COOH) in 20.00 mL. Concentration of diluted solution = 1.750 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.0875 mol L⁻¹. The vinegar was diluted 10-fold, so the original is 0.875 mol L⁻¹. 0.0875 mol L⁻¹ ignores the dilution.

**A1585.** C. phenolphthalein (colour change pH 8.3–10.0)
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-2 -->

Explanation: At equivalence the solution contains ethanoate ions, which are weakly basic, so the equivalence point pH is above 7 (about 8–9). Phenolphthalein changes colour in this range. The other indicators would change colour before equivalence is reached.

**A1586.** D. 1.14 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-3 -->

Explanation: n(Cr₂O₇²⁻) = 0.0500 × 0.01520 = 7.60 × 10⁻⁴ mol; n(ethanol) = 3/2 × 7.60 × 10⁻⁴ = 1.14 × 10⁻³ mol in the aliquot, so 1.14 × 10⁻² mol in 100.0 mL, which came from 10.00 mL of wine: 1.14 × 10⁻² ÷ 0.01000 = 1.14 mol L⁻¹. 0.507 uses the inverted ratio 2/3.

**A1587.** A. too high, because the titrant is diluted and a larger titre is needed
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-4 -->

Explanation: Residual water dilutes the titrant, so more volume is needed to deliver the required number of moles. Calculations use the labelled (higher) concentration, so the moles of titrant and hence analyte are overestimated.

**A1588.** B. starch; the first permanent blue-black colour
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-5 -->

Explanation: While vitamin C is present, added iodine is reduced to colourless iodide. Once all the vitamin C has reacted, excess I₂ forms a blue-black complex with starch, marking the endpoint. Acid–base indicators are not suited to this redox titration.

**A1589.** n(I₂) = 0.0250 × 0.02270 = 5.675 × 10⁻⁴ mol = n(vitamin C) in 20.00 mL; n in 100.0 mL = 5.675 × 10⁻⁴ × (100.0 ÷ 20.00) = 2.838 × 10⁻³ mol; m = 2.838 × 10⁻³ × 176.0 = 0.499 g (3 significant figures, limited by 0.0250 mol L⁻¹).
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1590.** Concordant titres are titres that agree closely with each other, typically within 0.10 mL; averaging only concordant titres excludes the rough/trial titre and any outliers, which reduces the effect of random errors and improves the precision (and reliability) of the result.
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1591.** n(NaOH) = 0.115 × 0.02465 = 2.835 × 10⁻³ mol = n(acid) (1 : 1 for a monoprotic acid); M = 0.250 ÷ 2.835 × 10⁻³ = 88.2 g mol⁻¹; this matches butanoic acid, CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH (88.0 g mol⁻¹).
<!-- id: chem34a3-vol-8 -->

Explanation:

### Food chemistry

#### Proteins and amino acids

**A1592.** A. Amino and carboxyl
<!-- id: chem-7-1-1 -->

Explanation: Each 2-amino acid has an amino (–NH₂) group and a carboxyl (–COOH) group bonded to the same carbon atom.

**A1593.** B. peptide (amide) bond
<!-- id: chem-7-1-2 -->

Explanation: The –COOH of one amino acid reacts with the –NH₂ of another, releasing water and forming a –CONH– amide (peptide) link.

**A1594.** C. 149
<!-- id: chem-7-1-3 -->

Explanation: Each peptide bond releases one water molecule and a chain of n residues has n − 1 peptide bonds, so 149.

**A1595.** D. 132.0 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-1-4 -->

Explanation: 2 × 75.0 − 18.0 (one water lost in condensation) = 132.0 g mol⁻¹.

**A1596.** A. Primary
<!-- id: chem-7-1-5 -->

Explanation: Primary structure is the order of amino acid residues, held together by covalent peptide bonds.

**A1597.** B. hydrogen bonds between C=O and N–H groups of the polypeptide backbone
<!-- id: chem-7-1-6 -->

Explanation: Secondary structure results from regular hydrogen bonding between backbone carbonyl oxygen and amide hydrogen atoms.

**A1598.** C. Cysteine
<!-- id: chem-7-1-7 -->

Explanation: Cysteine has a –CH₂SH side chain; two –SH groups can be oxidised to form a covalent –S–S– bridge.

**A1599.** D. The association of two or more polypeptide chains into one functional protein
<!-- id: chem-7-1-8 -->

Explanation: Quaternary structure exists only in proteins made of more than one polypeptide subunit, such as haemoglobin with four chains.

**A1600.** A. Peptide bonds
<!-- id: chem-7-1-9 -->

Explanation: Denaturation disrupts secondary, tertiary and quaternary structure, but the covalent peptide bonds of the primary structure are not broken.

**A1601.** B. Glycine
<!-- id: chem-7-1-10 -->

Explanation: Glycine's side chain is H, so its central carbon has two identical H atoms and is not a stereocentre.

**A1602.** C. cannot be made by the body in sufficient amounts and must be obtained from the diet
<!-- id: chem-7-1-11 -->

Explanation: Humans cannot synthesise nine amino acids (or enough of them), so these must come from dietary protein.

**A1603.** D. an ionic interaction between –NH₃⁺ and –COO⁻
<!-- id: chem-7-1-12 -->

Explanation: At pH 7.4 lysine's amino group is protonated and aspartic acid's carboxyl is deprotonated, so the oppositely charged groups attract (a salt bridge).

**A1604.** A. –NH₃⁺
<!-- id: chem-7-1-13 -->

Explanation: The amino group is basic, so at low pH it accepts H⁺ to become –NH₃⁺.

**A1605.** B. 2
<!-- id: chem-7-1-14 -->

Explanation: Gly-Ala and Ala-Gly are different because the free amino (N-terminal) and carboxyl (C-terminal) ends differ.

**A1606.** C. 6
<!-- id: chem-7-1-15 -->

Explanation: The three amino acids can be arranged in 3 × 2 × 1 = 6 different sequences.

**A1607.** D. amino acids
<!-- id: chem-7-1-16 -->

Explanation: Hydrolysis breaks every peptide bond, releasing the individual amino acid monomers.

**A1608.** A. in the interior of the protein, interacting with other non-polar side chains via dispersion forces
<!-- id: chem-7-1-17 -->

Explanation: Hydrophobic side chains are buried away from water in the core of globular proteins, held by dispersion forces.

**A1609.** B. It has a free amino group at one end and a free carboxyl group at the other end
<!-- id: chem-7-1-18 -->

Explanation: The chain begins with an N-terminal –NH₂ and ends with a C-terminal –COOH because each internal pair has been used in a peptide bond.

**A1610.** H₂NCH₂CONHCH(CH₃)COOH (water is also produced).
<!-- id: chem-7-1-19 -->

Explanation: Glycine's –COOH and alanine's –NH₂ combine to form the –CONH– peptide link, leaving glycine's amino group and alanine's carboxyl group free.

**A1611.** The extra kinetic energy disrupts the hydrogen bonds, ionic interactions and dispersion forces that maintain the secondary and tertiary structure, so the protein unfolds (denatures). Its specific 3D shape (e.g. active site) is lost, so it can no longer function, even though the peptide bonds remain.
<!-- id: chem-7-1-20 -->

Explanation: Mark for: weak bonds/interactions broken, change in 3D shape, primary structure unchanged.

**A1612.** Secondary structure is the local folding of the backbone into α-helices or β-pleated sheets, held by hydrogen bonds between backbone C=O and N–H groups. Tertiary structure is the overall 3D folding of the whole chain, held by interactions between side chains (hydrogen bonds, ionic interactions, dispersion forces and covalent disulfide bridges).
<!-- id: chem-7-1-21 -->

Explanation: Key distinction: backbone interactions (secondary) versus side-chain (R group) interactions (tertiary).

**A1613.** C₃H₇NO₂ (alanine).
<!-- id: chem-7-1-22 -->

Explanation: Dipeptide + H₂O = C₅H₁₂N₂O₄; subtracting glycine C₂H₅NO₂ leaves C₃H₇NO₂.

**A1614.** Many individual plant proteins are low in one or more essential amino acids (e.g. grains are low in lysine, legumes in methionine). Combining complementary proteins ensures all essential amino acids, which the body cannot synthesise, are obtained in sufficient amounts.
<!-- id: chem-7-1-23 -->

Explanation: Must link variety to obtaining all essential amino acids.

**A1615.** Lowering the pH protonates –COO⁻ side chains to –COOH, changing the charges on R groups. This disrupts the ionic interactions and hydrogen bonds that hold the tertiary structure, so the proteins unfold and clump together (coagulate).
<!-- id: chem-7-1-24 -->

Explanation: Award marks for change in charge on side chains and disruption of tertiary structure.

**A1616.** 900 g
<!-- id: chem-7-1-25 -->

Explanation: 51 residues have 50 peptide bonds, so 50 mol H₂O × 18.0 g mol⁻¹ = 900 g.

**A1617.** C. H₃N⁺CH₂COOH
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-1 -->

Explanation: At low pH there is a high concentration of H⁺, so both the amine group (protonated to –NH₃⁺) and the carboxyl group (remaining –COOH) are protonated. The zwitterion H₃N⁺CH₂COO⁻ predominates near the isoelectric point, and H₂NCH₂COO⁻ at high pH.

**A1618.** B. hydrogen bonds between C=O and N–H groups of the polypeptide backbone
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-2 -->

Explanation: Secondary structure results from hydrogen bonding between backbone carbonyl oxygens and amide hydrogens. Peptide bonds define the primary structure; disulfide bridges and ionic interactions between R groups contribute to tertiary structure.

**A1619.** A. primary
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-3 -->

Explanation: Denaturation disrupts the weaker interactions (hydrogen bonds, ionic interactions, dispersion forces) that maintain secondary, tertiary and quaternary structure. The covalent peptide bonds of the primary structure remain intact.

**A1620.** B. providing an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-4 -->

Explanation: Enzymes are biological catalysts: they provide an alternative pathway with lower activation energy, so a greater proportion of collisions are successful. They do not change ΔH or the position of equilibrium.

**A1621.** B. 6
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-5 -->

Explanation: Each sequence is a different primary structure, and a tripeptide has distinct N-terminal and C-terminal ends. The number of arrangements of three different units is 3 × 2 × 1 = 6. 27 would apply if each amino acid could be used more than once.

**A1622.** Changing the pH alters the concentration of H⁺, which changes the charges on acidic and basic R groups (e.g. –COOH/–COO⁻ and –NH₂/–NH₃⁺) in the enzyme; this disrupts ionic interactions and hydrogen bonds that maintain the tertiary structure, changing the shape of the active site; the substrate can no longer bind as effectively (and charged groups involved in catalysis may be altered), so activity decreases; large changes cause denaturation.
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1623.** A coenzyme is a non-protein organic molecule that binds to an enzyme (often at or near the active site) and is required for the enzyme to catalyse its reaction, frequently by carrying electrons, atoms or groups; without enough of the vitamin, the coenzyme cannot be made, so the enzymes that depend on it are less active and those reactions proceed more slowly.
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1624.** H₂NCH₂CONHCH(CH₃)COOH, i.e. glycine is at the N-terminal end: the –COOH of glycine and the –NH₂ of alanine join to form –CO–NH–; it is a condensation reaction releasing one H₂O molecule; the bond formed is a peptide (amide) bond.
<!-- id: chem34a3-prot-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Carbohydrates, fats and oils

**A1625.** C. structural isomers
<!-- id: chem-7-2-1 -->

Explanation: They have the same molecular formula but different arrangements of atoms (glucose is an aldose, fructose a ketose).

**A1626.** D. glucose and fructose
<!-- id: chem-7-2-2 -->

Explanation: Sucrose is a disaccharide of one glucose and one fructose unit.

**A1627.** A. glucose and galactose
<!-- id: chem-7-2-3 -->

Explanation: Lactose hydrolyses into glucose and galactose.

**A1628.** B. 342.0 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-2-4 -->

Explanation: 2 × 180.0 − 18.0 (one water released) = 342.0 g mol⁻¹.

**A1629.** C. glycosidic (ether) linkage
<!-- id: chem-7-2-5 -->

Explanation: Condensation between two –OH groups forms a C–O–C glycosidic link.

**A1630.** D. Glycogen
<!-- id: chem-7-2-6 -->

Explanation: Glycogen is a highly branched polymer of glucose stored in animal liver and muscle tissue.

**A1631.** A. they lack an enzyme that can hydrolyse the β-glycosidic links between its glucose units
<!-- id: chem-7-2-7 -->

Explanation: Human amylases only hydrolyse α-glycosidic links; we have no cellulase for the β links in cellulose.

**A1632.** B. is branched, so it is hydrolysed more quickly and tends to give a higher glycaemic index
<!-- id: chem-7-2-8 -->

Explanation: Branching gives many chain ends for amylase to attack, so glucose is released faster.

**A1633.** C. causes a rapid rise in blood glucose level after it is eaten
<!-- id: chem-7-2-9 -->

Explanation: GI measures how quickly a carbohydrate food raises blood glucose; high-GI foods are digested and absorbed rapidly.

**A1634.** D. glycerol and three fatty acids
<!-- id: chem-7-2-10 -->

Explanation: Each of glycerol's three –OH groups reacts with the –COOH of a fatty acid.

**A1635.** A. Ester
<!-- id: chem-7-2-11 -->

Explanation: A carboxylic acid reacting with an alcohol forms an ester (–COO–) group.

**A1636.** B. 2
<!-- id: chem-7-2-12 -->

Explanation: A saturated C₁₈ fatty acid is C₁₈H₃₆O₂; it has 4 fewer H atoms, so 2 C=C double bonds.

**A1637.** C. straight saturated chains pack closely together, giving stronger dispersion forces between molecules
<!-- id: chem-7-2-13 -->

Explanation: Cis double bonds in oleic acid put kinks in the chains, preventing close packing and weakening dispersion forces.

**A1638.** D. the first C=C double bond is on the third carbon from the methyl (–CH₃) end of the chain
<!-- id: chem-7-2-14 -->

Explanation: The omega numbering counts from the methyl end, so omega-3 means the first double bond starts at carbon 3 from that end.

**A1639.** A. reaction of oxygen at C=C double bonds in unsaturated fatty acid chains
<!-- id: chem-7-2-15 -->

Explanation: Oxygen attacks the C=C double bonds, producing smaller volatile compounds such as aldehydes that smell and taste rancid.

**A1640.** B. C₅₇H₁₁₀O₆
<!-- id: chem-7-2-16 -->

Explanation: C₃H₈O₃ + 3 C₁₈H₃₆O₂ − 3 H₂O = C₅₇H₁₁₀O₆.

**A1641.** C. 6
<!-- id: chem-7-2-17 -->

Explanation: Each linoleic acid chain has 2 C=C bonds, so the triglyceride has 6, and one I₂ adds across each.

**A1642.** D. 3
<!-- id: chem-7-2-18 -->

Explanation: There are three ester links and each hydrolysis uses one water molecule.

**A1643.** Glucose is a small molecule with five –OH groups that form many hydrogen bonds with water. A triglyceride is a large molecule dominated by long non-polar hydrocarbon chains that can only form dispersion forces, so it cannot form enough attractions with water to dissolve.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-19 -->

Explanation: Refer to hydrogen bonding (glucose) versus mainly non-polar chains (triglyceride).

**A1644.** (a) Glucose. (b) Glycerol and three fatty acids.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-20 -->

Explanation: Starch is a polymer of α-glucose; triglycerides are esters of glycerol and fatty acids.

**A1645.** The cis C=C double bonds in unsaturated chains create kinks, so the molecules cannot pack closely. This weakens the dispersion forces between molecules, so less energy is needed to separate them and the melting point is lower.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-21 -->

Explanation: Link structure (kinks) → packing → strength of dispersion forces → melting point.

**A1646.** 36.0 g
<!-- id: chem-7-2-22 -->

Explanation: M(maltose) = 342.0 g mol⁻¹, so n = 0.100 mol; this gives 0.200 mol glucose × 180.0 = 36.0 g.

**A1647.** Antioxidants are preferentially oxidised (they react with oxygen or free radicals more readily than the C=C bonds in the unsaturated fats), so the oils in the nuts are protected from oxidation and rancid-smelling breakdown products form more slowly.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-23 -->

Explanation: The antioxidant is oxidised instead of the unsaturated fatty acid chains.

**A1648.** Both are polymers of α-glucose. Amylose is unbranched (α-1,4 links only), while amylopectin is branched (α-1,4 and α-1,6 links). Foods high in amylose have a lower GI because the unbranched chains coil tightly and are hydrolysed more slowly.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-24 -->

Explanation: Must state branching difference and that high amylose gives lower GI.

**A1649.** 3 C=C double bonds; polyunsaturated.
<!-- id: chem-7-2-25 -->

Explanation: A saturated C₁₈ acid is C₁₈H₃₆O₂; it has 6 fewer H atoms, i.e. 3 C=C, and more than one double bond means polyunsaturated.

**A1650.** C. glucose and fructose
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-1 -->

Explanation: Sucrose is formed by a condensation reaction between glucose and fructose. Maltose is glucose + glucose and lactose is glucose + galactose.

**A1651.** B. 2C₆H₁₂O₆ → C₁₂H₂₂O₁₁ + H₂O
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-2 -->

Explanation: Two glucose molecules join by a condensation reaction forming a glycosidic link and releasing one water molecule, so maltose is C₁₂H₂₂O₁₁.

**A1652.** B. cellulose is made from β-glucose, and humans lack an enzyme that hydrolyses its glycosidic links
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-3 -->

Explanation: Cellulose is a polymer of β-glucose, while starch is made of α-glucose. Human digestive enzymes (amylases) are specific to the α-glycosidic links in starch, and humans do not produce cellulase. Cellulose is insoluble, not too soluble.

**A1653.** C. is branched, with α(1→6) links at branch points as well as α(1→4) links
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-4 -->

Explanation: Amylopectin is branched: chains of α(1→4)-linked glucose with α(1→6) links at branch points. Amylose is the unbranched, helical component. The many chain ends make amylopectin hydrolyse faster, giving a higher glycaemic index.

**A1654.** B. 499
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-5 -->

Explanation: An unbranched chain of 500 units contains 499 glycosidic links. Hydrolysis of each link uses one water molecule, so 499 water molecules are needed.

**A1655.** Glycogen is more highly branched than starch (amylopectin), with α(1→6) branch points occurring more frequently; the many branch ends provide many sites where enzymes can hydrolyse glucose units at the same time, allowing rapid release of glucose when needed.
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1656.** Because aspartame is about 200 times sweeter, only a tiny mass is needed to achieve the same sweetness; although it releases a similar amount of energy per gram to sucrose (it is a dipeptide derivative, around 17 kJ g⁻¹), the very small mass contributes negligible energy to the drink.
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1657.** Amylose is unbranched and coils tightly into a helix, so it has fewer exposed chain ends for amylase enzymes to act on and is hydrolysed more slowly; glucose is therefore released into the bloodstream more slowly, giving a smaller, slower rise in blood glucose (lower GI); a benefit is better blood-glucose control (e.g. for people with diabetes) or feeling full for longer / more sustained energy release.
<!-- id: chem34a3-carb-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1658.** B. three water molecules
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-1 -->

Explanation: Each of the three –OH groups of glycerol reacts with the –COOH of a fatty acid in a condensation reaction forming an ester link and releasing one water molecule, giving three water molecules in total.

**A1659.** B. 2
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-2 -->

Explanation: A saturated C₁₈ fatty acid is C₁₇H₃₅COOH (stearic acid). Linoleic acid has 4 fewer hydrogens, and each C=C removes 2 hydrogens, so it has 2 double bonds.

**A1660.** B. cis-unsaturated fatty acid chains, whose kinks prevent close packing and weaken dispersion forces
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-3 -->

Explanation: Cis double bonds introduce kinks in the hydrocarbon chains, so molecules cannot pack closely. The dispersion forces between them are weaker, giving lower melting points. Longer and saturated chains would raise the melting point.

**A1661.** C. vitamin D
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-4 -->

Explanation: Vitamin D is a largely non-polar molecule with only one –OH group, so it dissolves in fats and is stored in fatty tissue. Vitamin C and the B vitamins have many polar groups capable of hydrogen bonding with water, so they are water-soluble and must be consumed regularly.

**A1662.** C. 9 mol
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-5 -->

Explanation: Linolenic acid has 3 C=C double bonds (C₁₇H₂₉COOH is 6 H short of C₁₇H₃₅COOH). The triglyceride has 3 chains × 3 C=C = 9 double bonds, each requiring 1 mol of H₂.

**A1663.** An omega-3 fatty acid is an unsaturated fatty acid whose first carbon–carbon double bond is located at the third carbon counting from the methyl (omega) end of the chain; it is essential because the human body cannot synthesise it (cannot insert a double bond at that position), so it must be obtained from food (e.g. fish, flaxseed).
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1664.** Oxidative rancidity occurs when oxygen reacts at carbon–carbon double bonds, breaking the chains into smaller, unpleasant-smelling aldehydes, ketones and acids; polyunsaturated oils have more C=C bonds, so they oxidise more readily than saturated fats; manufacturers add antioxidants (e.g. vitamin E/tocopherols), store in airtight, opaque or nitrogen-flushed containers, or refrigerate to slow oxidation.
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1665.** Vitamin C's many –OH groups form hydrogen bonds with water, making it water-soluble; vitamin A is largely non-polar, so it interacts by dispersion forces with fats and is fat-soluble rather than water-soluble; consequence: vitamin C is not stored and excess is excreted, so it must be consumed regularly, whereas vitamin A is stored in body fat/liver and excessive intake can accumulate to toxic levels.
<!-- id: chem34a3-fat-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Enzymes and vitamins

**A1666.** A. providing an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy
<!-- id: chem-7-3-1 -->

Explanation: Like all catalysts, enzymes lower activation energy without changing ΔH and are not consumed.

**A1667.** B. the region where the substrate binds and the reaction is catalysed
<!-- id: chem-7-3-2 -->

Explanation: The active site is a specifically shaped pocket formed by the tertiary structure, into which the substrate fits.

**A1668.** C. the active site changes shape slightly when the substrate binds, improving the fit
<!-- id: chem-7-3-3 -->

Explanation: In induced fit, binding causes a small conformational change that strains the substrate's bonds and assists the reaction.

**A1669.** D. decrease, because the enzyme denatures and its active site loses its shape
<!-- id: chem-7-3-4 -->

Explanation: Above the optimum temperature the weak interactions holding the tertiary structure break and the enzyme denatures.

**A1670.** A. decrease, because the change in pH alters the charges on side chains and the shape of the active site
<!-- id: chem-7-3-5 -->

Explanation: Changing pH changes the ionisation of R groups, disrupting ionic interactions and hydrogen bonds that shape the active site.

**A1671.** B. the complementary shape and chemical properties of the active site and the substrate
<!-- id: chem-7-3-6 -->

Explanation: Only a substrate whose shape and functional groups match the active site can bind and react.

**A1672.** C. a non-protein organic molecule, often derived from a vitamin, that an enzyme needs to function
<!-- id: chem-7-3-7 -->

Explanation: Coenzymes are small organic cofactors, e.g. many B vitamins act as coenzyme precursors.

**A1673.** D. an inorganic cofactor
<!-- id: chem-7-3-8 -->

Explanation: Metal ions that enzymes require for activity are inorganic cofactors; coenzymes are organic.

**A1674.** A. binds to the active site and prevents the substrate from binding
<!-- id: chem-7-3-9 -->

Explanation: A competitive inhibitor resembles the substrate and competes for the same active site.

**A1675.** B. work more slowly because there are fewer successful collisions, but are not permanently denatured
<!-- id: chem-7-3-10 -->

Explanation: Low temperature reduces kinetic energy and collision frequency; activity largely returns when warmed.

**A1676.** C. A, D, E and K
<!-- id: chem-7-3-11 -->

Explanation: Vitamins A, D, E and K are largely non-polar and dissolve in fats; B and C are water-soluble.

**A1677.** D. it has several hydroxyl groups that form hydrogen bonds with water
<!-- id: chem-7-3-12 -->

Explanation: Its small molecule carries multiple –OH groups (and C=O groups), allowing extensive hydrogen bonding with water.

**A1678.** A. its molecule is mostly non-polar hydrocarbon with only one hydroxyl group
<!-- id: chem-7-3-13 -->

Explanation: The single –OH group is insufficient to overcome the large non-polar ring and chain structure, so it dissolves in fats, not water.

**A1679.** B. Vitamin C is water-soluble, is not stored in large amounts and the excess is excreted in urine, whereas vitamin A is stored in fatty tissue
<!-- id: chem-7-3-14 -->

Explanation: Water-soluble vitamins are not stored significantly, so a regular dietary supply is needed.

**A1680.** C. the body cannot synthesise them, or cannot synthesise enough, yet they are needed for normal function
<!-- id: chem-7-3-15 -->

Explanation: Vitamins are needed in small amounts, often as coenzymes, but most cannot be made by the body.

**A1681.** D. the skin is exposed to ultraviolet light from the sun
<!-- id: chem-7-3-16 -->

Explanation: UV-B radiation converts a cholesterol derivative in the skin into vitamin D, so sun exposure is a major source.

**A1682.** A. is water-soluble, so it leaches into the cooking water, and it is also broken down by heat
<!-- id: chem-7-3-17 -->

Explanation: Water-soluble, heat-sensitive vitamin C is lost both by dissolving and by decomposition; steaming retains more.

**A1683.** B. insufficient lactase enzyme to hydrolyse lactose in the small intestine
<!-- id: chem-7-3-18 -->

Explanation: Without enough lactase, undigested lactose passes to the large intestine, where bacteria ferment it, causing symptoms.

**A1684.** Amylase's active site has a shape and chemical environment complementary to the α-glycosidic links in starch. Cellulose has β-glycosidic links, giving a different 3D shape that does not fit the active site, so amylase cannot bind and hydrolyse it.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-19 -->

Explanation: Must refer to the active site being complementary only to α-linkages.

**A1685.** From 20 °C to 37 °C, particles have more kinetic energy, so enzyme–substrate collisions are more frequent and energetic, increasing rate. Above about 45 °C the hydrogen bonds and other interactions holding the tertiary structure break, so the enzyme denatures, the active site changes shape and activity falls.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-20 -->

Explanation: Two parts: collision theory below optimum; denaturation above.

**A1686.** A cofactor is a non-protein component needed for enzyme activity; inorganic cofactors are metal ions such as Mg²⁺ or Zn²⁺. A coenzyme is an organic cofactor, often derived from a vitamin, such as NAD⁺ (from niacin, vitamin B3).
<!-- id: chem-7-3-21 -->

Explanation: Coenzymes are a type of cofactor; the key difference is organic versus inorganic.

**A1687.** Vitamin C is water-soluble, so excess dissolves in blood and is excreted in urine. Vitamins A and D are fat-soluble, so excess is stored in the liver and fatty tissue and can accumulate to toxic levels.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-22 -->

Explanation: Link solubility to excretion versus storage.

**A1688.** The statement is incorrect. Denaturation disrupts the secondary and tertiary (and quaternary) structure by breaking weak interactions, but the covalent peptide bonds of the primary structure are not broken. Breaking the protein into amino acids would require hydrolysis.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-23 -->

Explanation: Distinguish denaturation from hydrolysis.

**A1689.** Vitamin D is mostly non-polar, so it forms dispersion forces with the non-polar hydrocarbon chains of triglycerides in fat tissue. It cannot form enough hydrogen bonds with water to dissolve in blood plasma, so it dissolves in and is stored in fat.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-24 -->

Explanation: 'Like dissolves like': non-polar vitamin with non-polar fat.

**A1690.** Bromelain in fresh pineapple catalyses hydrolysis of peptide bonds in gelatine, breaking it into smaller peptides that cannot form a gel. Heating during canning denatures bromelain, so its active site is lost and it can no longer hydrolyse the gelatine.
<!-- id: chem-7-3-25 -->

Explanation: Mark for: protease hydrolyses gelatine; heat denatures the enzyme.

#### Food energy and digestion

**A1691.** C. 37 kJ
<!-- id: chem-7-4-1 -->

Explanation: Fats and oils release about 37 kJ g⁻¹, compared with about 16 kJ g⁻¹ for carbohydrate and 17 kJ g⁻¹ for protein.

**A1692.** D. 556 kJ
<!-- id: chem-7-4-2 -->

Explanation: 20.0 × 16 + 5.0 × 37 + 3.0 × 17 = 320 + 185 + 51 = 556 kJ.

**A1693.** A. 7.52 kJ
<!-- id: chem-7-4-3 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 150 × 4.18 × 12.0 = 7524 J = 7.52 kJ.

**A1694.** B. 1.00 kJ °C⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-4-4 -->

Explanation: E = VIt = 6.00 × 2.50 × 90.0 = 1350 J; CF = 1350 ÷ 1.35 = 1000 J °C⁻¹ = 1.00 kJ °C⁻¹.

**A1695.** C. 10.2 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-4-5 -->

Explanation: q = 850 × 4.80 = 4080 J = 4.08 kJ; 4.08 ÷ 0.400 = 10.2 kJ g⁻¹.

**A1696.** D. are less oxidised, containing proportionally more C–H bonds and fewer oxygen atoms
<!-- id: chem-7-4-6 -->

Explanation: Carbohydrates already contain a lot of oxygen (partly oxidised), so less energy is released when they are oxidised to CO₂ and H₂O.

**A1697.** A. 15.6 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-4-7 -->

Explanation: 2805 ÷ 180.0 = 15.6 kJ g⁻¹.

**A1698.** B. heat is lost to the surrounding air and the food may not combust completely
<!-- id: chem-7-4-8 -->

Explanation: Not all the heat released reaches the water, and incomplete combustion releases less energy than expected.

**A1699.** C. determine the energy needed to raise the temperature of the whole calorimeter by 1 °C
<!-- id: chem-7-4-9 -->

Explanation: The calibration factor accounts for all parts of the calorimeter, not just the water, giving a more accurate energy value.

**A1700.** D. mouth, catalysed by salivary amylase
<!-- id: chem-7-4-10 -->

Explanation: Salivary amylase starts hydrolysing starch into maltose and shorter chains while food is chewed.

**A1701.** A. stomach, catalysed by pepsin
<!-- id: chem-7-4-11 -->

Explanation: Pepsin, active in the acidic stomach, begins hydrolysing peptide bonds.

**A1702.** B. emulsify fats into small droplets, increasing the surface area for lipase to act on
<!-- id: chem-7-4-12 -->

Explanation: Bile is not an enzyme; it physically disperses fat so lipase can hydrolyse it faster.

**A1703.** C. fatty acids and glycerol (including monoglycerides)
<!-- id: chem-7-4-13 -->

Explanation: Lipase hydrolyses triglycerides into fatty acids, glycerol and monoglycerides, which are absorbed.

**A1704.** D. Hydrolysis
<!-- id: chem-7-4-14 -->

Explanation: Digestive enzymes break glycosidic, peptide and ester bonds by adding water.

**A1705.** A. carbon dioxide and water
<!-- id: chem-7-4-15 -->

Explanation: C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O, releasing energy for the body.

**A1706.** B. 17.2%
<!-- id: chem-7-4-16 -->

Explanation: (1500 ÷ 8700) × 100 = 17.2%.

**A1707.** C. 10.0 g
<!-- id: chem-7-4-17 -->

Explanation: 370 ÷ 37 = 10.0 g.

**A1708.** D. 71%
<!-- id: chem-7-4-18 -->

Explanation: Energy released = (0.50 ÷ 46.0) × 1360 = 14.8 kJ; water absorbs 100 × 4.18 × 25.0 = 10.45 kJ; 10.45 ÷ 14.8 × 100 ≈ 71%.

**A1709.** 9.41 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-4-19 -->

Explanation: q = 250 × 4.18 × 18.0 = 18 810 J = 18.8 kJ; 18.81 ÷ 2.00 = 9.41 kJ g⁻¹.

**A1710.** 3.60 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-7-4-20 -->

Explanation: E = 5.00 × 3.00 × 180 = 2700 J; CF = 2700 ÷ 2.25 = 1200 J °C⁻¹; q = 1200 × 3.60 = 4320 J = 4.32 kJ; 4.32 ÷ 1.20 = 3.60 kJ g⁻¹.

**A1711.** Heat is lost to the surroundings (air, the can and the clamp), and the food may burn incompletely, leaving soot or unburnt residue, so the water absorbs less energy than the food contains. Improvements include using a bomb calorimeter or insulating/shielding the apparatus and calibrating it electrically.
<!-- id: chem-7-4-21 -->

Explanation: Any valid source of heat loss plus a matching improvement.

**A1712.** In the small intestine, bile emulsifies the fat and the enzyme lipase (from the pancreas) catalyses hydrolysis of the three ester bonds. The products are glycerol and fatty acids (often absorbed as monoglycerides and fatty acids).
<!-- id: chem-7-4-22 -->

Explanation: Must identify lipase, small intestine, hydrolysis of ester links and the products.

**A1713.** Fat releases about 37 kJ g⁻¹ compared with about 16 kJ g⁻¹ for carbohydrate, because it is less oxidised. Fat is also stored without water (it is non-polar), whereas glycogen is stored hydrated, so fat stores far more energy per unit mass.
<!-- id: chem-7-4-23 -->

Explanation: Mention higher energy per gram and/or lack of associated water.

**A1714.** 326 kJ
<!-- id: chem-7-4-24 -->

Explanation: 14.4 × 16 = 230.4 kJ; 1.2 × 37 = 44.4 kJ; 3.0 × 17 = 51 kJ; total = 325.8 ≈ 326 kJ.

**A1715.** Humans lack the enzyme cellulase needed to hydrolyse the β-glycosidic links in cellulose, so it cannot be broken down into glucose and absorbed. It passes through the digestive tract largely unchanged, though it aids digestive health.
<!-- id: chem-7-4-25 -->

Explanation: Key point: no enzyme for β-linkages, so no glucose is released.

**A1716.** B. 527 kJ
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-1 -->

Explanation: Energy = 6.0 × 17 + 15.0 × 16 + 5.0 × 37 = 102 + 240 + 185 = 527 kJ. Fat provides more than twice as much energy per gram as carbohydrate or protein.

**A1717.** C. 2.50 kJ °C⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-2 -->

Explanation: E = VIt = 5.00 × 2.00 × 120 = 1200 J. Calibration factor = E ÷ ΔT = 1200 ÷ 0.480 = 2500 J °C⁻¹ = 2.50 kJ °C⁻¹.

**A1718.** A. 12.5 kJ
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-3 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 200 × 4.18 × 15.0 = 12 540 J = 12.5 kJ (3 significant figures). Using the final temperature (35.0 °C) instead of ΔT would give 29.3 kJ.

**A1719.** B. Heat is lost to the surroundings and combustion may be incomplete
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-4 -->

Explanation: In an open spirit burner much of the heat escapes to the air and the container rather than entering the water, and incomplete combustion (soot, CO) releases less energy. Both make the measured energy per mole smaller than the true value.

**A1720.** B. 15.6 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-5 -->

Explanation: Energy per gram = 2803 ÷ 180.0 = 15.6 kJ g⁻¹, consistent with the value of about 16 kJ g⁻¹ used for carbohydrates.

**A1721.** Energy released = 2.50 × 6.40 = 16.0 kJ; energy content = 16.0 ÷ 1.20 = 13.3 kJ g⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1722.** q = 200 × 4.18 × 15.0 = 12 540 J = 12.54 kJ; n(ethanol) = 0.850 ÷ 46.0 = 1.848 × 10⁻² mol; ΔH = −12.54 ÷ 1.848 × 10⁻² = −679 kJ mol⁻¹; much less negative than −1364 kJ mol⁻¹ because heat is lost to the surroundings/container (and combustion may be incomplete), so not all the energy released is absorbed by the water.
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1723.** Fats consist mostly of long hydrocarbon chains with many C–H bonds and very little oxygen, so they are in a much less oxidised (more reduced) state than carbohydrates, which already contain many C–O/O–H groups; oxidising fats to CO₂ and H₂O therefore forms many more strong C=O and O–H bonds per gram, releasing more energy (about 37 kJ g⁻¹ versus 16 kJ g⁻¹).
<!-- id: chem34a3-en-8 -->

Explanation:

### Green chemistry & sustainability

#### Green chemistry principles

**A1724.** A. It is better to prevent waste from forming than to treat or clean it up after it has formed.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-1 -->

Explanation: Prevention is the first green chemistry principle: avoiding waste is preferable to treating it afterwards.

**A1725.** B. Use of safer solvents and auxiliaries
<!-- id: chem-8-1-2 -->

Explanation: Supercritical CO₂ is non-toxic and easily removed, replacing a hazardous chlorinated solvent.

**A1726.** C. They lower activation energy, allowing reactions at lower temperatures and are not consumed, reducing energy use and waste.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-3 -->

Explanation: Catalysts provide an alternative pathway of lower activation energy and are regenerated, so they save energy and are not waste; they do not change equilibrium position.

**A1727.** Preventing waste avoids the need for extra treatment processes, which themselves use energy, materials and money and can still produce hazardous residues. It also removes the risk of waste escaping into the environment.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-4 -->

Explanation: Mention cost/energy of treatment and environmental risk.

**A1728.** D. Use of renewable feedstocks
<!-- id: chem-8-1-5 -->

Explanation: Sugar cane is a renewable biological feedstock, whereas ethene from crude oil is non-renewable.

**A1729.** A. Choosing a catalyst that allows a process to run at ambient temperature and pressure
<!-- id: chem-8-1-6 -->

Explanation: Energy requirements should be minimised; running at ambient conditions avoids heating and compression.

**A1730.** B. Design for degradation
<!-- id: chem-8-1-7 -->

Explanation: Products should be designed to break down into harmless substances after use rather than persisting in the environment.

**A1731.** Catalysis: the enzyme speeds up the reaction without being consumed, reducing waste. Design for energy efficiency: the reaction proceeds at low temperature and atmospheric pressure, so less energy is needed. (Use of renewable feedstocks also accepted if a plant sugar is the substrate.)
<!-- id: chem-8-1-8 -->

Explanation: Two correctly named principles, each linked to the enzyme process.

**A1732.** C. monitoring a process continuously during production so that formation of hazardous substances can be detected and controlled immediately.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-9 -->

Explanation: In-process monitoring allows conditions to be adjusted before hazardous by-products build up.

**A1733.** D. Generating a toxic reactant in small amounts as needed rather than storing large quantities of it
<!-- id: chem-8-1-10 -->

Explanation: Minimising the amount and hazard of substances on site reduces the risk of explosions, fires and releases.

**A1734.** A. avoiding unnecessary protecting groups or temporary modifications, as these steps require extra reagents and generate waste.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-11 -->

Explanation: Each blocking/protecting step uses extra reagents and creates waste, so they should be avoided where possible.

**A1735.** Advantage: it is non-toxic and non-flammable and can be removed simply by releasing pressure, leaving no solvent residue. Disadvantage: high pressures are needed, requiring energy and specialised equipment (and CO₂ is a greenhouse gas if released).
<!-- id: chem-8-1-12 -->

Explanation: One valid advantage and one valid disadvantage.

**A1736.** B. Replacing a catalysed route at 40 °C with an uncatalysed route at 400 °C
<!-- id: chem-8-1-13 -->

Explanation: Raising the temperature without a catalyst greatly increases energy use, contrary to design for energy efficiency.

**A1737.** C. Use of renewable feedstocks
<!-- id: chem-8-1-14 -->

Explanation: Waste cooking oil is a renewable, plant-derived feedstock, replacing petroleum-derived diesel.

**A1738.** D. It is non-toxic, non-flammable, cheap and abundant.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-15 -->

Explanation: Water is safe and abundant; its main drawbacks are poor solubility of some organics and high energy to evaporate.

**A1739.** A renewable feedstock can be replaced by natural processes within a human timescale, e.g. sugar cane or vegetable oils. A non-renewable feedstock is used faster than it forms, e.g. crude oil or natural gas.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-16 -->

Explanation: Definition plus an example of each.

**A1740.** A. Green chemistry aims to reduce or eliminate the use and generation of hazardous substances across the whole life cycle of a product.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-17 -->

Explanation: Green chemistry considers design, manufacture, use and disposal, aiming to minimise hazards throughout.

**A1741.** B. developing a pesticide that is effective against the target insect but has low toxicity to bees and mammals.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-18 -->

Explanation: Safer chemicals keep their function while minimising toxicity to people and non-target organisms.

**A1742.** C. the energy input comes from a renewable source and no carbon dioxide is produced in the process.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-19 -->

Explanation: Green hydrogen uses renewable electricity, avoiding the CO₂ produced in steam reforming of methane; electrolysis of water has only 11.1% atom economy for H₂.

**A1743.** Continuous monitoring during a reaction detects changes such as rising temperature or formation of unwanted by-products as they occur, so operators can adjust conditions immediately. This prevents large amounts of hazardous waste forming or being released.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-20 -->

Explanation: Must link monitoring during the process to preventing hazardous products.

**A1744.** D. catalysts are used in small amounts and regenerated, whereas stoichiometric reagents are consumed and become waste.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-21 -->

Explanation: A catalyst is not consumed, so it does not add to waste; a stoichiometric reagent ends up as by-product.

**A1745.** A. The reaction N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃ has 100% atom economy and unreacted gases are recycled.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-22 -->

Explanation: All reactant atoms end up in ammonia, and recycling unreacted gases reduces waste; using natural gas and high pressure are less green.

**A1746.** B. Producing polymers such as PLA that can be broken down by microorganisms under composting conditions
<!-- id: chem-8-1-23 -->

Explanation: Biodegradable polymers break down into harmless products after use rather than persisting for centuries.

**A1747.** Replace the solvent with a safer one such as water or supercritical CO₂ (safer solvents), and minimise the quantity stored by generating or ordering it as needed (inherently safer chemistry for accident prevention).
<!-- id: chem-8-1-24 -->

Explanation: Any two valid changes linked to principles.

**A1748.** Steam reforming uses a non-renewable fossil fuel feedstock, needs high temperatures (often from burning fossil fuels) and produces carbon monoxide/carbon dioxide. Solar-powered electrolysis uses a renewable energy source and water, and produces only oxygen as a by-product.
<!-- id: chem-8-1-25 -->

Explanation: Compare feedstock, energy source and by-products.

#### Atom economy calculations

**A1749.** C. (molar mass of desired product ÷ total molar mass of all reactants) × 100, using coefficients from the balanced equation
<!-- id: chem-8-2-1 -->

Explanation: Atom economy uses the balanced equation and measures the fraction of reactant mass ending up in the desired product.

**A1750.** D. 51.1%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-2 -->

Explanation: AE = (2 × 46.0) ÷ 180.0 × 100 = 51.1%. 25.6% forgets the coefficient of 2; 48.9% is the CO₂ fraction.

**A1751.** A. 100%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-3 -->

Explanation: Addition reactions have only one product, so all reactant atoms end up in ethanol: (46.0 ÷ (28.0 + 18.0)) × 100 = 100%.

**A1752.** AE = 50.5 ÷ (16.0 + 71.0) × 100 = 50.5 ÷ 87.0 × 100 = 58.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-4 -->

Explanation: Use the balanced equation; total reactant mass in the denominator.

**A1753.** B. Addition reaction
<!-- id: chem-8-2-5 -->

Explanation: In an addition reaction all reactant atoms combine into one product; substitution, condensation and elimination all form by-products.

**A1754.** C. 17.6%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-6 -->

Explanation: AE = (3 × 2.0) ÷ (16.0 + 18.0) × 100 = 6.0 ÷ 34.0 × 100 = 17.6%. 5.9% forgets the coefficient 3.

**A1755.** D. 56.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-7 -->

Explanation: AE = 56.1 ÷ 100.1 × 100 = 56.0%; the remaining 44.0% of the mass is lost as CO₂.

**A1756.** With anhydride: 180.0 ÷ 240.0 × 100 = 75.0%. With ethanoic acid: 180.0 ÷ 198.0 × 100 = 90.9%. The ethanoic acid route has the higher atom economy.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-8 -->

Explanation: Both calculations plus a conclusion. (In practice the acid route is slow and has low yield.)

**A1757.** A. 45.8%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-9 -->

Explanation: AE = (2 × 55.8) ÷ (159.6 + 3 × 28.0) × 100 = 111.6 ÷ 243.6 × 100 = 45.8%. 69.9% ignores CO in the denominator.

**A1758.** B. 83.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-10 -->

Explanation: AE = 88.0 ÷ (60.0 + 46.0) × 100 = 88.0 ÷ 106.0 × 100 = 83.0%; water is the by-product.

**A1759.** C. 100% and 63.9%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-11 -->

Explanation: Route 1 is addition (100%). Route 2: 64.5 ÷ (30.0 + 71.0) × 100 = 63.9%.

**A1760.** Atom economy is calculated only from the balanced equation and molar masses, assuming complete reaction, so it shows the maximum fraction of reactant atoms that can end up in the product. Percentage yield compares the mass of product actually obtained with the theoretical maximum, so it requires experimental data.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-12 -->

Explanation: Contrast equation-based vs measured.

**A1761.** D. 98.7%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-13 -->

Explanation: AE = (2 × 40.0 + 71.0) ÷ (2 × 58.5 + 2 × 18.0) × 100 = 151.0 ÷ 153.0 × 100 = 98.7%. 52.3% counts NaOH alone.

**A1762.** A. 11.1%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-14 -->

Explanation: AE = (2 × 2.0) ÷ (2 × 18.0) × 100 = 4.0 ÷ 36.0 × 100 = 11.1%; most of the mass ends up as oxygen.

**A1763.** B. Finding a use for, and selling, the by-product
<!-- id: chem-8-2-15 -->

Explanation: If a by-product becomes a useful co-product, it is counted in the numerator, increasing effective atom economy; temperature, pressure and catalysts do not change the balanced equation.

**A1764.** If CO₂ is also a useful product, its mass is included in the numerator, so the effective atom economy rises from 51.1% (ethanol only) to 100%, because all the mass of the reactant ends up in useful products.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-16 -->

Explanation: Show change from 51.1% to 100%.

**A1765.** C. 75.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-17 -->

Explanation: AE = 180.0 ÷ (138.0 + 102.0) × 100 = 180.0 ÷ 240.0 × 100 = 75.0%.

**A1766.** D. 77.9%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-18 -->

Explanation: AE = 63.5 ÷ (79.5 + 2.0) × 100 = 63.5 ÷ 81.5 × 100 = 77.9%. 79.9% ignores H₂ in the denominator.

**A1767.** A. 60.9%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-19 -->

Explanation: AE = 28.0 ÷ 46.0 × 100 = 60.9%; water is the by-product of this elimination reaction.

**A1768.** AE = 28.0 ÷ 30.0 × 100 = 93.3%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-20 -->

Explanation: Only one reactant, so the denominator is 30.0 g/mol.

**A1769.** B. A reaction can have a high percentage yield but a low atom economy.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-21 -->

Explanation: Atom economy is theoretical, from the balanced equation; yield is experimental. They are independent, so a high-yield reaction may still waste many atoms as by-products.

**A1770.** C. Its atom economy is 100%, but its greenness also depends on the source of feedstocks and energy, e.g. syngas from natural gas.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-22 -->

Explanation: Atom economy is only one measure; feedstock source, energy use, catalysts and conditions also matter.

**A1771.** D. 52.2%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-23 -->

Explanation: AE = (2 × 55.8) ÷ (159.6 + 2 × 27.0) × 100 = 111.6 ÷ 213.6 × 100 = 52.2%.

**A1772.** In addition polymerisation all monomer atoms are incorporated into the polymer, giving 100% atom economy. In condensation polymerisation a small molecule such as water is eliminated each time a link forms, so some reactant mass is lost as by-product and atom economy is less than 100%.
<!-- id: chem-8-2-24 -->

Explanation: Identify the by-product in condensation.

**A1773.** AE = (2 × 40.0) ÷ (2 × 58.5 + 2 × 18.0) × 100 = 80.0 ÷ 153.0 × 100 = 52.3%
<!-- id: chem-8-2-25 -->

Explanation: Coefficients must be included in both numerator and denominator.

#### Percentage yield calculations

**A1774.** A. (actual mass of product ÷ theoretical mass of product) × 100
<!-- id: chem-8-3-1 -->

Explanation: Percentage yield compares the mass actually obtained to the maximum possible from the limiting reactant.

**A1775.** B. 82.2%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-2 -->

Explanation: Theoretical: 10.0 ÷ 180.0 = 0.0556 mol glucose → 0.111 mol ethanol × 46.0 = 5.11 g. Yield = 4.20 ÷ 5.11 × 100 = 82.2%. 164% forgets the 2:1 ratio.

**A1776.** C. 15.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-3 -->

Explanation: n(N₂) = 1000 mol → 2000 mol NH₃ theoretical = 34.0 kg. Yield = 5.10 ÷ 34.0 × 100 = 15.0%. 30.0% ignores the 1:2 ratio.

**A1777.** Overall yield = 0.85 × 0.60 × 100 = 51%. Fewer steps means less product lost overall, fewer reagents and solvents used and less waste and energy consumed.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-4 -->

Explanation: Correct multiplication plus a green chemistry link.

**A1778.** D. 65.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-5 -->

Explanation: n(acid) = 0.100 mol → 0.100 mol ester = 8.80 g theoretical. Yield = 5.72 ÷ 8.80 × 100 = 65.0%. 83.0% is the atom economy.

**A1779.** A. 75.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-6 -->

Explanation: n(acid) = 0.200 mol, n(ethanol) = 0.150 mol, so ethanol is limiting: theoretical = 0.150 × 88.0 = 13.2 g. Yield = 9.90 ÷ 13.2 × 100 = 75.0%. 56.3% wrongly uses the acid.

**A1780.** B. 50.4%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-7 -->

Explanation: Overall yield = 0.90 × 0.80 × 0.70 × 100 = 50.4%; step yields multiply rather than average.

**A1781.** Any two of: the reaction reaches equilibrium and does not go to completion; side reactions form unwanted products; product is lost during separation and purification (e.g. filtration, distillation); reactants are impure.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-8 -->

Explanation: Two distinct valid reasons.

**A1782.** C. the product was not fully dried and still contained solvent.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-9 -->

Explanation: A yield above 100% means the measured mass includes something other than pure product, such as water or impurities; the other options lower the yield.

**A1783.** D. Recording the mass of a product that is still damp
<!-- id: chem-8-3-10 -->

Explanation: Damp product adds mass and raises the apparent yield; the others reduce the amount of pure product collected.

**A1784.** A. 89.9%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-11 -->

Explanation: Theoretical CaO = (50.0 ÷ 100.1) × 56.1 = 28.0 g. Yield = 25.2 ÷ 28.0 × 100 = 89.9%. 50.4% divides by the reactant mass.

**A1785.** High atom economy means the reaction itself produces little by-product, while high yield means most of the possible product is actually obtained. Improving both minimises wasted raw materials and waste disposal, lowering cost and environmental impact.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-12 -->

Explanation: Must distinguish the two and link to waste/cost.

**A1786.** B. 75.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-13 -->

Explanation: n = 2.76 ÷ 138.0 = 0.0200 mol → theoretical aspirin = 3.60 g. Yield = 2.70 ÷ 3.60 × 100 = 75.0%.

**A1787.** C. 75.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-14 -->

Explanation: n(CO) = 2000 mol → 2000 mol CH₃OH = 64.0 kg. Yield = 48.0 ÷ 64.0 × 100 = 75.0%. 85.7% is product mass divided by reactant mass.

**A1788.** D. 17.5 g
<!-- id: chem-8-3-15 -->

Explanation: Theoretical ethanol needed = 23.0 ÷ 0.800 = 28.75 g = 0.625 mol, so ethene = 0.625 × 28.0 = 17.5 g. 14.0 g ignores the yield; 11.2 g multiplies by 0.8.

**A1789.** Theoretical ester = 0.250 × 88.0 = 22.0 g. Yield = 15.4 ÷ 22.0 × 100 = 70.0%.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-16 -->

Explanation: 1:1 mole ratio; use moles of the limiting reactant.

**A1790.** A. 97.0%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-17 -->

Explanation: n(SO₂) = 2.00 mol → 2.00 mol SO₃ = 160.2 g. Yield = 155.4 ÷ 160.2 × 100 = 97.0%.

**A1791.** B. 106 g
<!-- id: chem-8-3-18 -->

Explanation: Theoretical CaO = (200 ÷ 100.1) × 56.1 = 112.1 g; × 0.950 = 106.5 g. 112 g ignores the yield; 118 g divides by 0.95.

**A1792.** C. It does not change the equilibrium yield, but the maximum yield is reached in less time.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-19 -->

Explanation: Catalysts speed up forward and reverse reactions equally, so equilibrium position, and therefore equilibrium yield, is unchanged.

**A1793.** Only about 15% conversion occurs in a single pass because the reaction reaches equilibrium. Recycling the unreacted gases back to the reactor means they eventually react, giving an overall yield of about 98% and reducing waste of feedstock.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-20 -->

Explanation: Link single-pass equilibrium yield to overall yield.

**A1794.** D. Lowering the temperature
<!-- id: chem-8-3-21 -->

Explanation: Lowering temperature favours the exothermic forward reaction; a catalyst and an inert gas at constant volume do not shift the equilibrium; decreasing pressure favours the side with more gas particles (the reactants).

**A1795.** A. 84.9%
<!-- id: chem-8-3-22 -->

Explanation: n(Fe₂O₃) = 0.100 mol → 0.200 mol Fe = 11.19 g. Yield = 9.50 ÷ 11.19 × 100 = 84.9%. 42.4% forgets the 1:2 ratio.

**A1796.** B. In theory every reactant atom could end up in the product, but in practice only 40% of the maximum product mass was obtained.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-23 -->

Explanation: 100% atom economy means no by-products in the equation; a 40% yield reflects incomplete reaction, losses or side reactions in practice.

**A1797.** Theoretical aspirin needed = 5.00 ÷ 0.700 = 7.14 g = 0.0397 mol. Mass of salicylic acid = 0.0397 × 138.0 = 5.48 g.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-24 -->

Explanation: Divide by the yield fraction before converting to moles.

**A1798.** Mass cannot be created, so the mass of pure product cannot exceed the theoretical maximum from the limiting reactant. A yield over 100% means the product contains extra mass, e.g. residual water/solvent from incomplete drying or impurities.
<!-- id: chem-8-3-25 -->

Explanation: Conservation of mass plus a valid cause.

#### Circular economy and sustainability

**A1799.** C. An economy in which materials are kept in use for as long as possible through reuse, repair, remanufacture and recycling, with waste designed out
<!-- id: chem-8-4-1 -->

Explanation: A circular economy designs out waste and keeps resources circulating, in contrast to the linear 'take–make–dispose' model.

**A1800.** D. take, make, use, dispose
<!-- id: chem-8-4-2 -->

Explanation: In a linear economy resources are extracted, made into products, used and then discarded as waste.

**A1801.** A. Collecting bottles through the Victorian container deposit scheme and reprocessing them into new food-grade bottles
<!-- id: chem-8-4-3 -->

Explanation: Collecting and reprocessing bottles into new bottles keeps the material in use and avoids waste.

**A1802.** A linear economy follows a take–make–use–dispose model in which resources are extracted, made into products and discarded as waste. A circular economy designs out waste and keeps materials in use for as long as possible through reuse, repair and recycling, with materials recovered at the end of a product's life.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-4 -->

Explanation: Must describe both models.

**A1803.** B. The material is permanently lost and the carbon is released as CO₂, so new raw materials are needed for future plastics.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-5 -->

Explanation: Energy recovery captures some value, but the material leaves the economy, unlike recycling which retains it.

**A1804.** C. They soften on heating and can be remoulded, because there are only weak forces between chains and no cross-links.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-6 -->

Explanation: Thermoplastics lack cross-links so they can be melted and reshaped; thermosets have covalent cross-links and char instead of melting.

**A1805.** D. broken down into monomers or small molecules that can be used to make new polymers or chemicals.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-7 -->

Explanation: Chemical recycling depolymerises or cracks polymers into feedstock molecules, allowing high-quality new materials.

**A1806.** Advantage: it is made from a renewable feedstock and is close to carbon neutral, since the CO₂ released on combustion was absorbed during plant growth. Disadvantage: growing sugar cane uses land, water and fertiliser that may compete with food crops, and processing (e.g. distillation) requires energy.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-8 -->

Explanation: One valid advantage and one valid disadvantage.

**A1807.** A. The carbon released on combustion was recently absorbed from the atmosphere by the canola plants, so net CO₂ emissions are lower.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-9 -->

Explanation: Biofuels are close to carbon neutral over their life cycle, although farming, processing and transport still produce some emissions.

**A1808.** B. land and water used for fuel crops may compete with food production.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-10 -->

Explanation: Using arable land for fuel crops can increase food prices and lead to land clearing, which is a sustainability trade-off.

**A1809.** C. Using waste heat and by-product CO₂ from one factory to warm greenhouses at a neighbouring business
<!-- id: chem-8-4-11 -->

Explanation: In industrial symbiosis, the waste or by-products of one industry become the inputs of another.

**A1810.** Thermosetting polymers have strong covalent cross-links between chains, so they do not soften or melt when heated and cannot be remoulded; they decompose instead. Thermoplastics have only weak intermolecular forces between chains and can be melted and reshaped.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-12 -->

Explanation: Refer to cross-links and behaviour on heating.

**A1811.** D. Avoid/reduce, reuse, recycle, recover energy, dispose
<!-- id: chem-8-4-13 -->

Explanation: Avoiding waste is best, followed by reuse and recycling; energy recovery and landfill are least preferred.

**A1812.** A. Life cycle assessment
<!-- id: chem-8-4-14 -->

Explanation: A life cycle assessment (cradle-to-grave) evaluates energy, materials and emissions at every stage of a product's life.

**A1813.** B. it removes the need to use natural gas as the hydrogen source, eliminating CO₂ released in steam reforming.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-15 -->

Explanation: Most hydrogen for ammonia comes from steam reforming of methane, which emits CO₂; electrolytic hydrogen from renewable power avoids this.

**A1814.** The by-products, which would otherwise be waste, become inputs for other industries. This keeps materials in use, reduces waste going to landfill or the atmosphere, and reduces the need for new raw materials (industrial symbiosis).
<!-- id: chem-8-4-16 -->

Explanation: Link by-products becoming resources.

**A1815.** C. A mobile phone designed with screws instead of glue, so its battery and metals can be easily separated for recycling
<!-- id: chem-8-4-17 -->

Explanation: Products that can be easily taken apart allow components and materials to be reused or recovered.

**A1816.** D. It is made from a renewable feedstock and can biodegrade under industrial composting conditions.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-18 -->

Explanation: PLA is a bio-derived polyester that breaks down in industrial compost, but degrades slowly in normal environments.

**A1817.** A. Polymer chains are shortened and contaminants build up each cycle, so quality decreases (downcycling).
<!-- id: chem-8-4-19 -->

Explanation: Repeated melting degrades polymer chains, so recycled plastic is often used in lower-grade products.

**A1818.** An LCA assesses the environmental impacts, such as energy use, raw materials, emissions and waste, at every stage of a product's life: raw material extraction, manufacture, transport, use and end-of-life disposal or recycling. It allows products or processes to be compared and identifies the stages where impacts can be reduced.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-20 -->

Explanation: Stages listed plus a purpose.

**A1819.** B. keeps a valuable, finite resource in use and reduces the environmental impacts of mining.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-21 -->

Explanation: Urban mining of e-waste recovers finite metals and reduces waste, land disturbance and energy from ore processing.

**A1820.** C. Development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs
<!-- id: chem-8-4-22 -->

Explanation: This is the widely used Brundtland definition of sustainable development.

**A1821.** D. Electricity from solar and wind farms
<!-- id: chem-8-4-23 -->

Explanation: Aluminium smelting uses huge amounts of electricity; renewable sources greatly reduce its greenhouse gas emissions.

**A1822.** Currently hydrogen is mostly produced from natural gas by steam reforming, a non-renewable feedstock that releases CO₂. Electrolysis of water using solar or wind electricity uses renewable energy and water, producing only O₂ as a by-product, so greenhouse gas emissions and fossil-fuel use are greatly reduced.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-24 -->

Explanation: Compare hydrogen sources and emissions.

**A1823.** Reducing avoids using materials and creating waste in the first place, and reusing keeps a product in use without reprocessing. Recycling still requires energy, collection and processing, and often loses some material quality, so it has a greater environmental impact than reducing or reusing.
<!-- id: chem-8-4-25 -->

Explanation: Link to energy and resource use.
